3,2026-10-07,"Grok Bot will route tasks to whichever model works best, including those of its rivals. Musk gave no detail on which jobs go where, a day after Grok users reported access problems.
Elon Musk’s Grok Bot will no longer run only on SpaceX’s own AI models. It will also use models from rivals, including Anthropic’s Claude Opus 5.5, Midjourney and Suno, Musk said in a post on X on Wednesday.
“Going forward, @SpaceX will use the best back end model for any given task, including Claude Opus 5.5, MidJourney, Suno and other leading APIs. Whatever is most likely to give you the best outcome,” Musk wrote.
Grok Bot is the AI agent app from SpaceXAI, the AI business Musk has folded into SpaceX. It launched in August. Each Bot works on its own computer and can carry out several tasks in parallel. Musk did not say which tasks would go to which outside model.
Musk launched xAI in 2023 to compete with OpenAI and has spent heavily on computing to train its Grok models. Anthropic, Midjourney and Suno build models for text, images and music. The change came a day after users reported problems reaching Grok on its mobile and web services, City AM reported. SpaceXAI gave no official cause.
Anthropic released its cheaper Opus model in September, weeks before its planned stock market listing. This week, Musk said SpaceXAI will take the name SpaceXSI.
xAI has kept adding features to Grok Bot. Since late September, teams can publish a shared Bot that colleagues use in the app or in Slack, according to its product changelog.
Grok Bot competes with personal AI agents such as Meta’s Muse, which reportedly passed 3 million weekly users, and Instinct. Some early users have deleted such agents over privacy worries, Business Insider reported on Tuesday. One of them told the publication he still uses Grok Bot, but has not connected it to his email.

4,2026-10-07,"Endeavor Catalyst has backed more than 90 European companies, 20 of them now valued at $1bn or more. Its new fund already counts EnduroSat and HappyRobot.
Allen Taylor, managing partner of Endeavor Catalyst
Endeavor Catalyst has closed its fifth fund at $320M, and Europe is now its fastest-growing market. It made 12 investments through Endeavor’s European offices in the first half of 2026. That compares with 14 in all of 2025 and seven in 2024, the firm said on Wednesday.
Investors oversubscribed Fund V, which takes the firm’s assets under management past $850M. Endeavor Catalyst is the venture fund of Endeavor, a global network of entrepreneurs. It invests only in companies led by founders selected into that network, alongside other venture and growth investors.
“We are doubling down on Europe with Fund V to help ensure European founders have access to the capital they need to build the next generation of global companies,” said Pete Benedetto, head of Europe for Endeavor Catalyst.
The firm has made more than 90 investments across 10 European markets: Bulgaria, Greece, Ireland, Italy, Poland, Portugal, Romania, Spain, Turkey and Ukraine. Twenty of those companies have reached valuations of $1bn or more. They include Bending Spoons, voice AI firm ElevenLabs, Preply, ICEYE and Fever.
European companies raised $3.3bn in rounds that Catalyst joined across 2025 and the first half of 2026. That is more than in any other region, the firm said. Latin America remains its largest region.
Fund V’s first European bets include EnduroSat, founded in Bulgaria, and HappyRobot’s AI agents, from Spanish founders. Its other early investments include Replit, Moove, Preply, Cashea, Respond.io and Lunar Outpost.
Catalyst invests 10% of each round. So its $320M should bring in at least $3.2bn from other investors, managing partner Allen Taylor wrote in a letter on the fund.
Across five funds, Catalyst has backed 437 companies in 44 markets, according to its global announcement. Of those, 83 have reached $1bn valuations. The fund counts 39 exits, including 11 IPOs. Founders of Nubank, Revolut, LinkedIn, Snowflake, Checkout.com, Globant and Careem are among the fund’s backers. Endeavor’s own entrepreneurs make up 30% of the investors in the fund.
The firm expects to invest another $25M to $30M by the end of 2026.

5,2026-10-07,"Warren Buffett says almost nobody can do what he does, so they shouldn't even try. His advice continues to pay off.
The legendary investor has proclaimed for decades that 99% of people shouldn't try to pick winning stocks, dabble in options, time the market, or hire a money manager.
""My regular recommendation has been a low-cost S&P 500 index fund,"" the retired Berkshire Hathaway chairman and CEO wrote in his 2017 letter to shareholders.
The benchmark index, which tracks the performance of 500 of the largest publicly traded companies in the US, climbed to a record 7,819 points on Tuesday.
It has gained 14% this year, almost 80% over the past five years, and nearly 1,100% since it slumped below 700 points during the financial crisis.
Buffett, 96, has said that his go-to strategy avoids high fees that eat into investment returns, and owning a broad index avoids having to identify the best stocks, figure out when to buy or sell them, balance a portfolio, or place leveraged bets that could blow up disastrously.
Answering a question from author and lifestyle guru Tim Ferriss during Berkshire's annual shareholder meeting in 2008, Buffett said that his approach lets people invest their money then ""forget it and go back to work.""
""You will not get that advice from anybody because nobody gets paid to give you that advice,"" Buffett said. ""You will have all kinds of people telling you how much better they can do for you than that, and how if you just give them a wrap fee, or give them commissions, or whatever it may be, that they will do better — but they won't do better.""
Buffett has said he's given similar advice to the trustee who will manage the wealth he leaves to his wife upon his death: ""Put 10% of the cash in short-term government bonds and 90% in a very low-cost S&P 500 index fund.""
Take a smarter break in your day - and see how far you get.

                                    Add BI in Google so our reporting is easier to find when you’re searching for what matters.
                                  
""I believe the trust's long-term results from this policy will be superior to those attained by most investors — whether pension funds, institutions, or individuals — who employ high-fee managers,"" he said.
Buffett's advice is rooted in his belief that the US will have a bright future.
""Over the long term, the stock market news will be good,"" Buffett wrote in his famous op-ed at the height of the financial crisis, titled ""Buy American. I am.""
""In the 20th century, the United States endured two world wars and other traumatic and expensive military conflicts; the Depression; a dozen or so recessions and financial panics; oil shocks; a flu epidemic; and the resignation of a disgraced president,"" he continued. ""Yet the Dow rose from 66 to 11,497.""
Buffett's conviction in his go-to recommendation led him to once bet $1 million that over a decade, an S&P 500 index fund would outperform a basket of hedge funds after costs, fees, and expenses. He comfortably won the wager at the end of 2017.
This reporter has followed Buffett's advice for his two young children since they were newborns, dollar-cost averaging by making monthly investments in a low-fee S&P 500 index fund on their behalf. Their annualized returns to date are close to 20%.
Still, some investors have warned that owning the S&P 500 is less safe today than in the past. The 10 most-valuable companies account for about 40% of the market-capitalization weighted index's total value, and most of them are betting vast sums on AI, a nascent technology.
For example, Nvidia is approaching a $6 trillion market cap, Apple and Alphabet are each valued at over $4 trillion, and Microsoft is just behind at $3.9 trillion.
Other investors, including Michael Burry of ""The Big Short"" fame, have cautioned that so much money pouring indiscriminately into stocks has contributed to a more bloated and fragile market.
The S&P 500's concentration risk is real; there's no guarantee its historic gains will continue, and the passive-investing boom could mean the next crash is a doozy. 
But for now at least, the Buffett approach is living up to its billing.

6,2026-10-07,"You're viewing the US website. Looking for the Site edition?
Founders in gaming and finance are splitting into two camps on AI, and both camps are winning. The dangerous place is the half-automated middle.
Copied to clipboard

		Opinions expressed by Entrepreneur contributors are their own.	
Nigel Eccles co-founded FanDuel. His next company, BetHog, was a crypto casino. Crypto casinos in general are a super competitive industry. The product the team had built internally, an AI blackjack dealer that banters and remembers you, became so popular with their players that they were much better served by shutting down the casino and selling the dealer to other gaming operators as a B2B product.
The key insight is that users really love the AI dealer experience. Live dealing is a huge operational cost for casinos, but it’s popular with users. Hence, AI dealing might be a good alternative.
“Very typically, a lot of video games you can play against computer, or you can play against other people. Playing against computers is actually a lot more popular, right?” Eccles said on my podcast. “We found that AI Live Dealer is 10 times more popular than its Live Dealer equivalent. So people just find it less scary.”
At the same time, I keep meeting founders selling the opposite: verified, live humanity. A prediction market on what a real streamer does next. A paper trading app where retention is other people. People are gonna either want more efficiency and customization, in which case AI becomes the product, or they’re going to want more authenticity, in which case the humanity itself becomes the product. And it’s unclear which use case will prevail. 
Why would I choose the AI dealer versus a human, or the AI competition versus a human competition? That part is a little bit unclear, so I asked the founders on each side.
Eccles automated dealers because players were not actually buying the human. “Live dealers are a $15 billion industry. There’s probably about somewhere around 30 or 40,000 people who work as live dealers,” he said. The experience “from the player perspective just isn’t great, probably 70, 80% of the time.”
His forecast is blunt: “Within three to five years, I think 80% of live dealers will be AI-powered.” And he argues automation grows the pie instead of shrinking it. “I actually think we are going to dramatically grow the market because there’s this sort of whole group of people that would play a live dealer, but real humans are a little bit scary. This is the game they can play.”
Paper trading can be a great way to learn about finance and the stock market. But paper trading on your own can be boring, tedious and easy to deceive yourself. Which is why a social paper trading app has been successful in basically having a simulated environment where there’s nevertheless social and human interaction.
It’s a social trading app where people can learn from each other, but without the pitfalls of unlimited losses.
A finance creator who partners with the app told me the same thing about the fake money. “The problem is when you do paper trading, a lot of people don’t take it seriously,” he said. What makes it work is treating it like the real, social thing it imitates: “You should have a strategy when it comes to paper trading even though it, like, seems like it’s counterintuitive.”
I spoke to a Gen Z founder who runs a prediction market on live streams, with markets that settle in about a minute. “Instead of trading on the final thing happening in the stream, like, will you win the match? You’re trading on everything happening throughout the stream,” he told me. “How many grenades will he pick up in the next 30 seconds? How many kills will he get in the next 60 seconds?”
The launch video that pulled in millions of views came from the human side of the platform, not the esports side. “We had one of our streamers approach 50 women in the streets of Miami, and you were able to trade on each of those interactions — would he get the number or not?” Rama said. The numbers are his: “We’ve crossed $10 million in trading volume. We have 42,000 registered users. This is all in the last 45 days of launching.”
Demand, in his experience, follows the humanity. “It’s more entertaining. And cooler. To trade on an IRL stream or your favorite streamer stream.”
So ask where the human sits in your product and what is going on in that moment. Is it a moment of delight, or is it a moment of friction? And where does the human add value, as a peer, as competition?
One more thing we should mention: The issue is not with the best. Eccles talks about this. It’s not with the best online dealers. The best online dealers have demand, and will always have demand. The issue is with the sort of not-so-good ones, the ones that are basically less efficient. Those will be replaced by AI, because AI will provide a more personalized experience.
At the very high end of most categories, people will still prefer a human experience.
Nigel Eccles co-founded FanDuel. His next company, BetHog, was a crypto casino. Crypto casinos in general are a super competitive industry. The product the team had built internally, an AI blackjack dealer that banters and remembers you, became so popular with their players that they were much better served by shutting down the casino and selling the dealer to other gaming operators as a B2B product.
The key insight is that users really love the AI dealer experience. Live dealing is a huge operational cost for casinos, but it’s popular with users. Hence, AI dealing might be a good alternative.
“Very typically, a lot of video games you can play against computer, or you can play against other people. Playing against computers is actually a lot more popular, right?” Eccles said on my podcast. “We found that AI Live Dealer is 10 times more popular than its Live Dealer equivalent. So people just find it less scary.”

8,2026-10-07,"Skydance starts life with HBO, CNN, two studios and $82bn of debt. Its leaders promise AI tools for creatives, 30 films a year and difficult decisions on jobs.
Skydance is the new name of the combined Paramount and Warner Bros. Discovery
Paramount has completed its purchase of Warner Bros. Discovery and renamed the combined company Skydance. The company announced the close on Tuesday, and its shares now trade on the New York Stock Exchange as SKYD. Warner Bros. Discovery shareholders received about $31.02 a share in cash.
Skydance owns two major film studios, Paramount Pictures and Warner Bros. It also owns HBO Max, Paramount+, CBS, HBO, CNN and CBS News. Its sports arms are CBS Sports and TNT Sports.
The company said its streaming products will merge into a single service over time. It gave no timetable. Skydance also owns Discovery+ and Pluto TV. Paramount had already begun moving Pluto TV, BET+ and Paramount+ onto one tech stack in June.
In a “Day 1” memo to staff, published by Business Insider, chief executive David Ellison and co-chief executive Ynon Kreiz set four priorities. One is to become the most technologically capable company in media.
“We will embrace the opportunities AI provides to expand what our creatives can imagine and to make our businesses even more productive,” they wrote.
The memo added that technology must serve the art, never the other way around. Chief product officer Dane Glasgow sent his own memo the same day, telling product and tech staff to build prototypes quickly. Business Insider published it too.
Skydance has promised at least 30 films a year in cinemas, each with a theatrical window of at least 45 days.
Paramount agreed in February to buy Warner Bros. Discovery for $31 a share, after Netflix dropped its own bid. In July, California and 11 other states sued to block it. A federal judge in California then delayed the deal until a trial.
The states settled in September. The terms include minimum film releases, extra domestic production spending and an editorial independence board for CNN and CBS News. Judge Araceli Martínez-Olguín approved the settlement on 30 September. On Monday, Supreme Court Justice Elena Kagan denied an emergency application from five consumers who wanted the merger stopped.
Skydance named its leadership team on Monday. Kreiz, the former chief executive of Mattel, runs integration and daily operations. Casey Bloys, head of HBO, and George Cheeks lead streaming and TV content. On Tuesday, Laurene Powell Jobs and Activision founder Bobby Kotick joined its board, with Tony Blair as an adviser.
To pay for the deal, the company priced $41.4bn and €885M of secured notes on 30 September, plus a term loan of $8.5bn and €850M. The notes pay between 6.3% and 9.125% a year.
Skydance expects nearly $70bn in revenue and is targeting at least $6bn in annual cost savings within three years. It aims to bring net debt down to three times its core earnings by the end of 2029. The company carries $82bn of debt, The New York Times reported, and Fitch Ratings downgraded it on Tuesday.
“Integrating two companies will bring change, including difficult decisions that affect our workforce,” the memo said.
Ellison addressed staff at a town hall that afternoon, according to audio obtained by Business Insider.
“There will be changes, and there will be impacts. I’m not going to pretend otherwise,” Ellison said.
Kreiz also spoke. Semafor saw his prepared remarks.
“Ultimately, our success will be determined by performance,” Kreiz said.
CNN chief Mark Thompson and CBS News editor-in-chief Bari Weiss keep their roles. At the town hall, CNN anchor Anderson Cooper asked Ellison whether he was pledging not to interfere with CNN’s coverage. Ellison said he was, the Times reported.
Ellison also said he supported CNN’s challenge to the White House press ban. At a news conference later on Tuesday, he noted that other executives had attended state dinners with President Trump.
“We were in a transaction review process, but with that said, we are now turning that page,” Ellison said.

9,2026-10-07,"Google Playground is free, runs on Gemini, Nano Banana and Lyria, and needs no coding. Unity Spark adds pro tools later, and Roblox shares fell as much as 8% before the open.
Google has launched Playground, an experimental platform that turns text prompts into playable games. It is teaming up with Unity to add more advanced tools, the company said in a blog post on Wednesday.
Google Playground opens today to users in the US aged 18 and over. People describe a game, test it right away and change the physics, rules, characters or setting by asking, with no coding required, according to Google.
Roblox shares fell as much as 8% in pre-market trading after the announcement, Investing.com reported. Unity’s shares slipped 1.6%, and Alphabet’s rose 0.35%.
Playground runs on Gemini, Nano Banana and Lyria, Google’s existing models for text, images and music, Google spokesperson Nia Carter told The Verge. Google pairs them with custom software it tuned on games it built itself, she said.
The tool is free. Google One subscribers can use more tokens each week, depending on their plan, Carter told The Verge.
Playground runs in the browser, so people can play on phones and laptops. Creators can keep a game private, share it by link or publish it to the Playground Explore gallery. The gallery ranks games by player ratings and play activity.
Select genres support leaderboards and multiplayer. Players use a Google Play Games profile to claim a handle and follow creators. Google says every published game goes through safety screening, and users can report games.
Google has kept other AI features for Americans only before.
Unity Spark will add professional-level mechanics, high-fidelity 3D graphics and the Unity runtime, the engine that runs Unity games, the companies said in a joint release. It is in testing, with a closed beta coming soon, according to Google. It will also give access to Unity Asset Store assets, GamesBeat reported.
“Unity Spark is about being able to create real, unique interactive experiences,” Unity chief executive Matt Bromberg told GamesBeat.
The companies gave no financial terms for the partnership.
Roblox lets players build and share their own games. Jefferies downgraded the stock to underperform on 28 September, Investing.com reported.
“[The stock’s] 30% rally since second-quarter results reflects overly optimistic expectations for bookings over the next 12 months,” Jefferies analyst James Heaney wrote, according to Investing.com.
In software, Swedish app builder Lovable turns prompts into apps. It says employees at nearly two thirds of the Fortune 500 use it.
Playground’s games plug into Google Play Games. A UK claim separately accuses Google of overcharging UK consumers over Play Store fees.

11,2026-10-07,"ReadWrite's editorial staff consists of journalists, editors, and experts across iGaming & Technology. Over 20 years of combined experience and the latest in technology, AI,…
Maine’s online casino market is legal but not live, with the Maine Gambling Control Unit taking public comments on proposed rules through Oct. 30 as a federal lawsuit challenging tribal exclusivity remains unresolved. No launch date has been confirmed, leaving operators’ path into the market dependent on rulemaking and an ongoing court fight.
The Gambling Control Unit released its proposed online casino rulemaking notice on Sept. 30. It covers licensing, house rules, wagering systems, cybersecurity, geolocation, responsible gambling, advertising, promotions, contracts and complaints.
Comments are due Friday, Oct. 30. The proposed requirements are an important step, but they do not mean operators can begin accepting wagers: final rules and licensing steps still remain.
Churchill Downs, which owns Oxford Casino Hotel, filed a federal lawsuit in January against Gambling Control Unit Director Milt Champion over the tribal-exclusive model. The complaint challenges who can provide online casinos in Maine, including language tied to race or citizenship, according to the primary report.
The case remains unresolved. Its outcome could affect the structure of the market, while the rulemaking process determines what operators must meet to participate.
LD 1164 established a framework giving Maine’s four tribes exclusive access to online casino gaming. Gov. Janet Mills took no action on the bill in January, allowing it to become law; legalization, however, did not bring an immediate launch.
The Gambling Control Unit held earlier meetings in June at Hollywood Casino in Bangor and in August at Oxford Casino in Oxford. Another meeting is scheduled for Tuesday, Oct. 20, as the agency works through the regulatory process.
Maine has also moved to restrict sweepstakes casinos. The Veterans and Legal Affairs Committee approved a ban in March, and Mills signed it in April. The law targets operators using a dual-currency payment system to simulate casino-style gaming, with civil penalties ranging from $10,000 to $100,000.
The distinction between legal authorization and an operating market is playing out elsewhere, too. Ohio’s proposed online casino market is also being shaped by regulatory and venue requirements, as outlined in Ohio’s online casino proposal.
Caesars Palace Online Casino has announced partnerships with three Wabanaki Nations, a sign that an operator is preparing for a market that has yet to open. But partnerships alone do not resolve the outstanding questions around final rules, license applications and the lawsuit.
The next milestones are the Oct. 20 regulator meeting and the Oct. 30 comment deadline. After comments are reviewed, final rules must be adopted and license applications opened before operators can launch.
SportsLine has estimated that Maine’s online casino market could launch in early 2027, but the state has not confirmed a timetable. The unresolved lawsuit may also affect the rollout, so the estimate is not a guaranteed launch window.
The sequence matters: authorization establishes that the market can exist, while rules and licenses establish how it can operate. Alberta’s regulated online gambling launch offers a contrast, with authorization followed by a functioning market under consumer-protection rules.

DraftKings and FanDuel each added $3.5 million to Nebraska’s online sports-betting campaign as prediction markets topped $9.4 billion in weekend volume ahead of the state’s November 3 vote.
The contributions to Tax Relief Nebraska bring the sportsbooks’ latest fundraising push into focus as voters consider measures to authorize online wagering and establish its regulatory framework. The Nebraska online betting measures would allow up to 12 mobile sportsbooks to partner with the state’s six gaming facilities.
The latest financial filing reported $7.25 million in campaign contributions from July 28 to September 29. That total includes another $250,000 from BetMGM, while election-year fundraising has reached $14.65 million.
DraftKings and FanDuel had each previously contributed more than $3.5 million. BetMGM had already given $250,000, and Fanatics had contributed $75,000 but did not make a new contribution in this cycle.
The two measures put the question of mobile betting and its operating rules before voters together. The proposed framework would connect the online sportsbook market to Nebraska’s existing gaming facilities, making the November vote a consequential step for operators seeking a route into the state.
Aldrin Research estimated that prediction markets traded more than $9.4 billion in notional volume over the weekend. Kalshi accounted for $7.13 billion across Saturday and Sunday, according to TickerTracker, and its $3.68 billion on Sunday exceeded the previous day’s $3.45 billion daily record.
Sports and combo contracts made up about 91% of Kalshi’s volume on both days. Football represented roughly $4 billion, including $3.1 billion in combos and approximately $897 million in straight football markets.
Polymarket US also exceeded $1 billion in weekend volume. DraftKings Predictions reached about $372 million, up from $334 million the previous weekend, while Novig recorded around $286 million, compared with roughly $200 million. Combos accounted for more than half of volume on each platform.
Those figures show sportsbooks and prediction-market platforms competing for activity in overlapping areas, including sports. Prediction-market activity tied to NFL betting sits alongside the traditional sportsbook market that DraftKings and FanDuel are seeking to expand in Nebraska.
The Gambling Insider report also noted a separate growth opportunity for DraftKings in prediction markets. Bank of America upgraded the company to buy from neutral, kept its $27 price target and projected about $400 million in prediction-market fees for 2027, plus $200 million to $400 million from market making.
The estimates underline how DraftKings is pursuing both sportsbook expansion and prediction-market business. Its push into prediction markets comes as the CFTC approved Kalshi’s US500 perpetual futures contract on October 3, another step in Kalshi’s expansion beyond event contracts.
DraftKings shares rose more than 7% intraday on Monday, October 5, but remained more than 40% lower for the year, according to the report. In Nebraska, the immediate test is whether voters approve the measures on November 3; the campaign’s latest funding shows operators are investing ahead of that decision.

13,2026-10-07,"The petition before the Federal Communications Commission would scrap the council that schedules launch frequencies and leave users to coordinate among themselves, and Boeing, Lockheed Martin, RTX and GE Healthcare have all told the regulator it would cause chaos
Barcelona, Spain – 02.03.2026: Space X logo is seen on display, a space exploration company owned by Elon Musk.
SpaceX and the commercial space industry want the FCC to abolish the third-party council that coordinates the radio spectrum used by rocket launches and aircraft flight testing, replacing it with an unspecified system of self-coordination. Boeing, Lockheed Martin, RTX and GE Healthcare have objected, while the European Commission is moving the other way and reserving two-thirds of its own satellite band for European operators.
SpaceX wants the American regulator to abolish the body that stops rocket launches interfering with everyone else’s radio frequencies. Boeing, Lockheed Martin, RTX and GE Healthcare have all objected. The justification is a plan SpaceX’s own stock market filing says may never work.
The petition before the Federal Communications Commission would scrap the Aerospace and Flight Test Radio Coordinating Council and leave users of the bands to devise their own system of self-coordination, as Bloomberg reported. SpaceX’s filings describe the objectors as “legacy contractors attempting to maintain a veto over more advanced competitors“.
Every launch needs spectrum to steer a rocket into orbit, and operators currently book weeks ahead to avoid interference. Elon Musk wants 10,000 launches a year.
What are they all for?
Orbital data centres, which SpaceX’s own filing warns involve unproven technologies and may not reach commercial viability. Musk called space-based artificial intelligence a no-brainer at Davos three months before that document was written. The company is valued at about $2T, partly on the promise.
The objectors are not only in aerospace.
GE Healthcare told the commission it “should not elevate space launch operator convenience over the reliability of critical safety-of-life operations“, by which it means the wireless sensors that monitor patients in hospital. Boeing said self-coordination cannot work when much of what it tests cannot be described in public. A wireless microphone trade group warned about live sport.
Europe is moving in the other direction entirely.
The Commission plans to ringfence two-thirds of the EU’s 2 GHz mobile-satellite band for European operators, leaving Starlink and Amazon’s Kuiper to bid for the remaining third. That is the band the IRIS2 consortium of SES, Eutelsat and Hispasat would use, and the current licences expire in May 2027.
One regulator is being asked to remove the referee. The other is reserving the pitch.
SpaceX and three other American firms skipped Macron’s space summit in September after pressure from the White House. The EU Space Act at the centre of that row would make any operator serving European users seek prior authorisation and appoint a representative here.
Scale is the argument on both sides.
SpaceX is building a $100B spaceport in Louisiana designed for thousands of launches a year. Ariane 6 manages nine or ten.

14,2026-10-07,"

      Joel Rose
    


                Michael Paprocki, a regional transportation planner, stands on the Market Street Bridge over the Ohio River.
                
                    
                    Joel Rose/NPR
                    
                
hide caption

STEUBENVILLE, Ohio — The Market Street Bridge still looms over the Ohio River, spanning almost 1,800 feet between downtown Steubenville and the green hills around Weirton, W.Va., on the other side.
But this once majestic steel suspension bridge closed nearly three years ago. And up close, it's easy to see why transportation officials consider it beyond repair.
""You got severe rusting under here,"" explains Michael Paprocki, a regional transportation planner, as he points to visible holes in the rusted beams under the bridge's deck. ""In reality, if it stood long enough, it would probably collapse under its own weight.""
Local leaders say it's crucial to replace this bridge to reconnect the communities on either side. But it's also important for another reason: The Ohio River is a major obstacle in the path of the Great American Rail-Trail, an ambitious plan to turn former rail corridors into bike and pedestrian trails that stretch from coast to coast.
Political gridlock in Washington is slowing down bicycle and pedestrian trails. Transportation Secretary Sean Duffy has made clear that he doesn't want to spend federal dollars on what he calls ""DEI bike lanes.""
But deep in Appalachia, there is bipartisan support for a multiuse trail that would connect Ohio and West Virginia. Mayors from both sides of the river — and both sides of the political divide — say the trail is vital to the region's economic future.
That's why local movers and shakers gathered a few weeks ago on the Ohio side of the bridge to talk about how to make its proposed replacement a reality.
""This is huge,"" said Republican Ralph Petrella, the mayor of Steubenville. In an interview with NPR, Petrella said the bridge and trail would help raise the city's profile. ""This is a national entity that would help our economic development and continue the momentum we have for revitalization.""
The Democratic mayor of nearby Weirton, W.Va., was on hand too. Dean Harris worked at a local steel mill for 48 years. The mill closed in 2024, and Harris says Weirton is trying to attract new employers.
""Weirton is in the process of sort of rebranding ourselves. Steel is what made us, and brought people to our area. But obviously it's time for us to move on from that,"" Harris said. ""We need to look at the future and what's going to be good for future generations. And that's what we're in the process of doing.""
Harris says the rail-trail project is part of that effort. Weirton already has a rail-trail that connects to the suburbs of Pittsburgh, about 30 miles east. That helped Weirton attract a battery factory that opened several years ago, Harris said.
Local leaders also hope the rail-trail will encourage more young people to stay. Maura Barnes grew up in Ohio near Steubenville. While most of her friends moved away, Barnes stayed after college to raise her own family in downtown Steubenville.
""My husband and I, somewhat selfishly, really wanted a grocery store in walking distance, not just for ourselves and our family, but all the other people living in the downtown community,"" she said.
So Barnes and her husband opened the Steubenville Grocery Box with some friends. But she says the closing of the Market Street Bridge hit the neighborhood hard.
""I think a lot of it was almost spiritual,"" Barnes said. ""It's a loss, and it was a blow to the morale of the community.""
The Veterans Memorial Bridge, less than 2 miles away, carries highway traffic over the Ohio River. But the Market Street Bridge was the only way for pedestrians or bikes to cross safely, and the only bridge that brought cars directly into downtown.
""A lot of the businesses around us who had been open for years, they saw a pretty notable decrease in foot traffic and car traffic to their businesses when that closed,"" Barnes said.
There is a plan to replace the Market Street Bridge with another one a few blocks away that would include a dedicated trail for bikes and pedestrians. Congress has appropriated $87 million so far. But that's only a fraction of what the new bridge will actually cost.
""The challenge of crossing a body of water as big as the Ohio River is monumental,"" said Eric Oberg, the senior director of programs at the Rails To Trails Conservancy, the nonprofit group behind the Great American Rail-Trail.
More than half of the proposed 3,700-mile trail already exists, Oberg said, although there are still some big gaps, including the Ohio River crossing. That's why the group organized the September event in Steubenville.
Local leaders are pushing state and federal lawmakers to find more money for the bridge. And they're focused on the surface transportation reauthorization bill that's currently being negotiated.
But they're doing it at a time when the Trump administration has been hostile to federal spending on bike infrastructure. Oberg says that creates ""headwinds"" for projects like this, though it doesn't have to stop progress on the replacement bridge.
""It's gonna happen. This is an inevitable project. There's too much support for it. The question is the timeline,"" Oberg said.
In its heyday, the Market Street Bridge used to carry 40,000 cars a year, said Michael Paprocki, the regional transportation planner, who grew up in Steubenville. But the cables that anchor the bridge's piers to the ground have failed, leading West Virginia officials to close the bridge in late 2023.
Paprocki now serves as executive director of the Brooke Hancock Jefferson Metropolitan Planning Commission, a nonprofit working on transportation issues in the Steubenville and Weirton region. He says there's broad support for a new bridge on both sides of the political divide.
""It's not just liberals that ride bikes,"" he said. ""Ohio is an extremely conservative state and a lot of them ride bikes. West Virginia is an extremely red state. And believe me, they love to go out and ride their bikes.""

15,2026-10-07,"The benchmark S&P 500 is up 14 percent this year despite elevated oil prices and US government debt sell-off.
Save
Share
The United States stock market has hit an all-time high as excitement around artificial intelligence continues to fuel a buying frenzy on Wall Street.
The S&P 500, the benchmark stock index, closed 0.58 percent higher on Tuesday, topping the previous peak reached in mid-August.
The Nasdaq Composite, which is more heavily weighted towards tech stocks, also hit a record high, finishing up 0.45 percent.
Tech stocks were among the top performers, with each of the “Magnificent Seven” apart from Meta closing higher.
Amazon led the pack with a 1.95 percent gain, followed by Microsoft and Tesla, which rose 0.78 percent and 0.51 percent, respectively.
Apple and Alphabet both rose 0.22 percent, while Nvidia gained 0.14 percent.
Meta, which has risen more than 20 percent since the launch last month of its new AI assistant, Muse, fell 0.41 percent.
Other big movers in tech included Marvell Technology, which rose 5.81 percent, and Cisco, which gained 4.54 percent.
 

 
Keith Lerner, chief investment officer and chief market strategist at Truist Advisory Services in Atlanta, Georgia, said the market rally should be seen as a “technology and AI surge”.
“Every bull market has a dominant theme, and technology and AI remain this market’s dominant theme,” Lerner told Al Jazeera.
“Technology and communication services were the only two S&P 500 sectors to rise last month, while the other nine declined.”
Wall Street has shrugged off a host of challenges facing the US economy amid hyperscalers’ multibillion-dollar investments in AI.
Despite the energy crunch caused by the US-Israel war on Iran and a sell-off of US government bonds partly driven by ballooning government debt, the market is well on track to record its fourth consecutive year of double-digit returns.
The S&P 500 has risen 14 percent so far in 2026, while the Nasdaq Composite is up 18.78 percent.
“Investors remain bullish on the prospects for AI,” Lochlan Halloway, a senior equity strategist at Morningstar Australia, told Al Jazeera.
“They are betting that the money pouring into data centres will earn a good return, and so far, that belief has outweighed rising interest rates, more expensive oil and a 10-year bond yield above 5 percent,” Halloway said.
“We are positive on AI too,” Halloway added. “But the range of outcomes is wide, and the market is concentrated in a handful of companies, so the outlook for US shares, and by extension global shares, relies on the AI story continuing to deliver.”
Lerner at Truist Advisory said the market rally had the potential to continue through the end of 2026 given historical trends and expectations for strong corporate earnings.
“We do not expect a straight line higher for markets,” Lerner said.
“Still, the fourth quarter of [US] midterm-election years has produced an average gain of 7 percent and has been positive 84 percent of the time since 1950.”
Lerner said that rising interest rates posed the biggest risk to the upward trend.
“Still, on balance, the weight of the evidence suggests this bull market still has more upside potential,” he said.
Despite the rally on Wall Street, Asian stock markets fell on Wednesday, with key indexes in Japan, South Korea, and Hong Kong racking up losses.
The Kospi in Seoul led the sell-off, falling 1.98 percent, while Tokyo’s Nikkei 225 dropped 0.92 percent.
In Hong Kong, the Hang Seng Index was down 0.57 percent shortly before the market’s close.
Oil prices, which have been elevated since the start of the war on Iran, rose on Wednesday as traders weighed the fallout of fighting in Yemen between forces aligned with Yemen’s internationally recognised government and the Iran-aligned Houthis.
Brent crude futures for December delivery stood at $101.16 a barrel as of 07:30 GMT, up 0.58 percent.

Unlike plenty of cheaper tablets that use IPS LCD panels, Samsung gives you an 11-inch AMOLED display, which means richer colors, deeper blacks, and an overall much better experience when watching movies or other content.
Samsung isn't sacrificing performance either. The Galaxy Tab S11 is still powered by the MediaTek Dimensity 9400+ chipset and comes with up to 12GB of RAM, so you can throw pretty much anything at this tablet without worrying too much about performance. Whether it's note-taking, gaming, or even some video editing, there's still plenty of power here.
 



Speaking of note-taking, Samsung bundles the S Pen in the box, so you don't have to spend extra if you want to use the tablet for drawing or taking notes in class. There's also a microSD card slot if you want to expand the storage later.
But one of my favorite things about the Galaxy Tab S11 is still its design. The tablet is only 5.5mm thin while still offering an IP68 dust and water resistance rating, making it easy to carry around without giving up the durability you'd expect from a flagship tablet.
On the whole, if you're looking for an Android tablet with flagship specs that doesn't burn a hole in your pocket, the Galaxy Tab S11 is probably the one I'd look at during Prime Big Deal Days. It still gives you a portable design, plenty of performance, S Pen support, and, most importantly, an AMOLED display that you'll actually enjoy looking at.

Sanuj is a tech writer who loves exploring smartphones, tablets, and wearables. He began his journey with a Nokia Lumia and later dived deep into Android and iPhone. He's been writing about tech since 2018, with bylines at Pocketnow, Android Police, Pocket-Lint, and MakeUseOf. When he's not testing gadgets, he's either sipping chai, watching football, or playing cricket.

17,2026-10-07,"ReadWrite's editorial staff consists of journalists, editors, and experts across iGaming & Technology. Over 20 years of combined experience and the latest in technology, AI,…
Rep. Don Davis, a North Carolina Democrat, introduced the No Betting on Your Own Race Act, a bill that would bar federal candidates and specified people and campaign bodies connected to them from trading contracts tied to their own elections. Violators would face a civil penalty of $10,000 or three times the net financial gain, whichever is greater, according to Quartz.
The proposal would place a federal restriction on the people involved in the trading, rather than directly penalizing prediction-market exchanges for facilitating a trade. Its practical effect would be to give candidates and their associated parties a statutory rule to follow, backed by a defined minimum penalty.
The bill would apply to federal candidates, their spouses, dependent children and authorized campaign committees, Davis’s office said. Those covered could not buy, sell, acquire, dispose of or hold event contracts related to the candidate’s election.
The legislation would also require candidates to be notified of the prohibition during the filing period. That provision would put the restriction in front of candidates as they file for office, rather than leaving them to learn about it only after a trade has drawn scrutiny.
Davis introduced the measure after a controversy involving Laurie Buckhout, his Republican opponent in North Carolina’s 1st Congressional District. Kalshi concluded that Buckhout had traded contracts tied to her race. She resolved the dispute with the platform in August, paying a penalty of just under $2,600 and receiving a three-year suspension, according to CNBC.
Buckhout said at the time that she had bet on herself, described the trade as a mistake and said she had tried to put things right once she learned there was an issue. Davis criticized the trades in a post on X, calling them a breach of public trust, CNBC reported. The proposed bill would move the issue beyond a platform’s own enforcement process by establishing a federal civil penalty for covered conduct.
The proposal would also address a gap in existing congressional rules. A Senate resolution passed in April barred senators and their staff from prediction-market trades, but did not cover non-incumbent candidates seeking Senate seats, according to CNBC. No equivalent measure had cleared the House, although several resolutions seeking a similar restriction had been introduced.
Davis’s proposal is part of a broader debate over restrictions on political trading. Rep. Bryan Steil has separately proposed limits on lawmakers’ participation in prediction markets, including contracts tied to elections and public-policy outcomes. The proposal to restrict congressional prediction-market trading focuses on lawmakers, while Davis’s bill specifically targets federal candidates and parties associated with their campaigns.
Davis introduced the bill during a pro forma House session. Congress was not scheduled to reconvene until after the midterm elections, leaving the proposal little chance of taking effect during the current electoral cycle, Quartz reported.
For prediction-market operators, the bill would not itself impose a penalty simply for hosting a covered contract, based on the details reported by Quartz. But if enacted, it would make candidate participation in contracts tied to their own elections a federal compliance issue, rather than one handled only under platform rules.

Cars > Automobiles > Hybrids
The automaker has reaffirmed its hybrid plans just as its EV lineup finds its footing. A history of killing promising tech has me worried.
Senior Writer, Electrified Cars
Senior Writer, Electrified Cars
October 7, 2026, 10:32 am ET
A General Motors executive this week reaffirmed the automaker’s plans to bring hybrid cars to its lineup, sparking discussion across the web. It makes sense, as hybrids are having a moment, driven by Americans’ hesitancy toward adopting EVs and surging fuel prices. Many automakers are rushing to join the feeding frenzy with their own upcoming hybrid models. So why does hearing that GM, specifically, is shifting gears to hybrids seem different? It’s because I feel like GM’s been here many times before, and it didn’t end well.
Mike Anderson, GM’s vice president of propulsion engineering, reconfirmed the automaker’s hybrid plans with CNBC. The Detroit-based automaker said in 2024 that it was working to add hybrid models to its lineup, but hasn’t made any concrete announcements or updated its timeline since. Today, GM has only one hybrid in its lineup — the Corvette Grand Sport X, which replaces the E-Ray that debuted in 2023 — as it has largely focused on full-electric cars.
I’ve been following the automotive landscape for 30 years and covering the space professionally for nearly two decades, and I’ve watched GM make more than a few baffling decisions in that time. If there were an award for “most golden geese killed” or “coldest feet,” I’d give it to GM. Time and again, the automaker has brought truly innovative cars and powertrain technology to market just ahead of its time, only to back out before it reaches its stride and retreats to the familiar.
GM’s reputation as the company that killed the electric car, thanks to its handling of the EV1 in the 1990s, is a topic that’s been examined, perhaps to exhaustion. Later, in the early 2000s, the automaker dabbled in hybridization, but instead of starting with a small, efficient car like the Toyota Prius, GM focused on full-size SUVs and pickups that struggled to meet their fuel-saving targets. The so-called Two-Mode Hybrid lasted only about five years before being quietly discontinued.
GM’s engineers were cooking when the automaker later debuted its Voltec hybrid technology under the hood of the beloved Chevrolet Volt (and the subsequent Cadillac ELR). The Volt was the first commercially available plug-in hybrid electric vehicle in the US, beating Toyota’s Prius Prime to market by a year with more electric range, better fuel efficiency and more palatable styling. GM’s marketing initially confused customers, and even some journalists I knew at the time. (The automaker notoriously refused to call the Volt a hybrid.) By the time the second generation arrived with significant improvements, buyers had started to realize what a gem GM’s “not a hybrid” was — right before it was canceled in 2019. Its demise is still lamented by fans.
More recently, GM killed its Chevy Bolt EV, the brand’s first mass-market fully electric car, twice. First, at the height of its popularity in 2023, the Bolt was discontinued while GM refocused on its new Ultium EV platform, starting with larger, more expensive electric trucks and SUVs. Earlier this year, the Bolt returned only to be axed again: the second generation’s 18-month run is scheduled to end in 2027, just as interest in affordable electric cars is once again on the rise in the US. Talk about a double slap to the face for fans of the little EV.
Do you see the pattern? GM introduces a forward-thinking car, then kills its golden goose and retreats to the familiar, just as customers start to get on board. I don’t know if it’s a lack of patience or commitment, but it has made it hard to get excited whenever GM announces it’s changing direction.
Today, customer interest in EVs is beginning to rebound. GM is the only Detroit Big 3 automaker with a fully fleshed-out electric lineup, with BEVs in almost every popular class across its Chevy, GMC and Cadillac brands. The road to electrification in America has been a rocky one, but GM’s all-in-on-EVs strategy feels like it’s finding its footing, which makes me worry that GM is about to “pull a GM.”
General Motors isn’t the only automaker struggling to respond to wild swings in the US new-car and EV markets. Trade wars, actual wars, government policy and rising fuel costs have left American drivers stuck between the rock of paying hundreds of dollars more per year for gas and the hard place of fewer affordable EVs. Recently, Cox Automotive reported that 56% of surveyed shoppers said they would likely consider a hybrid or plug-in hybrid as their next car. 
Today, only a few brands (Toyota, Hyundai and Honda) control around 86% of the US hybrid car market. Toyota alone is responsible for nearly half of all US hybrid sales. The rest of the industry — all too eager to leapfrog straight to full electric just a few years ago — has been caught flatfooted and scrambling to meet surging demand, GM included.   
By and large, I’m a fan of GM’s current fleet of electric vehicles. The Bolt EV (while it’s still here) is a fantastic little city car. I recently took a road trip in the Equinox EV and was blown away by the range, technology and value it delivered for the price — even with the removal of Apple CarPlay and Android Auto. I’ve also had positive, recent experiences with the Cadillac’s Optiq EV, the Chevrolet Silverado EV and the GMC Sierra EV. It’s taken some time to build momentum, but you could argue right now that GM has the broadest spectrum of good EVs of any American automaker.
To me, GM’s history paints a shift to hybrids in an unconfident light. Imagine how it must look to layman buyers who are unsure of which green car tech to stick with. To be fair, this time GM hasn’t said it’s backing off on BEVs, merely adding hybrids to the mix.
“Our long-term vision is an all-electric future. That’s our goal,” Anderson told CNBC. “That’s the end state, but it’s going to be a journey that involves technology diversity.”
GM’s all-in-on-EVs commitment to electrify 50% of its fleet by 2030, with 100% of its passenger and light-duty vehicles going electric by 2035, appears to remain in place. The addition of hybrids or plug-in hybrids — that “diversity” Anderson spoke of — is sure to be a good thing for the brand and its customers. But it makes me worry.

23,2026-10-07,"A new report from Counterpoint Research shows that Apple overtook Samsung during the second quarter of 2026 to become the leading smartphone brand in Europe’s carrier channel, as operator sales held up better than the broader market. Here are the details.
In a report published today, Counterpoint Research says smartphone sales through carriers in Europe declined 4% year-over-year in Q2 2026, compared with an 11% drop in the open market. Total smartphone sales, per the report, declined 8%.
Apple was a key driver of this shift, having overtaken Samsung as the market share leader of Europe’s operator channel. Motorola and vivo also contributed to the growth with some strategic positioning with key European operators.
Counterpoint says this is yet another result of rising memory costs, which have hit Europe’s low-end smartphone market particularly hard. Carrier sales, meanwhile, have held up better because they tend to skew toward higher-end devices and premium brands such as Apple, with more phones purchased on contract.
According to the report, Apple’s share of smartphone sales through European carriers rose from 33% in Q2 2025 to 37% in Q2 2026, while Samsung’s fell from 39% to 34% over the same period.
That said, Chinese brand vivo posted the biggest year-over-year increase in carrier sales, at 90%, while Motorola came in a distant second with 27% growth.
Here’s Jan Stryjak, Associate Director at Counterpoint Research, on these numbers:
“Smartphone sales through operators in Europe are typically more high-end than through the open market, as more are purchased on a contract. They are also more skewed towards more premium brands like Apple, Samsung, and Google. The collapse of the low-end of the smartphone market so far in 2026 because of the memory price crisis has therefore benefitted the operator channel, which has declined comparatively less than the open market.”
Stryjak added that he expects the carrier channel to continue gaining share over the “next few quarters as the lower end of the market continues to be impacted by the memory crisis.” He also noted that independent retailers have increased promotional activity compared to carriers, “which could mitigate some of the price rises and provide addition [sic] stimulus to the open market.”

24,2026-10-07,"Geoffrey Hinton wants AI companies to clear a hurdle familiar to drugmakers.
On Tuesday’s “Smart Girl Dumb Questions” podcast, the Nobel Prize-winning computer scientist, often called the “Godfather of AI,” proposed that AI companies convince a regulator that their products are safe before releasing them.
“You're not allowed to just make a new drug and release it on the market,” Hinton told podcast host Nayeema Raza. “You have to convince the FDA. And to do that, you have to do a lot of work, about a billion dollars worth of work.”
“That seems like the very least we should have for AI,"" Hinton added.
Hinton's prescription for the AI safety debate came after predictions of AI catastrophes and after OpenAI’s decision to shelve its latest model, following testing that raised concerns about whether it would follow users’ instructions and stay within its authorization.
OpenAI's head of safety systems, Saachi Jain, previously told Business Insider that GPT-6.1 Astra improved at persisting through difficult tasks but fell short on permissions and communicating what it had done.
Hinton said that AI is increasingly helping itself improve, potentially accelerating both its capabilities and its dangers.
“Exponentials grow very fast,"" Hinton said. ""We're beginning to get recursive self-improvement because AI is being used to make AI better in many different ways.""
“So, it's just a guess,"" Hinton added, ""But I guess we have a year or two before everything gets much worse than it is now.”

25,2026-10-07,"TOPICS

                    personal tech
                

        Contract purchases hold up better than independent retail sales, with Apple overtaking Samsung in the carrier channel
    
European mobile operators are gaining a slightly larger share of smartphone sales, not because they are selling more devices, but because other sales channels are shrinking faster.
This is according to market watcher Counterpoint Research, which says that sales of new smartphones through European operators were down by 4 percent during the second quarter of this year, compared with the same period a year ago.
But device sales through the open market fared much worse, declining by 11 percent. This refers to phones that are bought outright from independent retailers or directly from manufacturers, which are typically SIM-free and unlocked so the customer can choose which network they hook up to.
Operators accounted for 36 percent of European smartphone sales in the second quarter, up from 35 percent a year earlier. The open market still represented the majority.
As The Register has covered numerous times this year, mobile phones have been hit by the AI-driven memory crisis, which has led to a shortage of the DRAM and NAND flash components needed for consumer kit like smartphones and PCs.
The resulting rise in prices has been felt most keenly at the lower end of the market, effectively making it commercially unviable to produce budget devices, according to some reports.
Phones sold through operators tend to be higher-end models than those bought on the open market. Contracts spread the handset cost over monthly payments, making expensive devices more accessible.
Apple played a key part in this slight shift, Counterpoint says, overtaking Samsung as the brand with the greatest market share in Europe's operator channel. Motorola and vivo are highlighted as the fastest-growing bands in the same segment.
Counterpoint associate director Jan Stryjak said operators were likely to keep gaining market share for the next few quarters at least, as the lower end of the market continues to be impacted by the memory crisis.
""However, we are seeing a steady increase in promotional activity by independent retailers compared to operators, which could mitigate some of the price rises and provide additional stimulus to the open market,"" he said.
Or perhaps more buyers might turn to the second-hand market instead, which has been predicted to see a rise in trade of 12 percent this year, as rising prices make new phones less affordable.
Counterpoint forecast that 80 percent of top-flight smartphones will feature agentic AI capabilities by 2027, meaning they will have software agents making decisions and carrying out actions on the owner's behalf. ®

28,2026-10-07,"
					By Jill Goldsmith 
Co-Business Editor

Skydance shares closed down about 7% in day two of trading Wednesday after a dip the day before, a second down session in a rocky NYSE debut for the merged company as investors started to weigh in.

	The combination of Paramount and Warner Bros. Discovery finally closed, a big step forward. A legal settlement with attorneys general who tried to block it went very much in the company’s favor. Beyond that, “It’s a show me story,” says Matthew Condon, an analyst at Citizens.

	Wall Streeters are wary of SKYD’s $80 billion in debt, high leverage, and ability to both generate a promised $6 billion in cost synergies ($3 billion each in 2027 and 2028) and ramp up content spending. There’s quite some excitement around streaming as HBO Max and Paramount+ catapult to top-tier status, but little clarity still on structure, strategy or pricing.

	










			Watch on Deadline		






RELATED: HBO Max And Paramount+ Will Eventually Combine After “Short-Term” Bundling Phase, David Ellison Says

	Chairman and CEO David Ellison and co-chief executive Ynon Kreiz at a press conference Tuesday reiterated a commitment to reduce leverage dramatically by 2028 and insisted they can spend and save at the same time.

	Condon is more sanguine than some with a “market overperform” rating and a $14 price target on the shares, which closed Wednesday down 6.8% at $8.98.

	“If you’re building a best-in-class content platform, and this is going to hopefully create a must-have streaming platform, between all the IP that Warner Bros. has and Paramount has and with 30 films coming per year that gives you a steady stream of content coming to streaming. You’ve got all these sports rights. You’ve got a $30 billion content budget. How do you optimize that to really go after the best opportunities? There are a lot of things that can go right here.”

	With the stock so low, he thinks “the risk/reward” looks favorable.

	But much of the deleveraging framework, Condon acknowledged, assumes the underlying businesses continue to perform largely in line with their pre-transaction trajectories, making execution incredibly important.

	










	“The question is, can they actually achieve” what they’ve promised, asked analyst Rich Greenfield on CNBC. “Because what Warner Bros Discovery ran into, the tidal wave they ran into, was their legacy business. The core [cable programming] businesses eroded faster than the synergies could be piled on. And so that’s going to be the real challenge facing David Ellison and Ynon Kreiz.”

	“We remain quite cautious on the ability of the company and its management to avoid integration and execution problems that have bedeviled other major media mergers,” said Doug Creutz of TD Cowen, who has a “hold” recommendation on the stock.

	The co-CEOs have modeled a cash ramp-up in coming years but meanwhile there’s not a big cushion if churn rises, cable-network declines accelerate, the box office softens again or there’s macroeconomic or geopolitical disruption. The company can’t issue more debt after Paramount’s just-concluded massive bond sale to finance the merger. Skydance does have the Ellison family’s backing. It could also potentially find new equity investors.

	One Wall Streeter believes there will be will a secondary offering at some point. “There are a lot of groups that helped in financing the deal, including the Ellisons themselves, who are going to want to sell stock … So why rush to get involved when a lot of stock is going to hit the market at some point?” 

	For now, “They need to launch streaming, show synergies, cohesiveness among management, and tell the story to investors.” 

	The Street will get an update on the new Skydance when it reports third-quarter earnings.

	“We remain guarded on the ability for management to reach its $3B net synergies goal by YE27 [year end] and $6B by YE28, but they still sound confident in their ability, and it will be quite impressive if they hit their goals – $6B is 11% of pro forma expenses, so it is not out of the realm of possibility, but we want to see how it unfolds,” said David Joyce of Seaport Research in a note.

29,2026-10-07,"Thoughts on Valve's latest hardware survey
This week, Steam published another one of their monthly hardware surveys, offering a granular market assessment based on optional and anonymous contributions. I don't read a lot of these. Perhaps if I did, I'd be less shocked to discover that 32GB is now the most popular RAM setup, with 42.22% of respondents choosing it, as against 37.82% for 16GB configurations, and 6.47% for 8GB.
Now you all see here! That is far too much RAM. It's indecent. The Apollo spacecraft guidance computer made it all the way to the Moon and back with just 4KB of on-board RAM, and yet, you would have me believe you need 8,000,000,000 times that to comfortably play a gussied-up remake of a PS2 game.  There is supposed to be a genAI-fuelled component availability crisis going on, for crying out loud. Only last week, one of the people responsible for the dearth of budget-friendly consumer hardware predicted that prices would go up significantly next year. I will make a deal with you: you are allowed to have 32GB of RAM if you go out right now and pour custard through the vents of the nearest Google Gemini datacenter.
I realise this is my usual old man yelling at cloud routine, but come on. If you won't hear it from me, hear it from one of the original developers of Thief: The Dark Project, who recently named 2010's Alan Wake as the high watermark for videogame photorealism. The original Alan Wake needs 2GB of RAM, and even that is an outrageous indulgence by the standards of, say, the Antikythera Mechanism, which probably runs Doom by now.
If I'm disgusted by the RAM situation, I'm absolutely beside myself to learn that 7.28% of you have over four terabytes of free hard drive space. What are you doing with all that spare room, renting it out to US aircraft carriers? I don't think there are even enough videogames in creation to fill up four terabytes of hard drive space. 
I'm not sure how representative the Steam survey is. I imagine that the people who accept the invitation to participate care more about gaming hardware than most, and are thus more likely to have beefy setups. Aside from provoking me to hoik my PS2 Slim from its grave 'neath the mattress and waggle it violently at the screen, the survey isn't too exhilarating. OK, so there's a noticeable uptick in people using NVIDIA GeForce RTX 5070 cards. There's also quite a dramatic spike in respondents who read and write Simplified Chinese – a 7.42% rise. Speculations on all that welcome.
I'll end with a tip of the hat to the obviously Satanic setups on the list. 0.03% of you are rocking PCs with 11 CPUs, for example. Just reading that line makes me feel the need for a shower. I will bet you £11.11 it's yet another ode to Spinal Tap.

30,2026-10-07,"It’s definitely a sign of the times when repair shops get handed sticks of registered ECC DDR5 RAM by businesses to try and fix, rather than just tossing the defective ones and slotting in a fresh bunch. This is how [northwestrepair] ended up with two of these ECC RDIMMs, with a current market valuation of a rather nice budget car.
The 128 GB stick was first on the bench, with the fault being that it would fail DDR5 training on boot. This suggests that one or more of the DRAM BGA packages had developed a cracked solder joint, which is something that you could conceivably fix with more diagnosing and testing for a single IC reballing.
In this case doing a reflow cycle was enough to bring the RAM back to life, with the DDR5 training now passing without issues in a test system.
For the 256 GB stick things wouldn’t be so easy, as it seemed completely dead. This suggested a dead PMIC, as this is the component on DDR5 RAM responsible for ensuring that the DRAM ICs even power on. The SPD ROM was another option, so that got swapped first, which does require reprogramming the RDIMM. For this the earlier SPD dump image was reflashed, but swapping this chip made no difference.
After some additional reflowing of a few suspect ICs, still nothing happened. Following this a lot of reballing and other troubleshooting steps followed, only for the problem to be one shorted MLCC and a broken PMIC, potentially caused by whoever dropped said RDIMM onto the server room floor.
Cracked MLCCs do appear to be a very common cause of failures, as they’re often used as filter capacitors, same as for this RDIMM. Checking for shorts across a number of such MLCCs can be a good way to shorten a diagnostic cycle. Since this RDIMM was dropped, reballing every IC probably was still a good move as this may have cracked some solder balls.


Up next repairing hard drives which should be a book into itself.
The shortage has now lasted a significant fraction of the length of WWII, when production was ramped up such that nearly all industries went 2x, and some specific crucial industries did 20x-35x.
I have heard the explanations of the bottlenecks and why we simply can’t do this, but it doesn’t really add up imo. It’s all just engineering problems, and there’s so much money to be made. What’s the deal? Is this a roundabout method to keep AI development under the speed limit? Maybe not intentional, but once people saw it functioning that way they decide not to build more of the fancy optical systems needed to increase capacity? That all still sounds a bit to conspiratorial.
Component complexity is the killer here. Say you need more rivets. You can mine more iron, open more smelters to process it, and more forges to make the rivet. You’ll need miners, picks, furnaces and dies.
If you want to make RAM, you need to mine more silicon, refined it, grow more crystals (this takes months), facilities for cutting and making wafers, patterning tools, packaging tools…
You need to make more tools to make more tools to make tools to make your product.
You forgot about helium used in operating wafer machines. Since the unrest in Ukraine started in 2022 helium avability went belly up. I had my little TIG welding workshop but had to cease operations in 2024 because price of helium gas was just too high.
Sadly most of factories which manufactured purified helium were located in Donbas and are now ruined. This was old but reliable 1970s technology maintained by engineers and technicans, but they are all now drafted or died in war so there’s no one to recreate old machines and start making new helium.
I did absolutely forget about that, good tip, thanks
I get that, and it makes sense… I just think that at some point it would still get brute-forced.
We also have tons more automation now than we had then. It’s more difficult than rivets, but we had to gear up RAM production to get where we are in the first place, and we should be able to do it again. I know that it has ramped up a little bit, just not enough to actually meet demand in any meaningful way that would prevent two sticks of RAM from costing a pretty serviceable Toyota Corolla.
The simplest (if not completely accurate and completely unsourced) answer is that DRAM manufactures don’t want to get bitten by a fall in demand if the AI bubble pops. It takes a LONG time to bring a DRAM fab online, years. When smartphones suddenly needed a lot of DRAM, companies rushed to open new fabs. By the time these new fabs opened the massive demand for DRAM had dried up and the companies had to foot the bill. This time around they decided to just let the price spike and keep their existing expansion timelines. That gives them a lot of immediate profit and shelters them from losses in case the new fabs end up being unnecessary. They were already spinning up new fabs but not to the scale that current demand requires.
…That’s a very sensible point actually, why didn’t I think of that? Thanks
In most extreme fabs are anchored directly into solid bedrock to achieve absolute stillness (via tens of thousands of deep shafts, filled with concrete). The mass helps to attenuate vibrations. Then the cleanrooms are floated above this foundation to further dampen vibrations. There is an insane amount of analysis and calibration that can last years involved. And not all locations around the world are suitable.
Yeah, I have heard the difficulty of production described and I believe it, I just also know that they built those fabs in the first place, and there’s definitely still suitable spots to build more if they needed to. Even if it’s extremely difficult, there’s just no way that we are incapable of making more of this commodity, and there’s a huge profit incentive to do so, so maybe there’s some non-engineering reason I’m missing. Some people here have brought up a few reasonable options
I’ll expose my ignorance – assuming this is needed for 2 nanometre chips, what if you “settled” for five nanometre designs?
Or ten, even twenty nanometre?
Well obviously the chips get bigger, and RAM per chip goes down, but your fab costs / requirements should also drop. If the engraving beam suddenly has (for example) a 3nm wobble tolerance for 10nm designs, there’s got to be a cash value saving associated.
Paradox of chip design is the smaller the node, the harder it is to switch it without electrons flying through silicon. With 130 nm Northwood chips it was rather easy to overclock, now it’s not so easy because clock speed is more restricted. Capacitive effects also play a role in loss so the more transistors switch in unit of surface the more capacitance gets shovvled about and the higher the current. At some point you must decide if you want more cores, more speed or more transistors because with constant power the chip will melt.
Why would they want to sink billions into new fabs just so they can sell their products for less money? From their point of view, everything is fking awesome right now.
If this made any sense, then why would any industrial producer ever develop economies of scale? They almost unanimously do choose to scale up and usually end up making astronomically more money afterwards. I think you need to spend more time on that theory.
The exception would be if demand immediately collapses afterwards and they’re left holding the bag, as G details above.. But I kind of don’t really buy that RAM demand is going away any time soon. Maybe it’ll level off or even reduce, not sure.
You are confusing Adam Smith Capitalism with Jack Welch Capitalism. The former is ‘make good products at a fair price’, the latter is ‘who cares if it makes any sense as long as number go up’.
Fair dibs
If this made any sense, then why would any industrial producer ever develop economies of scale?
you’re missing the amount of capital involved, and barriers to entry.
Often business will make more of something (instead of just putting prices up) because if they don’t someone else will.
However, if that someone else will need an large amount of capital, and have significant issues making it, you don’t have to worry about it as much and a business can simply put the price up – it does happen quite often, though this is an extreme case..
As one of the posts above says, why should they spend billions to make more capacity to lower price? Instead they can just act put the prices up ie act like any monopoly does..
my understanding of the shortage is that all of this stuff being produced in substitution for what consumers would buy is all accumulating unused in warehouses, since the server farms aren’t built and even if they were, there would be no power for them.  at some point the dumb money (which is all that is causing this) will realize there is no business model that works for LLMs and the whole thing will collapse, at which point consumers will have to figure out what to do with the high bandwidth memory that is being warehoused. hopefully there will be special motherboards soon thereafter

32,2026-10-07,"CoreWeave’s Navi Mumbai campus will run Nvidia’s Vera Rubin chips from mid-2028. Bloomberg puts the investment at multiple billions of dollars, and CoreWeave can double the capacity.
CoreWeave will open its first data centres in India, using 240 megawatts of capacity at an AdaniConneX campus in Navi Mumbai. The US AI cloud provider announced the deal on Wednesday. CoreWeave expects the first phase to come online in mid-2028.
AdaniConneX is a 50:50 joint venture between Adani Enterprises and data centre operator EdgeConneX. CoreWeave will be the only tenant across three 80-megawatt buildings at the Taloja campus. It has an option to add up to another 240 megawatts there.
The investment will total multiple billions of dollars over the life of the project, Bloomberg reported. CoreWeave did not give a figure in its statement.
“India has the talent, developers, and ambition to become a major center of AI innovation,” said Sachin Jain, CoreWeave’s chief operating officer.
The company plans to install Nvidia’s Vera Rubin platform for training, inference, reasoning and AI agents. The buildings will use direct liquid cooling for the GPU halls, CoreWeave said. Their chilled water system does not rely on evaporation.
CoreWeave will also open an office in India and hire local staff. The company linked the investment to the government’s IndiaAI Mission.
India is CoreWeave’s second market in Asia. In August, the company announced its first data centre projects in Indonesia. There, Nvidia-backed Zankore has raised a $3.1bn GPU loan.
CoreWeave had about 4.2 gigawatts of contracted power as of its earnings call on 11 August. Chief executive Mike Intrator said last month that the company is “largely sold out” of capacity, Bloomberg reported.
AdaniConneX is also one of the companies working on Google’s data centre project in India. That Google AI hub has been cleared for 2.51 gigawatts, the Guardian reported in September.
India built about 2.2 gigawatts of operational data centre capacity over the past three decades. A July report by Wood Mackenzie forecasts 12 gigawatts by 2030. It projects AI-specific capacity to grow almost 24-fold to 6.5 gigawatts.
CoreWeave, which listed on Nasdaq in March 2025, rents out GPU computing to AI companies. Specialist providers like it are known as neoclouds. Nvidia has held talks with insurers about loans backed by its chips, the Financial Times reported in September.
