33,2026-10-07,"York IE led the round in the New York startup, which was founded by Romanians Andrei Negrau and Lisa Popovici.
Siena, a New York-based startup that develops AI agents for consumer brands, has raised $17 million in its Series A round, with York IE as the lead investor. The new capital raised brings the company’s total funding to $29.7 million.
Moreover, Joyance Ventures took part in the round as a new investor, and the existing investors, Aglaé Ventures, Sierra Ventures, and the Super Angel Fund, also did so.
Siena was founded by Andrei Negrau, the company’s chief executive, and Lisa Popovici, its president, both of whom are from Romania.
They began with a single AI agent that responds to customer service inquiries for online shops. Since that time, it has developed agents to carry out other kinds of jobs.
The Shopping Agent answers questions about products and helps people to make purchases without having to leave the chat, and the Social Agent responds to comments and direct messages on social media. A voice agent for use with phone calls is also being developed.
The funds being raised will be used to develop more agents that can act independently, as well as to create the shared system that connects them. This is what Siena refers to as its Agent of Record.
In reality, it combines a brand’s orders, subscriptions, reviews, and previous customer chats so that each agent knows what the others have learned.
For instance, if a customer returns a product because it didn’t fit, the shopping agent can take that into account the next time. Negrau states that most customer software is divided into separate tools for support, social, and marketing.
“The customer is not particularly interested in your org chart,” he said.
The company states that hundreds of brands use its software, including SPANX, FIGS, HexClad, and Comfrt, and that its agents can handle up to 80% of customer interactions.
York IE, the team that won the round, has the potential to grow well beyond the level of support.
“We believe Siena can become as essential to running a brand as its commerce platform,” said Tom Holahan, a general partner at York IE.
Siena enters a crowded market, where competitors like Gorgias, Intercom’s Fin, and Sierra all offer AI agents for customer service, and investors have also supported companies that assess the quality of support work.
Siena doesn’t charge on a per-user-seat basis; rather, brands are charged based on the results they achieve and the data tools they use. The company has now started hiring.

34,2026-10-07,"The Surface Laptop Ultra starts at $2,599.99 and Microsoft claims it returns local AI prompts twice as fast as an Apple MacBook Pro M5 and generates video six times quicker, two figures it has published no test methodology for, while the only measurement it does footnote is screen brightness
Microsoft says the Surface Laptop Ultra has published repair guides, replacement parts and upgradeable storage.
Microsoft priced its first Nvidia-powered Surface at $2,599.99 and claimed on stage that it doubles a MacBook Pro M5 on local AI prompts, without publishing how either figure was measured. The chip inside is built on Arm’s architecture, from the Cambridge company Nvidia failed to buy in 2022.
Microsoft has priced its first Nvidia-powered laptop at $2,599.99 and opened pre-orders. It says the Surface Laptop Ultra returns prompts from locally run AI models twice as fast as Apple’s MacBook Pro M5, and generates video six times quicker. It has published no test methodology for either claim.
Executives made the comparison on stage in San Francisco on Wednesday, Bloomberg reported. Microsoft’s own product page carries the price, a processor of up to 20 Nvidia Grace cores, up to 128GB of unified memory and up to 2TB of storage.
The only measurement it footnotes is the screen. Peak brightness of 2,000 nits, it says, measured over a 10% window with HDR content, against laptops available in October 2026. The Apple numbers get no equivalent.
We asked the price question on Wednesday morning.
Our launch preview noted that analysts thought cost was the open question, because a memory shortage had pushed hardware up and Nvidia had raised its DGX Spark by about 75%, to $6,950. At $2,599.99 the Surface undercuts a 16-inch MacBook Pro by roughly $400.
The chip is the more interesting part.
RTX Spark pairs a Blackwell graphics processor with Nvidia’s Grace CPU, co-designed with MediaTek, and Nvidia claims up to one petaflop of AI compute. Dell, HP, Lenovo, Asus and MSI are shipping machines built on it. That puts Nvidia into a laptop market Intel and AMD have shared for decades.
It gets there on someone else’s instruction set.
Grace is built on Arm’s architecture, licensed from the Cambridge company Nvidia agreed to buy for $40B and could not, after regulators objected and SoftBank confirmed the deal dead in February 2022. Every RTX Spark laptop now pays Arm a royalty instead.
The buyers are the problem.
European laptop shipments are forecast to fall 6.4% this quarter and 20% in the fourth, with desktops down almost 30%, as memory costs raise system prices and companies stretch their refresh cycles. Gartner does not expect AI PCs to be a majority of shipments until 2028.
Microsoft’s pitch has an unusual audience.
Its executives argued that running AI on the machine in front of you is cheaper and more secure than paying a cloud provider. The Dutch tax office reached that conclusion about its email this week, and is moving it off Microsoft’s cloud onto its own servers.

				Our gifting experts are obsessively searching for holiday gifts that are unique, thoughtful and suitable for any budget.

Tech
US adults are juggling higher tech prices with smaller budgets this year. Can the sales help them afford more?
Senior Consumer Insights Editor
Senior Consumer Insights Editor
October 7, 2026, 6:07 pm ET
Early holiday sales are here, but so are bills. 
The holidays have never been merciful when it comes to balancing everyday expenses, so juggling your necessities during a busy (and expensive) shopping season can be tough. There’s a glimpse of that in CNET’s latest holiday survey. This time last year, holiday spending for tech products and services was higher, with the average US adult spending $931. Now, that budget has shrunk to $808, a $123 dip from last year’s survey findings. 
But there have been so many new releases this year, like Apple’s AirPods 5, the highly anticipated Legend of Zelda-themed Switch 2 console, and even Apple’s first foldable phone, the iPhone Duo. We can’t all comfortably fit new devices into smaller holiday budgets. The real question is, who’s to blame for me saying, “Bah humbug!” when I start my holiday shopping — the economy or tech brands? 
More from CNET
Let’s be real, we’re juggling a lot financially. There are higher prices on everything from everyday goods to gas and utilities. Covering necessary expenses doesn’t leave much room to pay for new personal tech that costs hundreds, even thousands, for some devices like the iPhone Duo or the iPhone 18 Pro Max. 
But why are US adults spending less on tech when so many new devices were released this year? CNET Senior Editor Adam Oram, an expert deal hunter, says tech is becoming unaffordable, and we’re dealing with higher everyday living costs. We’re having to be more mindful about how we spend our money — and some new tech simply isn’t worth it. 
Think about the rising cost of tech products, but the lack of innovation. For example, if you have the iPhone 17 Pro, there aren’t many differences from the iPhone 18 Pro that justify the cost to upgrade. What’s more, even if you’re considering existing tech products, you’re seeing price increases due to the RAM shortage, which has driven up hardware and service costs around the world.
“That means people are perhaps keeping their phone for an extra year or persevering with a laptop for a little longer, rather than jumping on the next shiny thing,” Oram says. It also could mean nonessential tech purchases fall into the luxury bucket, which may have less cash in it than before. And since many paychecks aren’t stretching as far, that doesn’t leave much discretionary money to splurge on nonessential items, such as upgrading your smartphone. 
The answer depends. If your holiday budget is tighter this year, you may be hoping that sales can help you buy the tech you want. However, don’t expect retailers to slash tech prices as much this year, Oram says. For all the reasons listed above, don’t expect big savings, like half off the original price, during early holiday sales, especially for newer device models. 
“In previous years, holiday sales have made for a great time to upgrade big-ticket tech items, but with rising component costs, these deals might not be as staggering this year as in the past,” he says. “That said, if you have a busted TV, laptop or tablet that needs replacing, those items should still top your list as they rarely go on sale for less than around the holidays.”
You may still be able to find deals at different retailers throughout the month of October, depending on the brand, retailer and model of the device you’re looking for. Oram adds that you can expect plenty of deals on items that will likely fall into most people’s “nice-to-have” list, such as smart home and kitchen gadgets. It’s also a good time to stock up on presents and spread the overall cost over the next few months rather than shopping at the last minute. 
On the other hand, if you don’t need to replace a device right away, it may be worthwhile to just wait a few more months. 
“Waiting for the hype around new product launches to die down can lead to scoring a better deal,” Oram says, “especially for things like phones, where carriers begin incentivizing upgrades by offering solid discounts after the initial buzz.”
For now, Oram doesn’t expect prices to drop significantly on tech, so think about whether you need to upgrade versus just wanting to. If you’re planning to upgrade, cover the needs first, and the wants after, if you can afford to.

		© 2026 CNET, a Ziff Davis company. All rights reserved. 	"
36,2026-10-07,"The AI arms race is a sprint to build the best model — or so you thought.
Andrew Huberman, the Stanford neuroscientist and podcaster, said that tech companies, including OpenAI, Meta, and Anthropic, will ultimately become biotech companies, pursuing ways to read and alter brain activity without implants.
""The goal is to be able to read and write from your brain non-invasively,"" he said in an episode of ""Invest Like The Best"" released on Tuesday.
That could mean tools that help people fall asleep, learn, focus, or regulate stress, he said. More provocatively, he imagines devices that could selectively increase motivation while someone tackles difficult work, then turn that state off so they can recover afterward.
""A non-invasive device where you could literally turn up — crank up — your level of motivation"" is where the technology is headed, he said.
""That's what the real arms race of AI is about,"" he added.
OpenAI, Meta, and Anthropic did not immediately respond to requests for comment.
That future is still a long way off, but early versions of consumer neurotech are already here.
Neurable sells headphones that monitor focus and fatigue, while Mave, Halo Neuroscience, and LIFTiD market brain-stimulation headsets intended to improve concentration.
Take a smarter break in your day - and see how far you get.

                                    Add BI in Google so our reporting is easier to find when you’re searching for what matters.
                                  
Sona, Truvaga, and Pulsetto sell vagus-nerve-stimulation wearables designed to help users wind down and manage stress.
None can yet reliably dial a worker's motivation up or down in the precise, AI-personalized way Huberman imagines.
He expects the shift to begin with consumer sleep technology, including devices that measure and influence eye movements or the autonomic nervous system. Eventually, he said, eye masks, implants, and perhaps one day glasses and wristbands could offer more direct ways to influence a person's mental state.
He predicted that some entrepreneurs could soon consider implantable vagus-nerve stimulators, which can affect alertness and have clinical uses, as readily as they consider cosmetic piercings.
Bryan Johnson, the longevity entrepreneur behind Blueprint, is among Pulsetto's high-profile users. In his Netflix documentary, ""Don't Die,"" he said he uses it for 10 minutes if he wakes in the night because it puts him back to sleep ""pretty quickly.""
Huberman acknowledged the obvious concern: whether people would make ""their entire brain"" vulnerable to noninvasive control. He said safety mechanisms would need to be built into such devices.
Still, he is broadly optimistic. Existing tools such as caffeine, medication, exercise, and breathwork are blunt ways of changing the brain, he said. The next wave will be more personalized and precise, with AI learning which level of stimulation produces focus or motivation for a particular person, he said.
""We're going to start shifting brain states into focus, into modes for learning,"" Huberman said. ""And we're going to start doing it with technologies.""

41,2026-10-07,"Spotify today announced a global rollout of its audiobooks section, giving users in more than 180 markets access to over 350,000 titles spanning more than 120 languages. Here are the details.
Four years after launching audiobooks in the U.S., and following a rather limited expansion into select countries in the years since, Spotify today announced that audiobooks are finally coming to more than 180 markets.
As Spotify explained in a blog post, the rollout begins today and will continue through the end of 2026, expanding audiobook access from 22 markets to more than 180 and making the service available to more than 750 million people worldwide.
Users in newly supported markets will have access to more than 350,000 titles, with additional catalogs available in Spanish, Portuguese, Italian, Polish, and other languages.
Access will vary depending on the market. Eligible Premium subscribers in countries including Brazil, Mexico, Colombia, Poland, Chile, Norway, Peru, Italy, Spain, and Portugal will get 12 hours of audiobook listening each month as part of their existing subscription.
Spotify is also expanding Audiobooks+, an add-on that gives subscribers additional listening hours each month. The service will serve as the primary way to access audiobooks in markets across Africa and Asia, as well as Argentina.
Users will also be able to purchase individual audiobooks outright, while pricing and availability will vary by market.
The company also announced an update to Page Match, its feature for switching between physical or digital books and their audiobook versions. Users can now scan a book’s ISBN barcode, and then enter a page number to find the corresponding point in the audiobook. Spotify added that the feature now works in more than 30 languages.
Audiobooks+ is also expanding to Student plans in Ireland, Belgium, Austria, and Finland beginning in November. Spotify says the add-on has surpassed 1 million subscribers since launching just over a year ago and now generates more than $100 million in annual recurring revenue.
According to Spotify, monthly audiobook listeners grew nearly 40% over the past year, while total listening hours increased by more than 30%.
To read Spotify’s full announcement, follow this link.

42,2026-10-07,"This as-told-to essay is based on a conversation with Paul Whitten, 37, the founder of Nashville Adventures. It's been edited for length and clarity.
I run history and walking tours here in good ol' Music City. I left a great job at Amazon in early 2024 to start my walking tour company in Nashville, where my wife and I moved in 2021. My wife thought I was crazy for doing this, but it's grown every year by triple digits.
I created the LLC in September 2023 and started running it nights and weekends while still working at Amazon. I did a soft launch on weekends in November 2023 at the end of the off-season, but this was just me doing tours for free.
When I started, I partnered with another company called Tours by Foot — I looked up who was number-one in walking tours of Nashville at the time, and they were, so I just called them up, and I gave free tours there on the weekends just for tips. I made $40 on my first tour and was so ecstatic. We're still part of the Tours by Foot network, but we do paid tours there now.
I quit my full-time job at Amazon in February 2024, which is when I fully launched my business and it became my full-time job.
In 2025, my business's gross profit was just under six figures with total revenue of just over six figures. Now in the first eight months of 2026, our gross profit is already over six figures with revenue in the mid-six figures. I hired my first employee — an additional tour guide to work with me — about a year in as well.
I chose walking tours because they combine three of my favorite things: talking about history, public speaking, and making money.
I was in the US Army from 2007 to 2014 and when I was in Afghanistan, I got injured and was sent to a warrior transition unit in the US. After my surgeries in the morning, I volunteered to give tours at the Patton Museum in the afternoon for three weeks.
I loved it; when I looked back at what I had the most fun working at in my life, it was giving tours for free.
Before getting a corporate job, I literally went from the Army to the Peace Corps in three years. I joined Amazon in 2019 as an area operations manager.
At the time, Amazon was growing its fulfillment needs, so while I ended up in tech, I started in fulfillment. My ability to work well with the contractors and other stakeholders got recognized, and I moved laterally into the tech field. I became an automation program manager at Amazon in 2021.
What I realized working there is that I do have a unique skill set. I can talk to anybody, and I can help people from wildly different arenas get on the same wavelength.
I made six figures in Big Tech, but I make more now. These three strategies are the ones that helped my small business income and profit grow quickly:
I started my company with $3,600, most of which went to getting the URL.
When I started this business, I didn't take a salary for eight months so that I wouldn't need outside funding.
I wasn't scared not to have a salary — I didn't have a daughter yet and my wife was still working, so we still had health insurance. I also learned from my three years in the Peace Corps — when I lived off $140 a month in the Republic of Armenia — that I can survive without a big cash flow and can be happy with next to nothing. My wife was in the same environment.
We don't need much, and we still spend pretty much the same as I did in the Peace Corps. Living below your means is a great way to survive and thrive in difficult environments. It lets you control your own destiny better when you know you can thrive on less than what others think is a lot.
My advice if you want to go this route is to reduce as much risk as you can by cutting back on every expense you have before you jump. This will help you make sure that you have an ability to survive as you try to make your business sustainable on its own.
I've always had a passion for history. I've never taken a business class in my life, but by pure luck, I noticed there was a gap in the market of tourism in Nashville. Everyone caters to a very specific audience in Nashville — the bachelorette party market — and I found another niche here that works: people interested in the authentic, true history of Nashville.
I picked unsexy walking tours on purpose because I could actually rank for them on Google — so I taught myself SEO by literally reading library books and became the search result for Nashville history tours. That's worked to rank well on GEO for AI search too, since it's pretty much the same foundations of SEO.
The great thing about a walking tour is that it has an extremely low barrier of entry. I could afford a pair of tennis shoes to give a tour, but I couldn't afford a $250,000 sightseeing bus. So walking tours were the manner of me getting to play in this arena with the budget I had at the time.
Having a really unique set of history knowledge and an interesting set of nerd knowledge that I was able to spit out in a fun way — that launched us.
I treated every single guest like they'd go tell 10 people, because in this business sphere, they do. That word of mouth plus free search traffic is our whole engine — no investors, no loans, just being findable and good.
I can't stand scripted tours so we don't do scripts — those are one step away from AI giving a tour. We find out where people are from, and then we adapt that tour to them based on what their interests are, after getting to know them in the first 30 minutes of the tour.
Like if I mention George Jones and someone says, ""I love him,"" then we'll go into a honky tonk just for that customer. Half these honky-tonks have historical things on the wall that are practically museum pieces.
Nudie's Honky Tonk has Elvis Presley's jacket in it; Tootsie's has Loretta Lynn's shirt in a dirty frame on a dirty wall. So we'll be like, ""Hey, let me show you this real quick.""
We get that connection quickly with guests when we provide that little bit of a special feeling. Then all of a sudden, it's not just a tour — it's a backstage pass.
Every job I've had before this makes me appreciate what I'm doing as an entrepreneur even more. I've done so many crazy things in my 20s, and one thing I got out of it is a firmer understanding of how to appreciate things now.
I want to have fun. I've had real jobs — they suck. I like this one. It took me 36 years to find a job I liked, and I had to make it myself.
You read all these books about the role of passion, and I always thought that was all b.s. until I lived it. Passion kept me going when I was making $40 on a Saturday and I was ecstatic about it — it was the sheer happiness of doing something I loved.

43,2026-10-07,"You're viewing the US website. Looking for the Site edition?
How Alexandra Zatarain and Matteo Franceschetti, a husband-and-wife team, built a profitable $1.5 billion company on “sleep fitness.”
Copied to clipboard

		Opinions expressed by Entrepreneur contributors are their own.	
People hear “Eight Sleep,” and their brains go straight to a mattress cover. The real story is more personal than that. Alexandra Zatarain was losing her father to cancer, and she noticed that one of the first things his illness took away was his sleep.
That detail shaped everything that came next. Alexandra, a marketer and storyteller by training, co-founded Eight Sleep in 2014, shortly after her father passed. The loss made the mission personal and intentional. If sleep is the first place the body signals that something’s wrong, then the bed you’re already lying in could be the earliest health check you have. That’s what she and her husband and co-founder, Matteo Franceschetti, set out to build: a bed that catches what a doctor’s appointment often catches too late. 
Alexandra believes we’ll get to a point where your bed diagnoses you. As of March 2026, investors are betting on that idea at a $1.5 billion valuation, and the company is already profitable.
I’ve slept on an Eight Sleep Pod for a few months, and I sat down with Alexandra because, as a fellow Mexican-American founder working in health and tech, I’ve long admired what she’s built in a field that rarely makes room for women who look like us. Here’s how they got there, and what founders can learn from it.
Most origin stories get polished for the pitch deck, but this one is better with the rough edges left in. Matteo grew up in Italy as a professional-grade overachiever. A competitive ski racer, tennis player and race-car driver whose father was a hillclimbing champion, he became a securities lawyer and launched two clean-tech companies on two continents, all before age 30. Sleepless nights were the cost of that life, made worse by restless legs syndrome. He’d spent his career engineering athletic recovery, and kept circling one question: why were the eight hours where the body actually recovers the one part nobody had bothered to measure?
Alexandra grew up on the U.S.–Mexico border, built a marketing career at Wall Street firms and startups, and, at age 24, walked away from a stable New York job to build with Matteo. She didn’t need convincing that sleep mattered; she slept well and already saw it as a pillar of health. She also knew something the industry kept ignoring: a woman’s sleep shifts with every decade and every life stage.
The couple was selling a promise before the now-renowned Eight Sleep Pod existed, and there was no obvious shelf for it. The competition argued about coils and foam. So-called cooling mattresses, in Alexandra’s blunt words, didn’t actually cool. 
“Nobody was building solutions,” she said. “They were building things they could market.” So the team zeroed in on temperature, knowing that if they could solve that, they would be solving a huge share of what ruins people’s nights. Snoring is second on the list.
Traditional mattresses have been around for decades. The category of “sleep fitness” has not.
Most early buyers came to Eight Sleep after years of wrecked rest. Alexandra, who leads brand, growth, and communications, coined “sleep fitness” to recast sleep as something you train, track, and optimize — no different from a workout. That repositioning pulled the bed out of the furniture showroom, where legacy makers fight over foam and price, and into recovery tech, where the competition is about data and outcomes and people gladly pay for ongoing improvement. It’s why Eight Sleep reads less like a mattress brand and more like Whoop or Oura.
Matteo takes the thesis to its provocative edge: sleep as a lever to engineer, squeezing efficiency out of the night to maximize the day. It’s a bold founder narrative, even if medical consensus holds that adults still need seven to nine hours. Both co-founders stress architecture over duration — six restorative hours beats eight restless ones. The goal is simple: everyone wants to wake up feeling unstoppable, and the Pod is built to deliver that.
Paired with the hardware is a recurring membership. I questioned Alexandra on the cost and she explained the technicalities of how processing and acting on a sleeper’s biometrics continuously throughout the night is costly. You’re not buying furniture; you’re funding a nightly, real-time intervention.
Going into business with your spouse is what most advisors warn against. Alexandra and Matteo did it anyway, and she’s refreshingly unromantic about how it works. Matteo is the ultimate decision-maker; he genuinely and often seeks her opinion, but he makes the final call. Alexandra owns brand, growth and communications. They keep the lanes clean right down to their phones: business lives on one messaging app, everything personal on another, so the two never bleed together. Which app is which, she keeps to herself, and that’s sort of the point.
When I asked what she’d tell her 24-year-old self, just launching the company, she didn’t talk about strategy. 
“It’s very much about people, and I got my partners right,” she said. 
You need the right people to build anything hard, and she’s candid that it was hard, the kind of hard where you want to quit, many times. What pulled the company back every time came down to the team. She said it like a dare: you can, and you will.
The competitor in both of them never fully switches off, either. Every morning, they compare sleep scores, a standing contest over who recovered better overnight. It’s the most on-brand thing a couple running a sleep-fitness company could do: the race-car driver and the marketer, turning REM into a leaderboard before coffee.
The Pod’s smartest move might be that it’s built for two. It splits down the middle so each person runs their own temperature, as most couples are quietly at war over the thermostat, and because pregnancy, menopause, and hormonal shifts change what a body needs in ways the mattress industry never bothered to design for.
Image Credit: Eight Sleep
Almost every sleep device on the market simply reports. Your Oura ring, your Whoop band and your Apple Watch hand you a morning scorecard and leave the fixing to you. Eight Sleep acts on the data while you’re still asleep — cooling your side before the heat wakes you, shifting the base when you snore. This intervention beats a tracker, and it points the company toward a far bigger prize: medicine itself. 
It’s pursuing FDA clearance to detect and mitigate sleep apnea, which would turn a wellness device into something a doctor can prescribe and insurance can reimburse, delivered from the bed you already sleep in.
You can see this direction in the Pod 6, recently launched on September 23. It features a smaller Hub that finally slides under most bed frames, cooling that’s 20% faster, and nine times more sensors — enough to tell two sleepers apart and ignore a pet who climbs in. The Pod 6 is also the first product offered in solo sizes, starting at $1,999, the lowest entry price in the company’s history. More beds mean more data, and more data trains the diagnosis engine.
The most pointed work is in women’s health, and it’s a lane Alexandra has made her own. Nearly 80% of women get hot flashes and night sweats during the menopause transition, often for seven years or more. As estrogen drops, the body’s comfortable temperature range narrows, so a small rise in heat that a younger woman would sleep through instead triggers a 2 a.m. drenching. 
As a woman in perimenopause, I can attest that the temperature regulation has really helped my sleep. Each night, I dial the cooling up or down, and the bed adjusts to my body through the night. Instead of waking up sweaty, I sleep straight through. A cooling bed can’t touch hormones, but it goes after the heat that actually wakes you — exactly what Hot Flash Mode and Pod 6’s faster cooling are built for. Add Pregnancy Mode, made for the way sleep shifts across trimesters, and the insight Alexandra brought on day one — that a woman’s sleep changes with every decade — is finally built into the bed itself.
Twelve years in, Eight Sleep is free-cash-flow positive, with cumulative Pod sales approaching $1 billion, a footprint in 37 countries, and more than a billion hours of sleep analyzed. These metrics are what the March 2026 round, $50 million led by Tether Investments at a $1.5 billion valuation, is betting on: a company that can afford the medical future it’s chasing.
For all the talk of AI and FDA filings, Alexandra’s own routine is almost aggressively simple: in bed before 10:30 p.m, no alcohol, limited caffeine, dinner by 7:30 or 8 p.m. Proof that she’s a real person and not a wellness robot is that she’s on her phone until the last second before she goes to sleep. 
“It works for me,” she says. Her non-negotiable: never socks in bed, because the body needs its extremities free to shed heat. If you trap that, you fight your own physiology all night.
For Alexandra, this was never about being a broken sleeper looking for salvation. She slept well. Her stake came from losing her father and understanding what it means when sleep is the first thing a body loses. Matteo came from the other direction: years of his own restless-legs-driven, broken sleep and an athlete’s refusal to accept it. Reaching the same conviction from opposite ends, the couple realized that the bed you already own might be the most important health device in your house.
I believe them, and not only because I’ve slept on it for months. I admired Alexandra as a fellow Mexican-American female founder building at the intersection of health and technology, and the Pod has delivered beyond the marketing. My husband and I now compete on sleep scores, and I no longer wear socks to bed. Underneath the ambition, the fundamentals are real: profitability, a decade of data, and a product people don’t quit. The vision is credible and this is the bed that, before long, may help diagnose you.
People hear “Eight Sleep,” and their brains go straight to a mattress cover. The real story is more personal than that. Alexandra Zatarain was losing her father to cancer, and she noticed that one of the first things his illness took away was his sleep.
That detail shaped everything that came next. Alexandra, a marketer and storyteller by training, co-founded Eight Sleep in 2014, shortly after her father passed. The loss made the mission personal and intentional. If sleep is the first place the body signals that something’s wrong, then the bed you’re already lying in could be the earliest health check you have. That’s what she and her husband and co-founder, Matteo Franceschetti, set out to build: a bed that catches what a doctor’s appointment often catches too late. 
Alexandra believes we’ll get to a point where your bed diagnoses you. As of March 2026, investors are betting on that idea at a $1.5 billion valuation, and the company is already profitable.

Dame Julia Hoggett says negative sentiment about the UK market has pushed companies to leave in the past
The UK needs to do more back its own companies at a time when a growing number are choosing to list their shares in the US rather than at home, the boss of the London Stock Exchange (LSE) has told the BBC.
Dame Julia Hoggett said the government needed to make it ""more attractive"" to invest in the UK stock market, otherwise big firms would continue to look overseas for their next stage of growth.
Over the last few years, scores of big firms have left the London market, are considering a move or have been bought by private foreign investors.
The fear is this weakens the UK economy by reducing tax revenues and depressing business valuations.
""If we want Britain to back Britain, which is what I hear the chancellor and the prime minister saying, then let's make sure that we're creating structural incentives to do so,"" Dame Julia told the BBC's Big Boss podcast.
""We need to take the handbrake off."" 
The LSE's main market is made up of around 930 companies with a total market value of about Â£4.9 trillion.
Almost 40% are international businesses, hailing from over 80 countries.
But in the last few years, many firms have delisted or moved away from the LSE, including takeaway chain Just Eat, which joined the Amsterdam stock exchange,  travel giant Tui which opted for Frankfurt, and Paddy Power-owner Flutter which now trades in New York.
Meanwhile, the number of companies newly listing their shares in London has dwindled.
Last year, there were 23 initial public offerings (IPOs) on the London market, with Â£2.1bn raised. In the US, which has much larger capital markets, there were 354 with $44bn (Â£33bn) raised.
It has coincided in a big rise of UK investment money flowing into US stocks in search of better returns.
""We talk as a nation about wanting growth in every postcode, but at the moment, a lot of us are funding growth in every zip code,"" Dame Julia said.
There was ""no shortage of great companies and no shortage of capital"", she added.
But negative sentiment about the UK market - which was often exaggerated - had contributed to companies leaving in the past, she said.
""We need to stop throwing shade at ourselves as a nation... it's a national habit."" 
Big Boss Interview: LSE's Dame Julia Hoggett
The boss of the London Stock Exchange speaks to the BBC about the attraction of the UK as a place for companies to list.
However, she said British people needed more ""incentives"" to invest in UK stocks.
She wants the government to scrap the 0.5% tax, external Britons pay when they purchase UK shares, pointing out there is no tax when they buy foreign stocks.
She also supports the idea of tax credits for Britons investing domestically. The UK had such a scheme until 2016.
Business lobbying group the Confederation of British Industry has called for urgent action to halt the exodus of firms from the London Stock Exchange.
It said that lighter regulation, better marketing and incentives for investors were needed to stem the outflow.
The government declined to say if stock market reform would be part of its Budget this month.
""As has always been the case, decisions on tax are a matter for the chancellor to set out at fiscal events, rather than routinely commenting on rumour, speculation or proposals,"" a spokesman said.

By Miranda Bonifield
What’s the deal with plastic straws?
 Heartbreaking images of sea turtles afflicted by soda straws may be distressing, but well-researched environmentalists know that the best way to save the seas isn’t banning Seattle’s straws. Not only are such bans a disadvantage to the disabled people who rely on plastic straws in their daily lives, but they don’t really clean up the oceans. (For instance, Starbucks’ move to straw-free lids will actually use more plastic.)
It’s estimated that more than a quarter of the ocean’s plastic pollutants originate in just ten rivers in Asia and Africa with insufficient waste management practices. So banning straws and other plastics isn’t environmentalism, it’s virtue signaling.
Expanding the nanny state won’t save the sea turtles. To really take the trash out of the oceans, we should be focusing our energies on promoting effective waste management practices. Organizations like the Asian Development Bank and the Asia Foundation inform local governments and empower local communities to mitigate their waste management problems. Meanwhile, proper recycling, voluntarily avoiding disposable plastics, and community beach cleanups are all accessible solutions for everyday environmentalists.
Miranda Bonifield is a Research Associate at Cascade Policy Institute, Oregon’s free market public policy research organization.

47,2026-10-07,"FCC filings reveal the designs of three upcoming LG smart home accessories that Apple reportedly helped develop, including a thermostat, a temperature sensor, and a deadbolt lock.
Yesterday, Bloomberg reported that Apple is working with LG Electronics on accessories for its upcoming home hub and other home products, citing regulatory documents without sharing the filings. FCC records MacRumors found contain label-placement drawings, three-dimensional renderings, and technical reports, providing a first look at the products' housings and additional details about their capabilities. 
The smart thermostat, model TMCTAW5W, has a round, puck-like body with a raised edge. The drawing shows its back, with a small socket near the center and two round bumps near the bottom. Its specifications list a 24-volt AC power supply.
The Matter logo and QR setup code sit in the middle of the back, with regulatory text printed in a circle around them. A separate drawing shows the wiring connections arranged in two curved rows, with one on each side. LG's logo appears in the printing instructions.
The thermostat supports 2.4 GHz and 5 GHz Wi-Fi, Bluetooth, and Thread. A dedicated radio test report also confirms a built-in 60 GHz radar. This could enable presence or approach detection.
According to the leaker known as ""pdfu,"" the thermostat offers a ""Far Sight Display"" setting, an option to display humidity, and controls for screen brightness and language. The leaker also lists temperature calibration and temperature offset settings, allowing users to adjust its temperature readings.
The temperature sensor, model TMST2A6W, is also round and runs on a 3-volt CR2477 coin battery. On its back, the product name, model number, and manufacturing information curve around the outer edge.
The middle contains the Matter setup code on the left, a battery warning symbol in the center, and a UL certification mark on the right. Its test report confirms Bluetooth Low Energy and Thread connectivity.
The temperature sensor measures both temperature and humidity, according to pdfu. Its magnetic back allows it to attach directly to metal surfaces, while an included metal plate with adhesive enables mounting on other surfaces. The sensor's battery is replaceable, and it provides alerts when a replacement is needed.
The smart deadbolt lock has a tall interior unit with straight sides and curved top and bottom ends. The filings reveal Graphite and Silver versions of the device, with model numbers TMCDD15G and TMCDD15S, respectively.
It runs on four AA batteries, which sit vertically side by side in a compartment in the upper half. Below them, a large, rotating circular section takes up almost the full width of the lower half. The Matter setup code and regulatory text sit above the battery compartment, near the top of the unit. 
The lock supports Bluetooth, Thread, near-field communication, and ultra wideband (UWB). An accompanying FCC order explains that it first verifies a compatible device's access credentials over Bluetooth, then uses UWB to determine its position before granting access. This provides more detail about the proximity-based automatic unlocking described in yesterday's report.
According to pdfu, the lock's keypad rotates open, with an included swivel pin allowing movement to be restricted on one side to prevent it from hitting a nearby wall. Its settings include controls for the touchpad's light ring, a ""Door Left Audio Alarm,"" and lock sound volume. The lock also reportedly supports automatic unlocking, tap to unlock, emergency power, and master codes, comes with two mechanical keys, and fits 2.375-inch (60.3 mm) and 2.75-inch (69.9 mm) brackets.

48,2026-10-07,"I have been a huge Sonos fan and customer for years, dating back to the original Sonos One. So, about two years ago, when I heard that Sonos was entering the over-ear headphone market, I knew I had to give it a try. The Sonos Ace looked great, was very comfortable, and brought some cool Sonos ecosystem features. But for a premium pair of headphones, there were still a few areas that left me wanting more, like strong bass, better ANC, and a better app experience. But now, the new Sonos Ace Ultra feels like they went down that list and addressed nearly every complaint that most customers had, including me. I have been using them as my main headphones for almost a month now, for travel, video editing, workouts, walks, and everyday listening. So I have a good grasp on them now, and here is what you need to know.
Be sure to check out our hands-on video review for the Sonos Ace Ultra below:
At first glance, the Sonos Ace Ultra does not look much different from the original Sonos Ace. They decided to keep the same clean, understated design with a few differently placed chrome accents and some additional colors. But the biggest differences are inside these headphones. 
Here are some of the biggest changes from the original Sonos Ace to the Ace Ultra:
The main things that I noticed in these changes are the redesigned acoustics, better bass response, and stronger ANC. With the original Sonos Ace, I would sometimes get this hissing noise when I was in ANC mode. This would rarely happen, but I did notice it. So far, the new Sonos Ace Ultra has alleviated that issue. You also still get some of the great specs from the previous model like:
They still have the same super high-quality design, combining plastics and metals to give you a premium, lightweight feel. The Sonos Ace Ultra also keeps the magnetically replaceable ear cups, and one of the best things that I have noticed is the new EQ settings that really let me hone in on how I like to listen to my music. But how were they as an everyday pair of headphones?
Again, it’s easy to put specs on paper, but the biggest reason I’ve enjoyed the Ace Ultras is that they actually sound noticeably better. My biggest complaint about the original Sonos Ace was the lack of bass depth. The bass was not bad, but for a pair of $450 headphones, I felt like they were lacking a bit. I listen to a lot of R&B, hip-hop, and pop music, so bass is important to me. I wanted more low-end impact and to feel the bass. The Ace Ultra finally delivers the experience that I wanted. 
There is substantially more punch and depth at the low end. But one thing to note here is that Sonos hasn’t achieved that by simply turning up the bass slider. Any headphone brand can crank up the bass, but the difficult part is maintaining separation so that the bass doesn’t overwhelm the vocals, instruments, and higher-end frequencies. That is where the Ace Ultra has really impressed me. The bass hits harder, but the vocals are still clear, crisp, and easy to listen to. And it’s not just for music; all media sounds better, like podcasts, movies, and all the YouTube videos I watch. They have sounded fantastic in all media listening situations. 
The improved ANC is something else I noticed immediately. The new 10 mic array allows even more sound to be dynamically pushed away from the headphones. Even when there is no media playing, it’s insane how quiet the headphones are in so many different environments. It’s almost too good! The biggest improvement to ANC is the unpredictable noises. It’s one thing to block out a consistent noise like an airplane engine, but another to block out passing cars, construction, sirens, or sudden loud sounds while walking outside.
Two final things I want to bring up are comfort and the Sonos ecosystem. These headphones are the most comfortable headphones I have worn. Even on a long 8-10-hour airplane ride, I wore them 95% of the time, and I felt no aches, pain, or heat at all. Lastly, I have a lot of Sonos speakers around my house, and they introduced a new feature that lets you move music from a speaker to your headphones with the press of a button. It’s a very cool feature, but I personally haven’t used it much.
My only complaint is that they need a new zipper or mechanism for the headphone case because that sticker gets stuck way too often!
The Sonos Ace Ultra is available today on Sonos’s site and Amazon for $449, keeping the same launch price as the original Sonos Ace. They come in four colors: Black, White, Agave, and Sand. I also noticed that the original Sonos Ace is available on Amazon for $239 while supplies last. At $240, now that could be a good choice as well.
At $450, the Sonos Ace Ultra is firmly in the premium noise-canceling headphone category alongside options from Apple, Bose, and Sony.
The original Sonos Ace felt like a good first-generation product. The Sonos Ace Ultra feels like a complete and finished product. As we saw, they did not reinvent the look or design, but they improved in all the areas that mattered and even added a few new cool features. The sound is noticeably better, the ANC is strong and smarter, battery life lasts longer, the app experience is smoother, and that Agave color is amazing.
If you already own the OG Sonos Ace, I actually think there is enough here to justify the upgrade, depending on what you value. If you are in the market for a premium set of headphones, I think the Sonos Ace Ultra needs to be on your shortlist. At $449, they are certainly not cheap, but if you want one of the best premium headphones and are a Sonos fan, then you will love these.
What do you think? Have you tried the Sonos Ace line at all? Are you a Sonos customer? What do you think of the app experience? Let’s discuss below!

49,2026-10-07,"Flu season is beginning, and it's a time that can be stressful for busy parents and families.
Influenza, more commonly known as the flu, can be more serious than the common cold and other illnesses, especially for kids with chronic conditions and those under 5 years old, the Centers for Disease and Prevention notes.
To help parents prepare, ABC News spoke with Dr. Jeanne Marrazzo, the chief executive officer of the Infectious Diseases Society of America, to get expert tips for parents to get ahead of the flu season.
The IDSA, as well as other physician groups, including the American Academy of Pediatrics, recommends everyone, including parents and kids over the age of 6 months, get a flu vaccine.
""The vast majority of really bad outcomes, the worst being death, but also including hospitalization for flu-related illness, occur in kids who are unvaccinated,"" Marrazzo said. ""When you look at the worst flu years, for example, 2025, 2024, they were not good for kids. We had between 150 and 180 deaths in kids, and over 90% of the kids who died, unfortunately, were unvaccinated.""
Young children between 6 months to 23 months old can receive an injection of the inactivated flu vaccine, while children between 2 to 8 years old have an option between the inactivated injection or an intranasal live attenuated flu vaccine -- a nasal spray.
""You can choose, in that young age period, between those two vaccines. That's nice because it helps avoid a shot in kids who are at that age, very concerned and very sensitive [to injections],"" Marrazzo said, adding that the exception is that children with a compromised immune system or conditions such as asthma should not receive the nasal spray flu vaccine.
For children 9 years and older, they can receive an inactivated flu vaccine shot, an intranasal vaccine, or a recombinant flu vaccine.
Parents should encourage and remind children to practice good hygiene, including washing hands regularly and covering coughs, to keep sickness at bay.
But when respiratory symptoms do develop, influenza may appear similar to other respiratory viruses so having common medications on hand can help support the recovery process.
Flu symptoms may include fever, chills, a cough, sore throat, runny or stuffy nose, muscle or body aches, headaches, fatigue or gastrointestinal issues, such as vomiting and diarrhea, according to the CDC.
""It's good to have on hand things like child acetaminophen or Tylenol, or even ibuprofen like Advil or Motrin, because you can treat those fevers directly,"" Marrazzo said. ""Otherwise, it's really supportive care. You want to make sure the kid is really well hydrated. Ideally, you'd like to have the child not be around other people in the household if you think it's the flu.""
Parents should always consult with and talk to their child's pediatrician or doctor about any concerns about their child's health, including before and when they get sick or if they develop any new symptoms.
""If you have a very young child, certainly less than a year old, you don't want to mess around because you want to make sure they're OK,"" Marrazzo said. ""[Other times to talk to your child's doctor are] if your child has serious medical conditions, asthma, cancer, or lung disease, if the baby or child is on dialysis. Those are some things that you want to pay special attention to.""

50,2026-10-07,"This as-told-to essay is based on a conversation with Bob Monahan, cofounder of UPPAbaby. It has been edited for length and clarity.
My youngest son, Denny, was born on the day that I launched my company. His birth was an induction, and I was there for it. Then, I hopped on a plane from my home in Massachusetts to a trade show in Florida.
The trade show itself was a funny story. I met the salesperson I'd been working with. Her cousin, who was just out of jail, was setting up the booth, making a mess of it. It wasn't even much of a booth, but people came by and liked our product.
That story embodies the early days of UPPAbaby. We had to be scrappy. I went from working successful jobs to living off PB&Js, while raising three kids under three. It was scary sometimes.
Before starting UPPAbaby, I worked for major brands including Ford and Reebok. After that, I worked for established companies in the baby space, designing products from baby gates and monitors to ride-on cars.
I noticed that European brands were considered the most high-end. But they weren't always user-friendly. American brands were typically lower cost and not that exciting.
I wanted to create a company that delivered a product that was high-end and easy to use. Dads like me were becoming more hands-on with baby gear, and I knew there was a market for a sleek, functional product that would appeal to moms, dads, and babies.
I wanted to be an entrepreneur for a lot of reasons. I felt that working for a company, I was pigeonholed. If I were an engineer or in sales, there would be little opportunity to cross over into product development or engage with other aspects of business operations.
As a business owner, I could do everything — and I had to. If I wasn't doing it, it wasn't getting done. That was an adjustment. In my previous roles, I had budgets of millions of dollars, which I could spend however I liked. There were a lot of resources.

With UPPAbaby, I could only do what we could pay for. Luckily, we were in the black pretty quickly. We moved forward with very intentional baby steps, surviving on hardly anything. But within a year and a half, I was able to fund the business and support my family pretty adequately.
The baby space is interesting, because your customer is always new. If you're selling watches or cars, you can rely on brand recognition. With strollers, no one really knows your brand until they need it. We're always marketing to those new customers who wake up suddenly in a world where they're having kids.
I love seeing people using the product. One time in the airport, a parent was having trouble folding up the stroller, and I showed them how to do it more easily. I knew exactly who had designed that button and all the people involved in making the stroller easy to use. Sharing that was pretty neat.
Today, the company — and my youngest son — are both 20. I've learned that things are always changing. The industry has had massive shifts at least three times since I've been in it. I've learned to embrace change and stay quick on our feet.
We grew without investment for a long time. That wasn't always easy. Sometimes it felt like we were just barely keeping it together. Eventually, I decided to bring in outside partners, who had not only money but expertise. My investors have helped me connect with resources that were too complicated for me to access on my own.
Today, we have a recognized brand. Still, I try to surprise and delight customers. We do that by being even better than they expect at serving their needs. If you serve the customers, the sales will follow, and if you surprise them in unique ways — like with stellar customer service — they'll tell their friends.

51,2026-10-07,"You're viewing the US website. Looking for the Site edition?
With 47% of Americans deleting a social app over stress, businesses need new ways to capture attention. Here are three strategies that work.
Copied to clipboard

		Opinions expressed by Entrepreneur contributors are their own.	
Americans are experiencing social media burnout this year, and it’s no surprise. A recent report showed that 47% of U.S. adults deleted a social media app due to stress.
The ones who are not going to extremes of deleting apps are more selective about how they use them. About 55% of people post less often now than they did five years ago, and 51% say they feel like maintaining an online presence is too much work.
I’ve spent decades adapting to changing marketing channels. I started my own business, PostcardMania, in 1998, and this year we’re on track to break $120 million in annual revenue — and I’ve learned that when consumers change where they spend their attention, businesses have to change where they spend their marketing dollars.
As social audiences and impressions shrink, brands need to reassess their outreach strategies. There are other, even better ways to market to consumers.
I’m not telling you to ditch these platforms altogether, but it may be time to tweak your marketing plan.
Here’s exactly what you can do to grow your business despite increasing social media burnout.
Social media advertising costs have increased dramatically. Meta’s price per ad increased 9% last year and is still up 12% so far for 2026. As prices increase, it becomes harder and harder to justify just throwing money at Meta ads.
The smartest businesses are proactively looking for ways to create value out of their social media presence rather than just riding the pricing rollercoaster. The ones succeeding at this are creating organic content people actually want to consume.
Think cooking brands that don’t just share recipes but videos of how to prepare each step along the way, home-improvement companies showing satisfying before-and-after projects, or fitness professionals demonstrating good and bad form or great stretching techniques.
Give people a reason to stop scrolling.
We did this by changing our social media ads from static images to videos of real customer stories. We based these videos on the 1,210 documented case studies that showcase interviews with our clients talking about their success with direct mail. As a result, our social media leads increased by 105%!
Your social media presence should demonstrate your expertise, solve a problem or genuinely entertain. If it doesn’t do one of those things, you probably don’t need to post it. The real goal is to stop the scroll and bridge the gap between the platform and your business. You want to get them off social media and straight to your website or Googling you.
If you invest in social media ads, make sure it has a call to action that gets them to sign up, to buy the product, to call you. If it doesn’t, it’s not worth your marketing dollars.
If consumers are intentionally spending less time on social media, you have to meet them somewhere else. You have a lot of options: out-of-home advertising, direct mail, TV (traditional and streaming), radio, podcasts, print and in-person events are just a few.
Traditional advertising methods are reliable and still deliver a high return on investment. For my business, direct mail not only generates the most revenue per lead out of all of our marketing channels, but its returns are growing much faster than digital.
Our revenue generated per direct mail lead grew from $233.35 in 2023 to $416.04 in 2025. During that same period, revenue per digital lead actually declined slightly, from $38.96 to $38.59. That’s a dramatic difference in the value of the leads we’re generating. It’s why I mail 260,000 postcards a week to potential clients and customers and continually increase this output as we grow.
And there’s a reason direct mail makes sense in an era of digital fatigue. Compared to social media, direct mail has:
Direct mail isn’t the only offline opportunity for marketing. In-person events and outdoor advertising can also give your audience a break from digital content.
The point isn’t to pick one magical marketing channel. It’s to test, track and improve.
Try a marketing tactic that isn’t digital. Then track your results, figure out which channels actually produce revenue for your business, and invest more in the ones that work. If it’s a poster on a bench in a popular public park, keep making them! If it’s postcards, keep mailing. Do what works for you.
Another problem with relying too heavily on social media: You don’t own the audience; the platform does.
Your followers can disappear in a single algorithm change.
That’s why it’s important to turn the attention you’re getting online into something you own: a database of customers and prospects. But simply putting a newsletter sign-up on your website isn’t enough. First, you have to give people a reason to visit your website — and then give them a compelling reason to share their contact information once they get there.
For example, promote a free guide or checklist through your chosen marketing channels that funnels those prospects to a specific landing page that only promotes one thing — that guide or checklist. Make sure it’s easy for visitors to land on that page and claim your offer.
Once someone has converted, you can continue the relationship with useful email content, special offers and other relevant communications. You can also build a referral program that gives happy customers a reason to bring someone new through your door.
Americans are experiencing social media burnout this year, and it’s no surprise. A recent report showed that 47% of U.S. adults deleted a social media app due to stress.
The ones who are not going to extremes of deleting apps are more selective about how they use them. About 55% of people post less often now than they did five years ago, and 51% say they feel like maintaining an online presence is too much work.
I’ve spent decades adapting to changing marketing channels. I started my own business, PostcardMania, in 1998, and this year we’re on track to break $120 million in annual revenue — and I’ve learned that when consumers change where they spend their attention, businesses have to change where they spend their marketing dollars.

55,2026-10-07,"Ever since the Anglepoise lamp's introduction in 1933, desk lamps typically cantilevered the light source outwards from the base.

Roughly four decades later, Ettore Sottsass threw this convention out with his irreverent Valigia desk lamp. 

There is no counterweighted base, just four legs that support the lamp head as if it's a tabletop. The form demands commitment, sitting directly above whatever you're reading.
 
Sottsass added a handle at the top, envisioning this as an object you'd carry from place to place. (""Valigia"" is Italian for ""suitcase."")

 
 
It's worth noting that Sottsass designed this in 1977, foreshadowing the Memphis movement by a few years. Here in the 2020s, original manufacturer Stilnovo has brought it back onto the market. But I think they missed a trick: While they've changed the lighting to LED, they failed to add a rechargeable battery as the power source. Had they done that and removed the cord, the design would've been closer to what Sottsass would've wanted. 

 

This “lamp” aberration demonstrates that an industrial design does not become worthy just because is relatively old.

And if try to justify things, like ""well, I like that brand, it is cute and comfortable, and I had the money and I have waited so much for this moment"" or whatever, she acts like I am naive, I don't know how to use money, I am acting like a fool, like if I was a little girl that knows nothing.
I don't know what to do with her! I am now just sharing the bare minimum. I don't think that talking her would help; she'll just say that she was trying to help me but I don't need her help, at all. I am better that her managing money in general, but for lot of things I prefer quality over quantity, but she doesn't understand that because she thinks that ""everything is the same"", and my philosophy is that I rather have one Uniqlo tshirt than 5 from shein. But I don't want to open that box, I don't want to have a conversation like ""I earn more than you, so I can choose more brands, and I like that expensive things that you CANNOT AFFORD"". She acts like her family and mine make the same money, and it is madness to spend more that her, and we are actually earning double, and she knows (she doesn't know the exact numbers, but my husband is a doctor so it is easy to guess the difference)
Nobody wants to feel judged by their friends. We all lead our lives differently, and that is okay. We don't all need to follow the same set of guiding principles. It would be boring if we did. It's important to accept our friends as they are and not constantly try and change them. You're not in control of their life, and nor should you be. You should be able to live your life free of judgement. If you can't do that with your friends, then it might be time for new friends. That's what this pair was struggling with.
