57,2026-10-07,"
					By Max Goldbart 
International TV Co-Editor

Welcome to our selection of the most scorching titles headed to MIPCOM this year, The Deadline Dozen. Our editorial team has carefully researched and selected the projects we expect to drive the chatter on the Croisette and beyond.

We start with unscripted. Check back tomorrow for the best of MIPCOM’s scripted fare.

HIDDEN PLANET

	BBC STUDIOS

Length: 5 x 60’





	
	
		
					Related Stories		
	













	News
	



	
		
					Iconic Japanese Gameshow 'Action Arena' To Be Shopped Internationally For First Time By NBCUniversal Formats		
			















	News
	



	
		
					Scripted Veteran Jason Simms Switches On Fusebox Entertainment		
			







Producers: BBC Studios Natural History Unit, The Open University




			Watch on Deadline		






Networks: BBC (U.K.), BBC America (U.S.)

	The BBC’s natural history output has set the global benchmark even with many more players in the field today than a decade ago. Hidden Planet is the latest in the canon, this time turning the micro-cameras on the world of insects.

	










	The five-part series shines a spotlight on insect societies, highlighting how ants, beetles and other bugs play the roles of parents, builders and sophisticated communicators as they strike alliances with larger animals and perform incredible feats of strength and intelligence.

	The BBC has equipped the BBC Studios Natural History Unit with a mountain of the latest scientific work and top-of-the-line cameras to aid the filming. In this case, the scientific theory has been combined with bespoke robotic systems and specially designed lenses to get to the heart of insect life. Worryingly, the series will show how their communities are increasingly under threat from humans.

	With BBC America already on board as a co-producer and backed by research from The Open University, BBC Studios hits Cannes with the closest thing to a sure-fire documentary seller on the Croisette this year.

THE BILLION DOLLAR HASH HUNT

	BLUE ANT RIGHTS

Length: 3 x 60’

Producer: Alibi Entertainment

Network:Crave (Canada)

	Since completing a reverse takeover of Canadian compatriot Boat Rocker just over a year ago, Blue Ant Rights has spent time refining its sales and streaming businesses and is hoping the corporate reshuffling leaves it with a stronger distribution operation than before. MIPCOM will be a good test.

	










	Among the highlights of its slate is The Billion Dollar Hash Hunt, the latest doc series from Canada’s Crave, the red-hot streamer behind Heated Rivalry. This one is billed as a “true-crime treasure hunt” that chronicles the story of former smuggler Billy Greer, who moved thousands of tons of hash for shadowy Dutch crime network The Octopus.

	Greer’s caper came to an end when 54 tons of drugs were seized and DEA agent Rick Carter sent him down for 20 years. It’s a familiar story until the plot takes a wild twist: Years later, Greer and Carter join forces to uncover what happened to the missing drugs.

	This opens the story up to startling confessional interactions, remarkable archive footage and a high-stakes present day search.

	With most docs in the true-crime space focused on building a picture of the past, Blue Ant will hope bringing the narrative into the modern day gives it the edge with discerning doc buyers who need their shows to cut through. Given it was greenlit by a streamer, this one looks set to work on digital platforms as well as linear TV.

MARRIAGE MARKET

	Fox Entertainment Global

Length: TBC

Producer: Fox Entertainment Studios

Network: Fox (U.S.), Joyn (Germany)

The Marriage Market is coming to market. A new reality dating format, the show is for U.S. network Fox, and the finished version and the format are launching internationally at MIPCOM.

	The Fox show is fronted by actress-comedian-podcaster Whitney Cummings. As well as a stand-up, she is the co-creator of CBS show 2 Broke Girls and creator of NBC’s Whitney. As a host, she fronted HBO’s Friends gameshow, Fast Friends.

	Billed as an immersive matchmaking experience and a new take on the dating show, the format features singletons who are tired of swiping and first dates and are ready to get hitched. The twist is, the would-be brides and grooms must hand total control of their love lives to their families.

	When two families think they have the perfect match, the couple are introduced and get engaged on the spot. Then the new pair and their families move in together and the matchmaking is put to the test.

	The families are still in charge and must decide if they have found them the one, or whether it has not worked out and their loved one needs to be pitched back into the marriage market, giving them another shot at finding the love of their lives.

	The show has an international pedigree. It is borne out of a formats initiative that saw Fox work with international broadcasters such as ProSiebenSat.1 in Germany and TF1 in France to jointly work up new unscripted shows. Accordingly, The Marriage Market bowed in Germany first, on ProSieben’s streaming platform Joyn. Cheerio Entertainment, part of the German broadcaster’s production and distribution division, made the German version.

	Fox Entertainment Studios makes the U.S. version, which is slated for Fox’s 2026-2027 season. Alycia Rossiter, who worked on The Bachelor and The Bachelorette, is the showrunner. Fox Entertainment Global is handling distribution and will be looking for love—from buyers—in Cannes.

RUINS: RISE & FALL

	BOSSANOVA MEDIA

Length:8 x 60’

Producer: Like A Shot Entertainment

Networks: Warner Bros. Discovery (EMEA), SBS (Australia), Channel 4 (U.K.), TV2 (Norway), Sky New Zealand

	Every year for a decade, London-based BossaNova Media has held a pitching event dubbed Development Day, which brings together its biggest producers and clients. The idea is to fast-track greenlights and get factual projects out the door at a quicker pace. Paul Heaney’s company itself acts as the central commissioner, putting projects into production and then sells the rights to buyers who have already stated their interest.

	










Ruins: Rise & Fall is one such series. The eight-part show was greenlit in April, with Heaney saying at the time, “Some projects you invest in are a bit of a risk, but Ruins: Rise & Fall isn’t one of them.” The reason for that is the unquenching market need for solid factual projects that can both be popular on linear channels and drive older viewers to streaming services.

	The series explores 24 sites around the world and is built on one idea: every ruin has two stories—how it rose and how it fell. Viewers are taken to ancient Sri Lankan rock fortress Sigiriya, the ruins of Ephesus in Turkey and Roman auxiliary fort Vindolanda in the U.K. and on to more modern places such as Hitler’s Wolf’s Lair in Poland and vast underground Nazi war factories.

	Archaeologists and historians tell their stories from the ambition and ingenuity that made them, to the conflict and catastrophe that transformed them into the ruins of today. Digital reconstruction will show sites being stripped back to before the first stone was laid to seeing them rebuilt and then torn apart through war, disaster, abandonment and decay.

	Given that several of the largest global territories are already off the market is a testament to BossaNova’s strategic foresight, so those buyers in the remaining territories should move fast.

100 COOKS

	Warner Bros. International Television Production

Length: 9 x 60’

Producer: Lando Entertainment

Network: Food Network (U.S)

	“Your number could come up at any moment.” That is the logline that greets chefs daring to take on Warner’s 100 Cooks, which found its perfect American host in the shape of Terry Crews. A Food Network original, it is now being served up internationally.

	There may be a few out there now, but 100 Cooks feels one of the highest-stakes (or should that be steaks?) on offer.

	The 100 contestants enter the vast kitchen arena ready for a chance to call the shots. A randomized Challenge Mixer reveals the next test: From weeknight meals and party bites to holiday sweets or the best burger. The Cook Mixer then selects which competitors will step forward and take on the challenge.

	Nobody knows when they will compete, what they will be asked to do, how many cooks will join them, or how many could be sent home. Everyone must be ready for anything, and as 100 becomes 50, the kitchen slowly empties and the competition intensifies until one cook eventually walks away victorious. In the case of the U.S. version, it was Bryce, an oil field worker from Montana.

100 Cooks was a big hit for Food Network and it’s no wonder Warner Bros. International Television Production is prioritizing this one in Cannes. Brooklyn Nine-Nine star Crews is a hugely recognizable name who brings a unique feel to the show, and so 100 Cooks could easily work as finished tape or be shopped as a format, for which territories would then seek a host who can similarly make a big noise. At MIPCOM, Warner is also selling TLC UK format Unacceptable, New Zealand’s Crackhead and the Buried with Michael Sheen doc about the Good Omens star’s investigation into toxic waste.

ALL HEART: A RUGBY STORY

	BOAT ROCKER STUDIOS

Length: 4 x 40’

Producers: Boat Rocker Studios

Network: Crave (Canada)

	The second Crave documentary in our Dozen, this four-parter has been selected to satisfy the hunger for sports access docs. The docuseries’ full working title is All Heart: Canada Women’s Rugby and the Quest for Rugby World Cup Glory. It was ordered last year and will stream on Bell Media’s Crave this year.

	










	The series follows the Canadian team’s historic run to the World Cup final, where they were ultimately toppled by host country England, going down 33-13 in front of a sellout crowd of 82,000 at Twickenham Stadium in South West London.

	It goes behind the scenes, showcasing the culmination of years of hard work and sacrifice, with Rugby Canada and World Rugby providing director Kathleen Jayme behind-the-scenes access to the players and coaching staff. James is known for making 2025’s Naomi Osaka: The Second Set, a tennis access doc that was sold to Fox Corp.-owned streamer Tubi for the U.S. market.

	Exec producers on All Heart include Jay Baruchel—the Canadian actor and filmmaker with credits including Knocked Up, How to Train Your Dragon and She’s Out of My League—and rock band The Tragically Hip, who are long-time supporters of women’s sports and fundraisers for the Canadian women’s rugby team.  Boat Rocker founders David Fortier and Ivan Schneeberg are also EPs.

	The title is only just hitting the market now despite its 2025 order, and takes a key spot in Boat Rocker Studios’ slate this year.

WORDLE

	NBCUniversal Formats, a division of Universal Studio Group

Length: TBC

Producers: Universal Television Alternative Studio, Electric Hot Dog, The New York Times

Network: NBC (U.S.)

	The New York Times daily word puzzle game Wordle is coming to U.S. TV, and the format is coming to the international market.

	The game is a phenomenon. Developed by Josh Wardle, a Welsh software engineer at Reddit, players have to find a five-letter word. They have six attempts, with colored tiles offering feedback on the correct and redundant letters, and their placement in that day’s word.

	The New York Times paid a reported $3 million to buy Wordle and it has become a cornerstone of its digital and games offering. The game has racked up billions of users. Getting a word with a small number of guesses is a source of pride for players.

	The Wordle TV game show will bow on U.S. broadcast network NBC. Broadcaster, journalist, Today anchor and avid Wordle player Savannah Guthrie is set to host. She has form with the game, previously playing a huge version of the game in Times Square, New York.

	Universal Television Alternative Studio is producing with Jimmy Fallon’s Electric Hot Dog and The New York Times. Fallon, who has increasingly moved into producing, is an exec-producer on the show. He is a Wordle fan, even having a segment on The Tonight Show Starring Jimmy Fallon in which he and the team played the game live on air.

	The TV game show has been a few years in the making. It will take place over three super-sized rounds with two teams solving the Wordle puzzles in each half-hour episode. NYT execs have promised the show will stay true to the spirit of the game.

	The Wordle game show is on trend with several TV adaptations of games in the cards. There are TV versions of Monopoly, Cluedo and The Settlers of Catan being developed. Historically, the likes of Scrabble, Boggle, Trivial Pursuit and Pictionary have all been adapted for TV.

	In terms of Wordle, NBCUniversal Formats, a division of Universal Studio Group, is across distribution and selling the format.

CAPTCHA

	SEVEN.ONE STUDIOS INTERNATIONAL

Length: 10 x 50’

Producer: Thinketers

Network: LA 2 (Spain)

	Spain is known in the unscripted world for the likes of Operación Triunfo and El Conquistador, but it has primarily been an importer of formats, rather than an exporter.

	










	Germany’s Seven.One Studios International is hoping to add another name to the canon with Captcha. The game show is based around those ubiquitous internet verification tests that check if the visitor to a website is human or a bot.

	The format tests the statement ‘I’m not a robot’ by putting four contestants through a series of visual puzzles, logic games and AI-powered, web-inspired challenges to prove their human credentials.

	Celebrity guests accompany the contestants, ideally helping and not hindering them, to an all-or-nothing final, where one test decides everything, including the true identity of the player.

	A final verification proves if they are human or not, and if they keep the cash that they have been accruing.

	The format has been already produced for Spanish broadcaster La 2, where it was a success across its first season.

	Seven.One Studios International, now part of the expanding MediaForEurope empire, will be looking to captcha interest with the show in a cluttered market for attention- grabbing formats.

HISTORY’S STRANGE FORTUNES WITH KEVIN BACON

	A+E GLOBAL MEDIA

Length: 10 x 60’

Producers: A+E Factual Studios, VaynerWatt

Network: The History Channel (U.S.)

	History is filled with stories of the strange ways people won and lost their fortunes. In A+E Global Media’s latest talent-led unscripted series, the aim is to tell as many of those stories as possible.

History’s Strange Fortunes with Kevin Bacon will see the Footloose, X: Men First Class and Crazy, Stupid, Love actor examine wild tales such as Elvis dropping $50,000 on a midnight sandwich one evening to million-dollar scams and gold rush windfalls. Expect greed, luck, obsession and the old adage that fortune really does favor the brave (and the strange, according to A+E).

	The talent-led model has dominated commissioning at The History Channel for some time, with numerous slates pairing well-known faces with lesser-known cases. Indeed, Bacon is just one of four star-fronted series in a March 2026 slate—the others being Extraordinary Origins with Ted Danson, Crime and Justice with Tom Selleck and History’s Greatest Machines with Dolph Lundgren.

	“Talent-led formats succeed on History because they unite inimitable personalities with amazing storytelling,” says Patrick Vien, Group MD for International at A+E Global Media. “Kevin Bacon’s global fan base paired with our universal fascination with money gives History’s Strange Fortunes broad international appeal.”

	At MIPCOM this year, both Bacon and Danson’s shows will take center stage for A+E, alongside the likes of Lifetime movie Buried in Barstow and miniseries The Bible Revealed.

TERRAIN: BATTLE OF THE FLAGS

	PRIMITIVES

Length: 10 x 25’

Producer: Roses Are Blue

Network: ZDF (Germany), YouTube

	Germany has been the progenitor of some of Europe’s most creative entertainment formats and release windows in recent years with shows such as 7 Vs. Wild and Terrain. Battle of the Flags is now hoping to be its latest success story.

	The 10-part, half-hour format is the brainchild of German actor, comedian and YouTuber Torge Oerlich, known to his followers as Freshtorge. He began developing the adventure-reality show, which aims to generate an immersive gameplay experience, with Belgian format maker Roses Are Blue. German pubcaster ZDF quickly boarded the project.

	Inspired by gaming mechanics, Terrain follows ten high-profile creators who divide into five teams and, taking a cue from The Traitors, compete in a purpose-built game environment set against a dramatic Scottish Highlands backdrop. Their mission is simply to capture the other team’s flag and defend their own. Unlike The Traitors, the gameplay does not compel them to form alliances, as the teams are fixed.

	










	The teams self-shoot their experience and wear specially developed third-person camera systems integrated into a bespoke vest to give the aesthetic a distinct look and feel of video games.

	At 25 minutes per episode, the show is designed for streaming services, and it has been given an innovative rollout in Germany this fall, when three episodes will launch on ZDF’s streaming service, with Episode 1 also available on YouTube. The following two episodes join the Google-owned service a day later, and the linear premiere on ZDFneo will follow later. Dedicated companion content will run on a standalone YouTube channel and ‘reaction programming’ will hit ZDF’s Twitch channel.

	Belgium-based Primitives, has worldwide rights (excluding German-speaking Europe) and will present the show to buyers in the run-up to MIPCOM, with a full market launch also prepared.

	Having the creators film the action themselves creates a level of authenticity and immediacy that is particularly exciting for younger audiences.

DIANA UNHEARD

	Cineflix Rights

Length: 3 x 60’

Producer: Love Monday TV

Network: TBC

	Having gained access to the audio tapes that formed the basis of Andrew Morton’s explosive book about the Princess Diana, Love Monday TV has made Diana Unheard, a documentary series that will air never-before-heard parts of the recordings.

	The producers hope that hearing Diana talk about her life, loves and family will reframe people’s perception of her, moving away from seeing through a lens of vulnerability and tragedy and showing her as self-aware, determined, and actively taking control of her life.

	Love Monday has made numerous shows about the British monarchy including Her Majesty the Queen: Behind Closed Doors for ITV and William and Harry: Love and Betrayal for Channel 5.

	Diana can be heard in revealing form in the tapes, which were conversations recorded with her friend James Colthurst in 1991. She talks about Prince Charles and Camilla (now King Charles III and Queen Camilla), as well as the then Prince Andrew, who has since been stripped of his royal titles following scrutiny of his ties to Jeffrey Epstein.

	As well as hearing the audio of the Princess, the three-part doc series explains how and when they were recorded, and the extraordinary lengths Diana went to in order to ensure her story could be heard. For example, she devised a daring plan to have the tapes secretly smuggled out of Kensington Palace.

	Colthurst and Morton appear in the series, as does Michael O’Mara, publisher of Morton’s book, Diana: Her True Story.

	The recordings and Morton’s tome had a huge impact on the Royal Family and the whole British establishment. The timing of the new doc is appropriate, with 2027 the 30th anniversary of Diana’s death.

	Cineflix Rights moved for the sales rights and will bring the doc to market at MIPCOM. Stories about the British Royal Family have enduring international appeal. Interest is heightened when royal stories cover Princess Diana, who was one of the most famous women in the world, with people fascinated by her life.

THE CHASE AROUND THE WORLD

	ITV Studios

Length: 6 x 60’

Producer: Bright Entertainment

Network: ITV (U.K.)

	Fancy joining the “smartest road trip in the world”?

	There’s no better way to give a stalwart format a bit of global appeal than by sending its icons and contestants on a journey around the globe. The Chase Around the World does just that, taking a tried-and-tested, colossally successful format and an additional bit of jeopardy, with firm potential for international sales.

	










	Much like splashy premium factual shows such as Race Across the World or Destination X, the show sees contestants travel across the globe, this time in a race to find all six Chasers in secret international locations, holding onto the show’s trademark general knowledge challenges but adding an adventure element.

	In the U.K. version, where the format originated, contestants played for £100,000 ($133,000), although this figure is not fixed for the international remakes.

	The six pairs went around Rome, haring after two iconic Chasers, the Beast and the Governess, as they journeyed towards Bradley Walsh at the finish line. The route took them through some of the Italian capital’s iconic landmarks as they answered questions that, if incorrect, could lead to serious consequences.

The Chase Around the World rated well in the U.K. and is just the latest in a line of entertainment shows that have used travel as a bedrock. It both showcased the spin-off opportunities that such a strong format can open up, and also carries potential to run and run, given that the world is The Chase’s oyster. Local buyers will be licking their lips.

58,2026-10-07,"Gate is the latest platform to bet that ""everything"" exchanges are the future model for crypto companies, with CEO and founder Lin Han predicting that all-one-money apps will become one of the biggest consumer trends in the coming year.
The Cayman Islands-headquartered firm, which has $2 billion 24-hour trading volume and over 60 million users, is entering a widening race to turn trading accounts into broader consumer-finance apps. Coinbase expanded into investing, derivatives and banking, while Bybit is building a European super app. Robinhood’s U.K. offering already combines crypto with stocks, ISAs, options and futures. Binance announced its bet on becoming a crypto super app in July.
The opportunity is to make crypto one feature of an everyday money account, rather than an activity reserved for traders. 
Gate now allows users to hold and convert cash, cryptic currencies, stocks, gold and other assets in one account, then spend from those holdings through a card, Han told CoinDesk in an interview prior to unveiling its new app ""Gate Money"" in Singapore.
“We don’t want to stay with a small group of people who are professional at trading,” Han said. “We want to provide this product to a wider range of customers.” The Gate founder said he believes it’s time for all crypto firms to bring in non-crypto users. That’s where the biggest potential for growth is, he added.
He added that said the company intends to allow users to convert between holdings at any time, including outside normal stock-market hours, but did not explain how such conversions would be priced or executed when underlying markets are closed.
Gate’s most ambitious claim is that users will be able to spend assets without first holding cash in their accounts. “You can buy a cup of coffee with Nvidia stock,” Han said. “You cannot do that with any app in the world right now.”
He added that the biggest market for this service may be Asia, where people want access to U.S. stocks but can’t access them because they have restricted access to international brokerage accounts.
Han said Gate sees money apps such as Revolut, Cash App and Wise as more immediate competitors than traditional banks, though he also sees competition with other exchanges pursuing similar products. He said crypto firms now have the technology, licensing and banking relationships needed to offer services that once sat largely with banks and brokers.
As stablecoins move into regulated finance, APAC is becoming a key proving ground. This report maps the region’s rules, use cases, and RLUSD’s role.

60,2026-10-07,"Alibaba’s chairman told a Turin audience that Europe has the talent to compete. His answer for AI sovereignty is open models, trained on European data and run on European infrastructure.
Alibaba co-founder and chairman Joe Tsai speaking at Wave by Vento in Turin
Alibaba co-founder and chairman Joe Tsai says open-source AI is the only way for Europe to become independent in the technology. He spoke to founders and investors on Wednesday at Wave by Vento in Turin, in a session titled “The Other Side of the Story”.
“Open source is the opportunity for Europe… That’s the approach Europe can take to truly be independent. We talk about technology independence, we talk about AI sovereignty in Europe. I think the open source approach is the only way to go,” Tsai said.
James Anderson, managing partner and chief investment officer of Lingotto Innovation, led the interview. Exor owns Lingotto. Anderson spent more than three decades at Baillie Gifford, where he ran the Scottish Mortgage investment trust.
Tsai said Alibaba, founded in 1999, grew very big and lost focus over its first two decades. In 2023 it narrowed itself to two businesses: e-commerce and what he called “full-stack” AI. That runs from computing infrastructure to models to applications.
He said Alibaba uses about $25bn a year of free cash flow from e-commerce to fund the AI push. It has doubled its capital spending on computing infrastructure every year for the past three years, he said. In August, TNW reported that Alibaba’s AI spending had cut its quarterly profit by 75%.
By Tsai’s estimate, the US hyperscalers together will invest around $1tn this year, a scale China does not match. He said AI is already very good at saving people time. He put the market for knowledge-worker productivity at about $50tn.
Anderson asked whether limits on China, including US curbs on chip supplies, had pushed its companies to innovate. Tsai said they had, and compared it to growing up without rich parents.
“If you are scraping all along, that drives you,” he said.
He said Chinese companies open-source their work on model design and inference efficiency, and still publish papers. The closed US labs no longer publish, he said, because they do not want to share.
Tsai said China accounts for about 30% of global industrial production, and its factories hold valuable data for training models. Its complete supply chains for electric vehicles, batteries and robots give it a further edge, he added.
Asked how Europe can compete with the US and China, Tsai said the talent is already there. Europeans make up 20% to 30% of the staff at some US frontier labs, he said.
“It is not a talent problem,” Tsai said.
Instead, he said, Europe should look at demand. Its industrial companies, including those around Turin, hold factory data they would not want to send to a closed model. They could take open models, train them further on that data and run them themselves.
No country should fully trust another country’s technology, Tsai said. A change of government or circumstances could see it switched off. That applies to American and Chinese technology alike, he said, so having several choices matters. In September, the ECB’s Christine Lagarde warned that Europe risks being cut off without its own AI.
Tsai compared open-weight models to a recipe that anyone can take and improve. A company can run one in its own data centre and train it on its own data. It then no longer depends on the original developer, he said. It also avoids paying for access to closed models. On Tuesday, Paris-based Mistral released its Large 4 model, which has open weights.
The missing piece, Tsai said, is computing power. Europe needs to “get serious” about building data centres, he said, to train and run models on its own infrastructure. In June, Alibaba Cloud opened data centres in France.
Anderson said part of the problem lies with his own industry. He recalled Spotify co-founder Daniel Ek telling him that Europe’s real trouble starts when it misses a second generation of technology. Europe has no companies that spend their cash flows the way US hyperscalers, Tencent or ByteDance do, Anderson said. Finance is not filling that gap.
Asked whether they would have the same conversation in five years, Tsai said AI would by then be part of every business, the way the internet is today.
“We’re not going to say AI anymore,” he said.

For many, a stubborn booger can be hard to ignore and getting it out might even make breathing easier. But according to a new video from the YouTube channel Institute of Human Anatomy, the way people go about it can turn a minor annoyance into a dangerous nosebleed.

In the video, uploaded last Sunday (October 4), the show’s host, Jonathan, examined an actual human nasal cavity to show what a finger can reach and why one particular spot is so easy to injure.
“Hopefully, you’re not going two knuckles deep into your nasal cavity looking for buried treasure,” he joked.

Cookie Studio/Adobe Stock (not the actual photo)
Most people think of the nose as the part they can see in the mirror. Jonathan started there, then showed the much larger cavity behind it.
He identified the nasal septum, the wall dividing that cavity into left and right sides. He then showed the curved structures called nasal conchae, or turbinates, that project into the space. Their shape gives incoming air more surface to pass over.
Jonathan explained that the extra surface helps prepare air for the lungs. Mucus adds moisture, while the vessels beneath the tissue help warm it. The septum also has a rich supply of vessels, some of which lie close to its delicate surface.

kei907/Adobe Stock (not the actual photo)
The host pointed to a spot near the front of the septum called Kiesselbach’s area, or Little’s area. Several arterial branches meet there, with blood supplied through branches of both the internal and external carotid arteries.
It is also close to where a fingernail lands when someone picks their nose.
“If you start to scratch that mucosal layer hard enough, you can rupture some of those blood vessels,” Jonathan explained.

Sebastian Kaulitzki/Adobe Stock (not the actual photo)


He said that usually caused an anterior nosebleed, which starts near the front of the nasal cavity. Dry air, irritation and forceful nose blowing can affect the same area. 
The amount of blood may look frightening, but these bleeds are usually manageable. Most nosebleeds originate toward the front of the nose, often around Kiesselbach’s area.

Nadzeya/Adobe Stock (not the actual photo)
Jonathan then turned to posterior nosebleeds, which begin deeper in the nasal cavity. He said ordinary nose picking was unlikely to cause one: most fingers simply cannot reach that far.
These bleeds are less common, but their location makes them more difficult to control. Blood can travel backward into the throat, and pinching the front of the nose may not put pressure on the source.

Mdv Edwards/Adobe Stock (not the actual photo)
Jonathan recalled a patient whose bleeding appeared to start spontaneously and would not stop. The person’s systolic blood pressure was around 170 and they had a history of hypertension, but were not taking their medication.
He did not claim that the elevated pressure caused that particular bleed. He said it was a concern in the case, especially because uncontrolled blood pressure can make a nosebleed harder to stop.
The host then discussed older age and medications that interfere with clotting as factors that could complicate a bleed. These include warfarin, apixaban, aspirin and clopidogrel.

Andrii Lysenko/Adobe Stock (not the actual photo)
If the nose started bleeding, Jonathan recommended a gentle blow to clear some blood and clots. He then advised sitting upright, leaning slightly forward and pinching the soft part of the nose, just above the nostrils.
The pressure should be held continuously for 10 to 15 minutes. Letting go every few seconds to check gives the vessel less time to seal. Tilting the head back is a mistake, he explained, because blood can run into the throat.


The UK’s National Health Service (NHS) guidance likewise recommends leaning forward and maintaining pressure on the soft part of the nose for 10 to 15 minutes.
For a more stubborn bleed, Jonathan mentioned a couple of sprays of oxymetazoline, sold as Afrin, after the initial gentle blow and before applying pressure. He also said soft material in the nostril could add pressure. The aim was to compress the bleeding tissue rather than merely soak up blood.

maroke/Adobe Stock (not the actual photo)
He advised seeking medical attention if the bleeding was heavy or would not stop, or if the person felt weak or lightheaded. A significant amount of blood running down the throat was another warning sign.
A posterior bleed, on the other hand, might require specialized packing or a balloon device to reach the vessel.

Syda Productions/Adobe Stock (not the actual photo)
“Is it useful to pick one’s nose?” the video asked. “Sometimes,” Jonathan said.
He explained that mucus traps dust, debris and microorganisms before they travel deeper into the respiratory system. Tiny hairlike structures called cilia normally move that mucus toward the back of the nose, where it can be swallowed. Near the nostrils, exposure to air can dry some of it into a booger.

If a large piece irritates the tissue or partly blocked airflow, removing it could help. “There’s nothing inherently wrong with removing it,” Jonathan said.
The trouble begins when someone repeatedly scrapes at the inside of the nose with a fingernail and ends up damaging the Kiesselbach’s area.

EXCLUSIVE 👀First look at LG’s upcoming smart home lineup:LG Video Doorbell WiredLG Floodlight CamLG Outdoor Cam Plug-InLG Indoor Cam Plug-In pic.twitter.com/vblGECQLM1

66,2026-10-07,"
In the early 1950s, Chicago-based industrial design firm Painter, Teague and Petertil won an interesting commission: Design the world's first portable transistor radio. Texas Instruments had worked out the miniaturization, then partnered with electronics brand Regency to create a consumer product. All they needed was an ID firm to give their technology a consumer-friendly form.
The design team got to work, and the Regency TR-1 hit the market in 1954. 
 
This was a game-changing device, a predecessor to the Walkman or the iPod. Up until then, families owned a single radio that they'd gather around, like it was a hearth. But now a teenager could take this tiny radio into their room and listen to whatever stations they wanted, or take their music on the go.



 
Regency sold at least 150,000 of these, and competitors wanted in. Raytheon released their 8TP in 1955. It was sleeker in appearance, but considerably larger than the TR-1.

 
In 1957, a Japanese upstart called Sony released their TR-63.

 
In 1960, Zenith released their Royal 50 model.
 
 
While each of these objects has their own aesthetic, they all clearly belong in the same class. It took an ultra-talented industrial designer, Richard Sapper, to produce a true design classic. Here's his Telefunken Match, which came out in 1962:
 
The preceding models look like plastic toys. Sapper's leather-and-aluminum form looks like a well-crafted precision object.






You can find surviving examples of all of these on secondhand sites. Which one would you rather own? 

Google search terms: when did halogen lamps require a wire to prevent the hot bulb from touching nearby objects AI overview begins: In August 1997, the U.S. Consumer Product Safety Commission (CPSC) and the halogen lamp industry officially initiated a massive recall requiring wire guards for roughly 40 million halogen torchiere floor lamps. [1, 2] The Safety Issue Halogen torchiere floor lamps—popular in the 1980s and 1990s—featured an upward-facing, shallow bowl design with high-wattage tubular bulbs. Because halogen bulbs must operate at temperatures exceeding 482°F (250°C) and frequently peak over 900°F (482°C) , they posed a severe fire hazard. If the tall lamps were knocked over or came into contact with flammable materials like curtains, clothing, or bedding, they would instantly ignite them. By 1997, the CPSC had linked these unshielded lamps to at least 189 fires and 11 deaths. [1, 2, 3, 4, 5] The Implementation of Wire Guards The 1997 Recall: To address the immediate hazard, the CPSC partnered with major retail chains to distribute free, clip-on wire grilles to consumers. These wire cages attached directly over the top of the lamp's bowl to create a physical barrier, preventing fabrics and nearby objects from resting against the hot bulb. [1, 2] Manufacturing Changes: Following the recall, Underwriters Laboratories (UL) updated its safety standards. Any halogen torchiere lamps manufactured after late 1997 were required to feature built-in wire guards or glass shields directly from the factory to pass safety certifications. 
Richard Sapper, also famous for IBM ThinkPads. He also designed a kettle for Alessi - my mate owned one, much to the annoyance of his housemates because of the noise it made upon boiling ( a good safety design feature, since you would seek to remove it from the gas hob as soon as possible).  He also designed a lamp that a clumsy user could use to set fire to papers on a desk top - so it was modified with a small protrusion that would prevent the hot bulb from touching flammable materials.

Xbox is about to celebrate its 25th Anniversary, and while there have been numerous brand collaborations to mark the occasion, the 8BitDo Xbox collection has actually been in circulation since November 2024. Inspired by the OG Xbox, the collection is a transparent-green dream consisting of a chunky keyboard, numpad, mouse, and even a controller.I only got my hands on the set this year, which feels fitting with the Xbox anniversary about to be in full swing. While 8BitDo has just added a gorgeous Gears of War edition to the lineup, I'm firmly sticking with my bleed-green setup, which I've been using every day for around four months now. The TL;DR of this review is that I absolutely adore this chunky, obnoxiously green keyboard, and I can't see myself using anything else, but read on if you need a little more swaying.DEAL ALERT: This keyboard is currently 25% off thanks to Amazon Big Deal Days
View Original
View Original
View Original
The 8BitDo Retro 87 Mechanical Keyboard (Xbox Edition) retails for $119.99 in the US and £99.99 in the UK. However, it frequently sees discounts and at the time of writing, it is on sale for $89.99 on Amazon US.
Out of the box, the keyboard includes 8BitDo’s iconic Dual Super Buttons, a pair of massive, programmable arcade-style macro buttons that plug directly into the back of the board via 3.5mm jacks without needing extra software configuration. If you are a sane person who enjoys using a Numpad, rejecting all minimal keyboard ideology, then 8BitDo also offers a matching standalone Retro 18 Mechanical Numpad (Xbox Edition), which retails separately for $44.99 / £39.99.The main tech specs you need to know are:
View Original
View Original
View Original
Unboxing this was an experience in itself; the box was roughly twice the size of any keyboard package I’ve opened before. Coming from the Roccat Vulcan I’d been rocking (or should I say Roccing hur hur), I expected it to be chunky, and while it was different for sure, I'm kind of loving that 8BitDo has embraced the maximalist approach. To be honest, as I get older, I’m really appreciating the larger keycaps and moving away from that classic all-black ""squint at the keys"" aesthetic.
The green design is unabashedly Xbox of course, but with a lot of small, thoughtful touches. Like the A/B/X/Y colored arrow keys that mirror the OG controller and the glowing Xbox home button (which matches the look on the new 25th Anniversary Series X console.) This is a hell of a lot cheaper than that console too! If the Xbox Duke controller were a keyboard and mouse, this would be it.
View Original
View Original
Even before typing a single sentence, the convenient design details impressed me. Having a dedicated magnetic compartment built into the base to store the 2.4GHz receiver is a godsend; I’ve lost count of how many wireless dongles I’ve misplaced over the years.Connectivity is versatile, offering 2.4GHz wireless, Bluetooth, or a wired USB-C connection. I wouldn't normally dedicate a sentence to a cable, but 8BitDo even made the braided wire matching Xbox green. Talk about attention to detail!
 



The board itself feels incredibly hefty, solid, and durable on the desk. That robust build quality is a major plus for me: regardless of how gentle I may be with the keyboard, my son also loves gaming at my desk and is considerably heavier-handed. This chassis easily withstands the enthusiastic key-slams of a kid navigating mods in Wobbly Life. The physical dials at the top for adjusting volume and switching connection modes feel equally premium and far from flimsy.
View Original
View Original
View Original
The box includes 8BitDo’s famous Dual Super Buttons. At first, I had no idea what to do with them other than getting my cat to play the bongos, but they plug in via a 3.5mm jack on the rear. You can map them on the fly directly from the board without software, though downloading the 8BitDo Ultimate Software V2 is essential if you want to set up complex macros or multi-key shortcuts. While I didn't get overly creative with my bindings, mapping the giant ""A"" button to instantly grab screenshots is super handy during streams.
 



If you love RGB backlighting, the board comes packed with bright lighting modes and a full spectrum of color profiles. I usually keep the backlighting on boring white as I think having multi-colours looks a little 'busy' through the green casing, but the customization options are there if you want a full rainbow light show (I do not, and prefer less visual noise).The stock switches deliver a wonderfully satisfying clickety-clackety feel (that’s a technical term), offering great audio and tactile feedback. I also didn't note any input lag even over wireless. For tinkerers, the PCB is fully hot-swappable if you ever want to pull the stock switches and experiment with custom ones later on. Finally, if you opt for the matching standalone numpad, it snaps flush against the side of the board with a deeply satisfying magnet click, just another detail that makes this feel pretty premium.
 



As I said, I love this keyboard, and I'm hard-pressed to find anything I don't like about it, but if I had any criticisms at all, I'd start with the fact that I have to clean it more often!
The super-matte, double-layered ABS keycaps tend to show finger oils and shine much faster and more noticeably than keycaps on previous boards I’ve used. I find myself wiping the keys down more often, but that's a small price to pay for how good the setup looks on my desk.
Second, while I appreciate that 8BitDo made the matching Numpad an optional extra, it's a bit frustrating that both the keyboard and the numpad require their own separate 2.4GHz USB dongles. USB ports on my dock are always at a premium, so I’ve ended up running the main keyboard wired most of the time. Given that the keyboard sits stationary on my desk anyway, it’s hardly a dealbreaker just a minor setup niggle to keep in mind if you are beholden to getting the Numpad too.
You'll only be reading this if you're already interested in grabbing a very in-your-face Xbox fan-themed keyboard, in which case, it's worth mentioning that Razer have released the Xbox 25th Anniversary Collection just recently, and within that, a Black Widow V4 75% keyboard. At $209.99 / £199.99, it carries a distinctly ""Razer price tag"" compared to 8BitDo’s accessible $119.99 MSRP.
 



However, that higher price tag gets you a sleeker, smaller frame and quieter tactile switches.
If you prefer a more subtle, understated tribute to the original Xbox without the chunky retro footprint (and don't mind waiting for pre-orders to ship on October 20), the Razer option is a gorgeous alternative. But for that loud, unapologetic early-2000s green Duke-style aesthetic at nearly half the price, 8BitDo is a no-brainer for me.
View Original
View Original
View Original
✅ You want a nostalgia-packed OG Xbox aesthetic
This board is certainly cheaper (and easier to buy) than the Xbox Series X25, and it nails the retro 2000s theme
✅ You love big keycaps and clicky switches
The keyboard is just as loud as the design
✅ You want versatile wireless options and handy extras
With built-in magnetic dongle storage, 2.4GHz/Bluetooth toggles, a dedicated volume dial, and programmable Dual Super Buttons, it’s packed with great quality-of-life details.
❌ You want a silent, low-profile keyboard
The stock switches are loud and proud, and the chunky retro frame takes up a good chunk of desk space.
❌ You need a built-in numpad without extra dongles
It’s a Tenkeyless layout, and while 8BitDo sells a matching separate numpad, taking up two separate USB ports for two wireless dongles can quickly clutter up your hub or dock.
The 8BitDo Retro 87 Mechanical Keyboard Xbox Edition is just perfect for anyone who holds a soft spot for early 2000s gaming tech, translucent and chunky. I can't wait to sink many hours of WoW Forever on it and live my millennial retirement home dreams. It looks like a collectors edition item without the price tag you would normally expect, while managing to also be built to last and something I want to keep on using (not just take pretty pictures of for Instagram). If you want a desk setup that brings a smile to your face every time you sit down to type or game, this obnoxiously green and thicc beast is worth every cent.
 




Level up your desktop setup with this fitting tribute to the original Xbox. The 8BitDo Retro 87 combines a iconic transparent-green aesthetic with high-performance mechanical typing. Featuring tri-mode connectivity (2.4GHz wireless, Bluetooth 5.0, USB-C wired), tactile clicky Kailh Jellyfish X switches, dedicated volume and mode dials, and a hot-swappable PCB, it’s as versatile as it is striking. Plus, it includes 8BitDo’s programmable Dual Super Buttons for instant macro access right on your desk..

Anything slaying monsters with magical weapons will get a thumbs up such as Dark Souls, Dragon Age, Diablo, and Monster Hunter. When not playing games, she'll be watching a horror or trash reality TV show, she hasn't decided which of those categories the Kardashians fit into. You can follow Jen on Twitter @Jenbox360 for more Diablo fangirling and general moaning about British weather.

68,2026-10-07,"Update font size. Reset
Apple’s new chief executive, John Ternus, sold about $8.5 million of company stock on October 2nd. The headline number looks large, and it is his first reported open-market sale since he succeeded Tim Cook on September 1st. The Form 4 filing shows a routine compensation event, not a discretionary exit.
On October 1st, 99,878 restricted stock units from a 2023 performance award vested on a schedule set years earlier. Vesting is taxable income whether or not the executive sells anything. Apple withheld 49,054 shares to cover that tax bill. Those withheld shares are not part of the sale. Of the shares that remained, Ternus sold 25,412, at prices between $331.38 and $334.05, for roughly $8.46 million. After the vest, the withhold, and the sale, he still held more Apple stock than he did before the award settled.
The trades ran under a Rule 10b5-1 plan Ternus adopted on May 21, 2026 — months before he became CEO. That rule lets insiders lock in future sales in advance, so the execution date is not a judgment on news that day. The plan adoption date matters more than the sale date.
The same calendar hit other executives. Under a plan adopted May 28th, Cook, now executive chair, sold 191,753 shares for about $63.8 million after 374,541 units vested, and still held 3,237,843 shares. Senior vice president Deirdre O’Brien sold 46,389 shares for about $15.5 million. Several officers selling on the same day after one company-wide vest is what a shared pay calendar looks like. Those awards also paid above target: Apple’s three-year total shareholder return was 90.67%, ranking in the 76th percentile of the S&P 500.
Apple’s fiscal third-quarter revenue rose 16.4% to $109.42 billion, and earnings per share of $2.02 beat the $1.89 estimate. Net income grew 27.1%, and Services brought in $30.74 billion. Shares closed at $333.63 on October 6, up 0.22%.
Investors who read Form 4s should separate the codes: an “S” is an open-market sale, an “F” is tax withholding. Here the sale was half of a post-tax vest, pre-scheduled under a 10b5-1 plan, mirrored by Cook and O’Brien, and followed by a larger stake than Ternus held the day before. That is compensation mechanics and certainly not a signal to sell.
 ‎
MacDailyNews Take: Apple stock remains undervalued.

OLED gaming monitors are hot right now, and Prime Big Deal Days is an excellent time to be in the market.

When you purchase through links on our site, we may earn an affiliate commission. Here’s how it works.

OLED panels are quickly becoming a must-have item for desktop PCs. Most of us were introduced to the panel technology in smartphones, and it’s become increasingly popular on laptops. For desktop gamers seeking an immediate visual upgrade, OLED is the way to go, and fortunately, Prime Day is proving to be a great time to take the plunge. From massive DQHD panels on the Samsung Odyssey OLED G9 (G93SC) to more value-oriented offerings like the Acer Nitro VG277U Xbiip, we have you covered.
Here are the best OLED gaming monitors that we’ve seen so far:
Samsung's Odyssey OLED G9 (9G3SC) is massive, with a 49-inch QD-OLED panel. It also features integrated Wi-Fi for interfacing with Samsung's Gaming Hub service.
Mega-ultrawide gaming monitors are not for the faint of heart. Not only can these monitors be quite expensive, but they can also occupy a substantial amount of desktop space. However, for those who have the need (and the space), there are options such as the Samsung Odyssey OLED G9 (G93SC), which features a 49-inch DQHD (5120 x 1440) QD-OLED panel. It's currently priced at $898 on sale at Amazon.
The G93SC sports a maximum 240 Hz refresh rate. We reviewed the G93SC and came away impressed not only with its overall image quality and expansive contrast, but also with its premium build quality. Samsung also includes some excellent extras, such as Bluetooth and Wi-Fi, the latter of which can be used with Samsung TV Plus and the Gaming Hub.
View Original
View Original
Our testing revealed that the G93SC was among the class leaders in input lag and response time, as expected from a premium OLED gaming monitor.
The Samsung Odyssey OLED G9 (G91SD) is a slightly less ambitious 49-inch QD-OLED gaming monitor with a DQHD resolution. Instead of a 240 Hz top speed, the G91SD dials it back to a maximum of 144 Hz.
If you want to save a few bucks, you can also opt for the Samsung 49"" Odyssey OLED G9 (G91SD). It is currently on sale for just $795.99 at Amazon.
You still get a gorgeous 49-inch QD-OLED panel with a 5120 x 1440 resolution, but instead of a maximum 240 Hz refresh rate, you'll top out at 144 Hz. Brightness tops out at 250 nits (typical), and you'll still get a fast 0.03 ms GtG response time. The monitor supports AMD FreeSync Premium Pro, and it's also Nvidia G-Sync compatible. As for ports, you'll find one DisplayPort 1.4, two HDMI 2.1, and two downstream USB-C ports.
The AOC Agon Pro AG346UCD is a highly desirable QD-OLED monitor that strikes a sweet spot with its 34-inch WQHD panel and a 165 Hz refresh rate.
MSI's MAG 341CQP offers a 34-inch WQHD WOLED panel with a 175 Hz refresh rate. It is also a good-looking monitor with thin bezels, which helps with gaming immersion.
If you're looking for something more in a “mid-range” size class, the AOC Agon Pro AG346UCD should be on your shopping list. This is a 34-inch QD-OLED monitor with a 3440 x 1440 resolution and a 165 Hz refresh rate. While that figure may not be as high as the 240 Hz more commonly found with 34-inch panels, the Agon Pro AG346UCD is priced at just $599, taking $50 off its typical asking price. In our review, we praised the monitor for its well-saturated color, excellent contrast, and out-of-the-box accuracy.
 



It's also worth considering the MSI MAG 341CQP, priced at $549.99 ($100 off its usual selling price). You still get a 34-inch QD-OLED panel, but the maximum refresh rate is slightly higher at 175 Hz.
This mid-sized gaming monitor sports a QD-OLED panel rated at a decent 400 nits of brightness, with a 240 Hz refresh and a resolution of 2560 x 1440. If you're after a gaming screen on a budget and want inky blacks and saturated colors, this is a tough deal to beat.
The Alienware AW2725DF might be small in stature, but its 27-inch QHD panel delivers a sizzling 360 Hz maximum refresh rate.
One of the best things to happen to gaming monitors in recent years has been the emergence of OLED panel technology. OLED panels are fast, offer rich color and blacker blacks, but have come with a big price premium. But as the technology has matured, prices have come down. That fact is exemplified by the 27-inch, 240 Hz model (VG277U Xbiip) from Acer's Nitro line. It sports a 240 Hz QD-OLED panel rated at 400 Nits of brightness and a 0.03 ms response time. Best of all, the monitor is currently on sale for just $269, which is unheard of.
We haven't tested this monitor, so we can't vouch for its performance. However, Acer typically makes solid gaming panels, and the QD-OLED panel ensures that the blacks will be truly black and the colors should be vivid. The 400-nit brightness rating isn't the best for an OLED, but it should be sufficient for most home environments (as long as you're not sitting next to a sun-blasted window). The VG277U Xbiip has one DisplayPort (1.4) and two HDMI (2.1) ports. And AMD FreeSync Premium is on the menu for smooth gaming performance.
 



Another option is the Alienware AW2725DF, which also sports a 27-inch QHD panel, albeit using QD-OLED technology. When the AW2725DF arrived in our testing lab, we noted its large color gamut, fast response, and stunning image quality. The monitor also boasts a 360 Hz refresh rate for ultra-twitch gamers. Currently, the AW2725DF is on sale for $469.99, representing a 28% savings.

71,2026-10-07,"U.S.-based financial services company Robinhood HOOD$109.52·Market Closed has added approximately $25 million worth of bitcoin BTC$83,207.05 to its corporate balance sheet. 
The disclosure was made by Johann Kerbrat, Robinhood's Senior Vice President and General Manager of Crypto and International, during an interview on The Starting Block broadcast at the Digital Asset Summit (DAS) Asia conference on Wednesday.
Kebrat explained that the decision to add BTC to the balance sheet represents how deeply we care about bitcoin and the ecosystem.  
“Robinhood is a massive company at this point, with a market cap in the $100 billion [range]. So the $25 million worth of bitcoin is not going to change a lot of the current trajectory of the company,"" he added.
CoinDesk separately reached out to Robinhood’s press team inquire about potential plans to acquire more BTC or other tokens in the future.
The move puts Robinhood among a growing cohort of public companies holding BTC as a treasury asset. Nasdaq-listed Strategy MSTR$154.55·Market Closed started this trend in 2020 and now its followed by more than 170 firms worldwide. Current corporate holders include names such as Tesla, Block, MARA Holdings, CleanSpark, and Riot Platforms, which together account for well over 1.2 million BTC across their balance sheets.
Robinhood, which has over 28 million funded customers globally, is not new to crypto. In July it launched Robinhood Chain, an Ethereum‑compatible Layer 2 built on Arbitrum and geared toward tokenized real‑world assets and decentralized finance. 
Since mainnet’s debut, the chain has rolled out tokenized stock products, a stablecoin (USDG), and yield‑bearing lending via Robinhood Earn, while integrating major DeFi protocols like Uniswap and infrastructure from Chainlink, Alchemy, and BitGo.

Grab these SSDs at an even better price before the deals run out

When you purchase through links on our site, we may earn an affiliate commission. Here’s how it works.

Amazon has gone absolutely rogue by slashing prices on the Samsung 9100 Pro again before its Big Deals sales event is over. We're in the last stretch, but the 1TB, 2TB, and 4TB models of this top-spec SSD are now cheaper than they were yesterday. The 4TB 9100 Pro has seen a huge cut, from $1,119.99 yesterday to $734.99 today, a price we've not seen in months. Elsewhere, the 1TB drive has dropped below $200, while the 2TB drive is now below $335, and the 8TB is staying steady at $2299.99.
This really is the best time to pick up one of these Samsung SSDs, especially if you want to upgrade your storage at a price that beats the current market. There's no guarantee that the stock will last, but we do know that these deal prices are set to go in less than 24 hours. These Gen 5 drives are still, somehow, beating the Gen 4 Samsung 990 Pro on pricing, making this one of the deals of the whole event.
This is a brilliant SSD, no doubt about it, and easily one of the best SSDs you can buy. As our Samsung 9100 Pro review explains, this drive ships with Samsung's own Presto controller and 236-layer V8 NAND. You're getting max sequential reads and writes of 14,800 MB/s and 13,400 MB/s apiece, although speeds do vary slightly, depending on capacity.
At those speeds, however, you're not really hitting too many bottlenecks. This is a drive that can take the lot, from basic tasks to intensive AI workloads without much issue. That said, a high-performance drive in the current market isn't really the point. Price is the factor that you'll need to consider most, and so is the warranty, to ensure that you're protected if the drive fails.
This PCIe 5.0 SSD comes with a five-year limited warranty as standard, but it also has pretty good endurance specs, so it should last pretty well under tough conditions. The 2TB model, for example, is rated for 1,200 TBW. You'd need to be writing to more than a third of the drive every single day for five years to beat the warranty's coverage.
The Samsung 9100 Pro is a high-end PCIe 5.0 SSD featuring Samsung's own Presto controller and 236-layer NAND. The 1 TB model delivers sequential read and write speeds up to 14,700 MB/s and 13,300 MB/s, respectively.
The Samsung 9100 Pro is a high-end PCIe 5.0 SSD featuring Samsung's own Presto controller and 236-layer NAND. The 2 TB model delivers sequential read and write speeds up to 14,700 MB/s and 13,400 MB/s, respectively.
The Samsung 9100 Pro is a high-end PCIe 5.0 SSD featuring Samsung's own Presto controller and 236-layer NAND. The 4TB model delivers sequential read and write speeds up to 14,800 MB/s and 13,400 MB/s, respectively.
As specs go, the Samsung 9100 Pro is pretty much one of the best-performing drives on the market. You'll really notice the difference if you're upgrading from an older PCIe 4.0 drive. For instance, the Samsung 990 Pro, one of the best Gen 4 SSDs you can buy and this drive's predecessor, can 'only' hit read and write speeds of 7,940 and 6,900 MB/s, compared to the 14,800 MB/s and 13,400 MB/s here. You'll see even slower speeds at Gen 4, and below 600 MB/s with an older SATA SSD.
This isn't the fastest Gen 5 SSD, but that's hardly the point. The 9100 Pro is still a high-performance drive and, given these prices, almost impossible to beat on a cost-to-performance basis.
View Original
View Original
View Original
View Original
View Original
View Original
Compared to the prices we saw yesterday, the 2TB model is even better value at just $0.167 per GB. The 1TB model isn't bad, though, as it drops below $200 for the first time in a while, and you're getting $0.196 per GB there. The 4TB model, meanwhile, sits in the middle, at $0.183, but that huge $385 discount on yesterday's pricing is difficult to ignore.
You can't go wrong with any of these high-performance Samsung 9100 Pro SSDs, though. You just can't wait long to buy one. These prices are set to expire by the end of the Amazon Prime sales event later today, but these SSDs could sell out before then. Grab one while you can, as time is running out fast.

EXCLUSIVE: Smart-TV maker Vizio, which was acquired by Walmart in 2024, is bringing to market an original holiday movie and more programming from premium suppliers and creators in a major content expansion.

	The initiative, unveiled Wednesday during Advertising Week in New York, is designed to re-introduce brands to Vizio, which is now the top smart TV operating system thanks to Walmart‘s massive retail scale. 

	The film, Jingle Bells Wedding Bells, starring Rachel Bilson and Scott Foley and produced by ITV America Nashville and Happy Accidents, will premiere exclusively on Vizio’s WatchFree+ platform this holiday season. Geico is presenting sponsor of the premiere.

	“As viewing habits continue to evolve, partnerships like this give us new ways to connect with audiences,” Arianna Orpello, Geico’s chief marketing officer, said in a statement.




			Watch on Deadline		






	WatchFree+, a free, ad-supported live and on-demand streaming destination, has more than 45,000 film and TV titles along with more than 400 live and local channels. The platform said Wednesday it is deepening its ties with MGM, NBCUniversal, Disney, A&E and A24, adding programming titles this fall like The Good Doctor, Chicago Fire, Law & Order, Property Brothers, Little House on the Prairie.

	Another key component of the expansion is creator content. Also bound for the platform are more than 40 creator-led FAST channels spanning comedy, food, gaming, family, lifestyle, sports and travel. Creators fronting the channels include MrBeast, Celine Dept, Hot Ones, The Royalty Family, Ben Azelart, Ryan Trahan, Nick DiGiovanni, Preston Playz, Rebecca Zamolo, Sam and Colby, and Uncle Roger.

	A creator-led series, Holiday Chocolate Workshop, will center on chocolatier Amaury Guichon. The series follows Guichon and his team of expert chocolatiers as they craft “ambitious, larger-than-life holiday masterpieces for a season-ending reveal,” according to the official logline.

	Originals and channels with creators enable WatchFree+ to retain more viewers before they fall into YouTube, which doesn’t provide the same level of advertising returns for stakeholders. 

	










	Since it ceased being a public company and becoming part of Walmart, Vizio has not regularly released data on viewership. Along with the programming news, the company and its corporate parent said the Vizio OS now reaches 32 million yearly active devices, up 20% year-over-year. Viewing hours of WatchFree+ is up 58% year-over-year, according to internal data.

75,2026-10-07,"My Synology DS1621xs+ got its first upgrade years ago, and it wasn't the obvious one. I filled both M.2 slots with 500GB Gen4 NVMe drives and set them up as an SSD cache because the spec sheet said that's what the slots were for. The RAM upgrade, from the stock 8GB to 32GB, came later. I can't say I've ever filled the memory up, and I bought those two modules based on the maximum my NAS would support.
I've since tested SSD caching in my NAS properly, and I've had it backward the whole time. For most home setups, memory does the job people buy a cache for, and does it faster. There are a few cases where the cache still earns its slot, though, and they're worth knowing before you spend money on either.
Product segmentation works in odd ways sometimes
Every major NAS operating system runs on Linux, including Synology DSM, QNAP QTS, UGREEN UGOS, Unraid, and TrueNAS SCALE. Linux treats any memory that apps aren't using as a read cache, called the page cache. Open a file once, and it stays in RAM until something else needs the space, so the second read never touches a drive.
That cache is faster than any SSD you can put in an M.2 slot; it needs no setup, and it doesn't show up as a cache anywhere in DSM. Resource Monitor just calls it ""Cached"" memory. So the real question isn't RAM or cache. It's whether your NAS needs a second, slower cache on top of the one it already has.
ZFS users figured this out years ago. The standard advice on the TrueNAS forums is to max out the RAM before adding L2ARC, its SSD read cache, and XDA's own guide to using a spare SSD for L2ARC on TrueNAS says the same. A Synology box plays by the same rules. It just doesn't advertise it.
If it ain't broke...
The average user might be using a stock NAS running Plex, a couple of Docker containers, Synology Photos, and a backup task. None of those are heavy on their own, but together they can push memory use to the limit. When that happens, DSM starts moving memory to swap on the hard drives, and a swapped-out app waits on spinning platters every time it wakes up.
One popular Synology Docker guide warns that DSM ""will begin using swap on disk and killing services when everything is competing for it,"" even while it shows free memory, and recommends at least 8GB, ideally 16GB or more. An SSD cache doesn't address this problem. It speeds up reads from your volume, not the memory pressure making everything else crawl.
A read cache only helps with data you read more than once. On a home NAS, that's usually a small set: the folders you browse every day, photo thumbnails, a Plex or Jellyfin database, and the file system's own metadata. For one household, that hot set is often a few gigabytes, which fits comfortably in 16GB or 32GB of RAM.
Picture someone scrolling through a big photo library over SMB. The first pass pulls thumbnails and directory listings off the hard drives. With enough RAM, every pass after that comes from memory, and an SSD cache would just be keeping a second, slower copy of the same data. Memory wins this one because it would serve those reads before the SSD could.
My own testing hinted at this. When I finally put that empty NVMe slot on my NAS to work, a read-only Optane cache hit 64% and quadrupled synthetic random read speed. But container starts, database work, and file extraction all ran the same or slower. Those numbers were also measured with the RAM cache deliberately switched off, which is why the SSD looked impressive.
Synology's own SSD cache documentation says the SSD cache only accelerates random I/O and skips sequential transfers like video streaming. Movies, music, Time Machine backups, and big file copies are mostly sequential, so they go straight to the hard drives as if the cache wasn't there.
To be fair, more RAM isn't a miracle here either, since hard drives and the network set the pace for big transfers. But the extra memory still buffers incoming writes and keeps metadata handy, and it costs nothing to configure. If your NAS is mainly a media library and a backup target, an SSD cache is two M.2 slots doing very little.
The memory shortage made the decision for me.
Here's the part almost nobody mentions. Synology's SSD cache keeps a map of what's on the SSD in system memory, roughly 400KB of RAM for every gigabyte of cache. DSM also limits the cache to 25% of installed memory, according to Synology's cache creation guide.
I've got 32GB in my Synology, so that overhead is noise. On a stock DS224+ or DS423+ with 2GB, it comes out of the same small pool your apps and page cache are already fighting over. So the people most tempted to add a cache instead of RAM are the ones for whom it costs the most. ZFS has the same catch, since L2ARC keeps its index in RAM too.
A read-only cache is safe, because everything on it already lives on the hard drives. A read-write cache is different. New writes sit only on the SSDs until DSM flushes them, which is why Synology requires two drives in RAID 1 and still warns that you can lose data if more SSDs wear out than the array can tolerate.
RAM has no equivalent failure mode. If a module dies, replacing it keeps your storage volume untouched. Upgrading is easier too. DSM runs third-party ECC memory with an ""unsupported"" notice, while Synology still won't let you create a new SSD cache with third-party NVMe drives on its 2025 models.
I added NVMe caching to my media NAS and found the biggest improvement wasn’t faster file transfers, but quicker access to Jellyfin and app data.
It wouldn't be fair to stop there. Databases and VM disks don't just write data; they force it onto permanent storage before moving on, with a call named fsync. RAM can't speed that up without turning off the safety net that keeps your database from corrupting after a power cut.
That's where a read-write cache shines. In my testing, a read-write Optane cache cut a Postgres container's cold start from 33.1 seconds to 7.2 seconds and more than tripled database throughput, from 109 to 366 transactions per second. No RAM upgrade would have done that. The other case is a NAS that's already at its memory limit, with lots of users and a hot set bigger than RAM, where a read cache is the only way to add another fast tier.
Even then, I'd consider an NVMe storage pool for the databases first. You get the same write speed without risking your main volume.
SSD cache won’t transform a hard-drive array, but it can make the small, repetitive storage tasks that feel slow noticeably less annoying.
If you're weighing the two, open Resource Monitor or whatever shows you memory usage on your NAS first. Swap in use or memory pinned near full means it's time to upgrade your NAS RAM. If databases or VMs are slow, look at the write path with a read-write cache or an NVMe volume. And only consider a read-only cache once the RAM is maxed and your re-read data still doesn't fit. The only caveat is memory pricing, so don't overbuy if you are expanding: 16GB is plenty for most users with prebuilt NAS enclosures.
We want to hear from you. Share your perspective in the comments below, and please keep the conversation respectful.

76,2026-10-07,"
Physical media is quietly winning again, phones are starting to treat light as a material instead of an afterthought, and the humble power bank just got embarrassingly thin. This month’s list pulls from every corner of consumer tech: a CD player built for a generation that never owned one, a camera disguised as a roll of film, and a keyboard that looks more like furniture than gaming gear.
What ties these eight together isn’t a single trend so much as a shared instinct: solve one problem well instead of chasing a longer spec sheet. Some of these are shipping this month. A couple are concepts worth watching closely. All of them earned their spot through genuine design thinking rather than a press release checklist.


Collecting CDs only matters if you have something to play them on, and that’s been the real gap for anyone under thirty who’s started buying physical music again. The R400, designed by ORGDOT for Syitren, solves that with a detachable modular shell that lowers cost without making the player feel cheap. For you, it means an entry point into physical media that doesn’t ask you to hunt down a decade-old Discman on eBay.
What makes it worth owning day to day is the small stuff. A sunken charging compartment keeps your cable from fraying, Bluetooth 5.3 handles wireless streaming, and anti-vibration engineering means no skipping on your commute. At four hours of battery per charge and a price well below premium audio gear, it fills the exact space Sony abandoned when it quietly discontinued its CD boomboxes in the US.


Pick this up without knowing what it is, and you’d swear it was an unopened roll of 35mm film, right down to the box it ships in. It’s actually a fully digital camera, and that disguise is the entire point. For you, it turns picture-taking back into something tactile, a small object you display on a shelf rather than another app icon buried on your phone.
The 1 megapixel CMOS sensor leans into grain and softness instead of correcting for it, so every shot looks like a well-worn photograph rather than a clinical digital capture. At 25 grams with USB-C and microSD support, it travels light and clips onto a bag strap without you noticing the weight. At $55, it’s priced like a fun accessory rather than a serious camera, which is exactly what it’s going for.
Click Here to Buy Now: $55.00


The last Lamborghini-branded phone cost $6,000 and ran on two-year-old silicon because a logo was stamped onto gold plating. The badge on this one actually does something on this one: 177 individually controlled mini-LEDs draw the Lamborghini “L” in light, while a red Pulse Line shifts and rises across the back panel using internal mirror structures rather than a printed graphic. For you, it’s proof that a co-brand can be more than a sticker job.
TECNO spent close to four months reworking the software to match, down to icon shapes nudged toward hexagon and shield silhouettes. The manufacturing challenge wasn’t making one unit look right; it was making thousands of units look consistent, which TECNO calls one of the most complex rear cover processes it has run. The specs mirror the standard POVA 8 Pro, so you’re paying for the surface, not new hardware underneath.


Florent Porta noticed something nobody else did: Sony’s MZ-RH1 MiniDisc recorder mounted its display on the front edge instead of the top, readable at an angle no other player offered. The MAD-5 concept takes that detail and builds an entire player around it, a square aluminum body with a thin blue ticker running along the clipped edge. For you, it solves what clip-on players have ignored for two decades, showing information without needing a screen on the face.
The clip itself borrows from the fourth-generation iPod Shuffle, while a jog disc on the front nods to Teenage Engineering’s TP-7 without the motorized reel underneath. There’s no Bluetooth, no balanced output, and nothing marked for expandable storage, all treated as current audiophile DAP standards elsewhere. The real idea might be its USB DAC mode, letting it double as an audio interface the moment you plug it into a laptop.


Most gaming keyboards announce themselves before you even press a key, all glowing lights and cases that belong beside a spaceship. This one goes the other direction entirely, a walnut frame and bottom case giving a genuinely fast magnetic switch keyboard the look of desk furniture instead of gaming gear. For you, it means one keyboard that works all day instead of one you hide before a video call.
Underneath that calm exterior sit Gateron Double-Rail Magnetic Nebula switches with actuation adjustable from 0.2 to 3.8mm and Rapid Trigger for instant key reset. The tenkeyless layout drops the number pad so your mouse hand has room to move, and switching between Bluetooth, 2.4GHz, or a cable means it follows you from laptop to gaming PC without a second purchase. Browser-based customization skips installing an app entirely.


A mouse that folds flat enough to disappear into a laptop sleeve solves a problem anyone who’s worked from a café already knows: that full-sized comfort and genuine portability rarely come in the same device. Designed by Horace Lam, OrigamiSwift borrows its triangular structure from origami folding and springs into shape in under half a second. For you, that means leaving the bulky travel mouse at home without giving up ergonomic comfort.
At just 40 grams, it barely registers in a bag until you need it, and the USB-C rechargeable battery lasts up to three months on a single charge. The HD infrared sensor tracks at up to 4000 CPI with a 4000 frames-per-second imaging rate, numbers that hold up against mice twice its size. Soft click buttons keep it quiet enough for shared workspaces or quiet flights.
Click Here to Buy Now: $85.00


Power banks have been stuck in the same bulky shape for years, so a 6mm thick, 98 gram unit that’s thinner than most smartphones sounds like a typo until you actually hold one. Xiaomi pulled it off using a silicon-carbon battery with 16 percent silicon content, packing 5,000mAh into something closer to a metal business card. For you, it means a backup battery you forget is in your pocket until the moment you need it.
It snaps onto your phone magnetically for up to 15W wireless charging, though iPhones are capped at 7.5W due to Apple’s MagSafe restrictions, and a USB-C port handles up to 22.5W wired. Ten layers of protection cover overvoltage, overheating, and short circuits, with dual temperature sensors and a graphite sheet managing heat in real time. At roughly $60 to $65 depending on region, the price tracks with the engineering involved.


Wired earbuds are having a real moment again, mostly because nobody wants to worry about a charging case dying or buds getting lost in transit. Bose answered that comeback with active noise cancellation built into a tethered pair, something only a handful of trusted brands currently offer. For you, it means the reliability of a cable paired with the quiet you’d normally expect from a much pricier wireless set.
USB-C connectivity keeps things current, while two microphones per bud handle adaptive noise cancelling across Quiet, Aware, and Off modes. The proprietary Sound Design processing outputs a fixed 24-bit, 48kHz signal for lossless listening, and low-latency performance makes gaming or fast-paced video noticeably smoother than typical wireless buds. An IPX4 rating covers sweat and splashes, with a zippered case for carrying.
Eight very different problems, solved with eight very different budgets. The cheapest entry here costs $55, and the most expensive sits just under $200, yet the thinking behind each one matters more than where it lands on a price chart. A CD player built for people too young to remember CDs, a phone that turns a logo into light, a mouse that folds flat enough to vanish- these are the kind of details that separate genuine design work from a spec sheet with a release date attached.
If one thread runs through this month’s picks, it’s restraint. Nobody here tried to cram in every feature available. The R400 leaned into a modular shell instead of extra buttons. The MAD-5 skipped Bluetooth to focus on one screen placement nobody else thought to try. That kind of focus is rarer than it should be, and it’s exactly why these eight earned a spot this October.
