Nature
                             (2026) Cite this article

Gram-negative bacteria are surrounded by a multilayered cell envelope with a mostly impermeable outer membrane that provides intrinsic resistance to many antibiotics1,2,3,4,5. The outer membrane is an asymmetrical bilayer with phospholipids in the inner leaflet and lipopolysaccharide (LPS) in the outer leaflet6,7. An LPS transport (Lpt) machine called LptB2FGCADE moves LPS across a protein bridge from the inner membrane to the outer membrane8,9. LPS biosynthesis is regulated to prevent toxic accumulation of LPS molecules in the inner membrane during growth10,11,12. Whether LPS transport across the Lpt bridge is also regulated has been unclear. Here we present three structures of the trans-envelope Lpt complex in LPS-free, LPS-bound and ATP-bound states, along with a structure of a partial bridge. These structures, combined with biochemical experiments, show that Lpt bridge assembly triggers movement of the transmembrane helix of LptC (TM-LptC) in an LPS-dependent manner, resulting in increased ATP binding and hydrolysis. We also show that LPS transport in vivo requires bridge formation and movement of the TM-LptC. Our data support a model in which assembled Lpt bridges respond to the presence of LPS in the inner membrane to turn on transport by moving the TM-LptC, thus coordinating LPS transport activity with bridge assembly and the presence of LPS at the inner membrane.
Bacterial cells have an inner (cytoplasmic) membrane (IM) containing protein machines that assemble other cell-envelope layers, such as the peptidoglycan cell wall. Gram-negative bacteria also have an outer membrane (OM) that surrounds the peptidoglycan cell wall1,2. This OM has a phospholipid inner leaflet and an LPS outer leaflet6,7. The LPS layer of the OM excludes many antibiotics that are effective against Gram-positive bacteria, greatly limiting therapeutic strategies to treat Gram-negative infections4,5.
LPS is synthesized at the IM and must be transported across the aqueous periplasm and through the OM to the cell surface3. The LPS transport (Lpt) machinery consists of an IM ABC transporter complex (LptB2FGC), an OM translocon (LptDE) and a soluble periplasmic protein (LptA)13,14,15,16,17,18,19,20,21 (Fig. 1a). During the transport of LPS to the cell surface, LptA assembles with the two membrane complexes, forming a continuous trans-envelope protein bridge (LptB2FGCADE)22. LptB2FGCADE uses the energy from ATP binding and hydrolysis in the cytoplasm to extract LPS from the IM and transport it against its concentration gradient directly to the outer leaflet of the OM22,23,24,25,26,27.
a, Cartoon schematic showing the LPS transport proteins in the unassembled (left) and assembled (right) states. LPS is transported across a membrane-spanning protein bridge. Pi, inorganic phosphate. b, Representative electron micrograph of LptB2FGCADE complexes purified in detergent. Dotted white circles indicate selected LptB2FGCADE complexes. The representative micrograph was chosen to contain approximately the median number of particles per micrograph out of particles included in the final reconstruction. Scale bar, 50 nm. c, Representative 2D class averages generated in cryoSPARC, with orientation aligned to aid visualization. Scale bar, 210 Å. d, Composite density for LPS-bound LptB2FGCADE consisting of the local refinements of LptADE and LptB2FGC, shown from a front view perpendicular to the micelle containing LptDE. The consensus map used for local refinement, low-pass filtered to 10 Å, is shown as a transparent envelope. Cross-sections of the LptB2FGCADE density at the indicated positions are shown. For comparisons of the IM and OM refinements of LptB2FGCADE with previously reported structures of Lpt subcomplexes38,57,60,61, see Supplementary Fig. 5. e, Model of LptB2FGCADE shown in the same view and colours as in d. The periplasmic domains of LptFG did not refine to high resolution; for visualization, these regions of LptF (residues 137–246) and LptG (residues 142–244) from Protein Data Bank (PDB) 6MHU were fitted as rigid bodies into the consensus structure and are shown in semi-transparent grey. The dotted box indicates the position of the magnified views shown in f. f, Magnified views of the periplasmic portion of the bridge viewed from different angles to illustrate the opening that accommodates the LPS substrate during transport.
A key requirement for LPS transport is that newly synthesized LPS must be transported to the OM efficiently to prevent toxic build-up of LPS at the IM. One mechanism of controlling cellular LPS levels is to turn off LPS biosynthesis10,11. Regulation of the rate of LPS transport would provide an alternative strategy to control IM LPS levels, but it has been unclear whether such regulation occurs.
The rate of LPS transport depends on how many bridges exist and whether they are in an active state. Two types of LptB2FGCADE bridge with different lifetimes have been observed in cells, and we have proposed that only the longer-lived bridges actively transport LPS28. Reducing the levels of LPS decreases the fraction of Lpt components that form assembled bridges in cells, which implies that bridge assembly depends on LPS28. However, the mechanism that underlies the formation of active bridges has been unclear. Here we present three distinct structures of the assembled Lpt bridge, together with a structure of a partial bridge. These structures, combined with biochemistry, reveal the molecular mechanisms that control the formation of active Lpt bridges.
To obtain a structure of the full Lpt bridge, we first attempted to isolate intact bridges directly from cells, but the assembled bridges dissociated during purification (Supplementary Fig. 1). We therefore sought to reconstitute the bridge in vitro from separately purified subcomplexes. We have previously shown that a variant of the IM subcomplex in which LptA is fused to LptC as a single polypeptide joined by a flexible poly-glycine-serine linker (hereafter termed LptC–LptA fusion) is able to form Lpt bridges28. Bridges formed with the IM subcomplex containing the LptC–LptA fusion (hereafter termed LptB2FGCA) are more stable in cells than are wild-type bridges, so allowed us to avoid the tendency of soluble LptA to oligomerize when assembling Lpt bridges in vitro18,28,29 (Extended Data Fig. 1a). We confirmed that the LptC–LptA fusion allows growth on minimal medium in the absence of native LptC and LptA (Extended Data Fig. 1b–d) and assessed the activity of LptB2FGCA through in vivo and in vitro assays. We observe in vivo site-specific cross-linking of the LptC–LptA fusion to LPS at positions known to cross-link LPS in the individual proteins (Extended Data Fig. 1e). The ATPase activity of LptB2FGCA in proteoliposomes is similar to that of LptB2FGC (Extended Data Fig. 1f) and we used a flow-cytometry-based assay to confirm that LptB2FGCA can form bridges with LptDE in vitro without the addition of soluble LptA (refs. 22,30; Extended Data Fig. 1g and Supplementary Fig. 2). Thus, LptB2FGCA recapitulates the functions of the individual LptC and LptA proteins both in vivo and in biochemical assays.
We also stabilized the interface between LptD and LptA by site-specifically incorporating the photocrosslinkable amino acid p-benzoyl-l-phenylalanine (pBPA) at position Y63 in the N-terminal domain of LptD. This site has been shown to cross-link to LptA with high efficiency31. We separately purified LptB2FGCA and LptD(Y63pBPA)E in detergent and co-incubated the purified complexes while exposing to ultraviolet (UV) light. We detected high-efficiency cross-link formation, enabling purification of the full bridge by size-exclusion chromatography (SEC) (Supplementary Fig. 3). We prepared samples for cryo-electron microscopy (cryo-EM) using the purified, covalently stabilized bridge (LptB2FGCAD(Y63pBPA)E; hereafter, LptB2FGCADE).
We observed dumbbell-shaped particles, consistent with two connected membrane complexes (Fig. 1b and Extended Data Fig. 2). The two-dimensional (2D) class averages showed that the angle between the IM protein complex and the bridge is variable (Fig. 1c), indicating a high degree of flexibility in this region. The flexibility limited our ability to simultaneously resolve the IM and OM portions of the Lpt bridge to high resolution. We therefore performed local refinements focused on the region of the structure comprising either LptB2FGC or LptADE starting from a consensus map of the LptB2FGCADE bridge (Extended Data Fig. 2). These refinements yielded a 3.3-Å-resolution density map for the IM portion and a 3.4-Å-resolution density map for the OM region of LptB2FGCADE. We integrated these local refinements into the consensus map to construct a composite density map (Fig. 1d) for the Lpt bridge and built a model corresponding to this composite map (Fig. 1e). The periplasmic domains of LptFG (LptF residues 137–246; LptG residues 142–244) remained poorly resolved in the composite map because of flexible connections to their respective TM regions and were therefore not modelled. We speculate that the observed flexibility of the LptFG periplasmic domains in bridges could be required to allow the formation of a trans-envelope complex (results of three-dimensional (3D) variability analysis are shown in Supplementary Fig. 4 and Supplementary Video 1). Proteins in the OM are mostly immobile, whereas IM proteins can laterally diffuse freely32,33. Thus, the flexibility we observe could afford LptB2FGC a range of 2D motion in the IM to move relative to LptDE without breaking the bridge.
The Lpt trans-envelope complex is an elongated structure, approximately 280 Å in length from edge to edge of the membrane complexes (Fig. 1d). There is clear density corresponding to a LPS molecule bound in the lumen formed by the TM helices of LptFG (hereafter, LptFG lumen; Fig. 1d, panels 7 and 8). The periplasmic domain is approximately 150 Å in length, somewhat shorter than the estimated periplasmic dimension for Escherichia coli34 (180–240 Å). It is possible that Lpt bridges in vivo incorporate more than one copy of LptA (approximately 40 Å), although our data indicate that the LptC–LptA fusion with no free LptA is viable (Extended Data Fig. 1b–d). The structure shows that the IM and OM complexes are connected by the β-jellyroll domains of LptF, LptC, LptA and LptD, which assemble to form a groove that spirals slightly8,18,35,36 (Fig. 1f). We propose that the spiralling groove serves to minimize clashes between the oligosaccharide portions of adjacent LPS molecules as they are being trafficked. Cross-sections perpendicular to the long axis of the groove show variability in the space available to accommodate the LPS substrate (Fig. 1d, panels 1–6). A representation of the internal volume of the periplasm chute calculated with pyKVFinder37 is shown in Extended Data Fig. 3. The concave surface of the groove is lined predominantly with hydrophobic residues, consistent with the idea that this groove accommodates the acyl chains of LPS during transit to the OM. However, we observe some constriction points in the chute, with insufficient volume to allow passage of LPS. Thus, conformational flexibility in the periplasmic domains must be required to allow LPS transport.
The TM helices of LptF and LptG and the bound LPS molecule are well resolved in our local refinement of the LptB2FGC portion of the Lpt bridge, but we did not detect density for the TM helix of LptC (TM-LptC), which suggests that the TM-LptC is not ordered in our structure of the Lpt bridge bound to LPS (Fig. 1d,e). Moreover, the TM helices of LptFG adopt a conformation more similar to that seen for LptB2FG (root mean square deviation (RMSD) < 1 Å) than to that of LptB2FGC (RMSD = 2.3 Å) (refs. 32,33,34,35; Supplementary Fig. 5). In this conformation, the helices of LptFG make close contacts with the bound LPS molecule. Previous structural work has shown that the TM-LptC is not resolved when LptB in the LptB2FGC complex is bound to nucleotide in the closed dimer conformation38,39. However, it has been unclear whether the TM-LptC moves because ATP binds and promotes LptB dimerization or whether movement of the TM-LptC allows ATP binding38,39,40,41. Our structure of LptB2FGCADE suggests that in the context of the assembled Lpt bridge, the TM-LptC can move before nucleotide binding.
We wondered if it would be possible to obtain a structure with LPS on the periplasmic portion of the Lpt bridge rather than in the LptFG lumen. We have previously established that residue T47 in LptC, when substituted with pBPA, forms a very strong cross-link to LPS23,30. We have proposed that LPS binds in this location after extraction from the IM. We site-specifically incorporated pBPA at T47 in the LptC–LptA fusion and attempted to obtain structures with LPS trapped on the bridge by purifying LptB2FGC(T47pBPA)A from cells exposed to UV light before protein purification. We detected LPS cross-linking to LptB2FGC(T47pBPA)A by immunoblotting and used this LPS-cross-linked complex to prepare bridges (hereafter, LptB2FGC(T47pBPA)ADE; Extended Data Fig. 4). We solved the cryo-EM structure using a similar approach to that used for the structure above (Fig. 2a,b and Extended Data Fig. 4). We again observed considerable flexibility between the periplasmic and TM domains of LptFG, which resulted in these regions being unresolved in our density maps (Supplementary Fig. 4 and Supplementary Video 1). Because this unresolved region includes LptC T47, we were unable to visualize the cross-linked LPS.
a, Composite density map for LptB2FGC(T47pBPA)ADE. b, Model of LPS-free LptB2FGCADE in the same view, coloured as in a. The periplasmic domains of LptFG (LptF139–243 and LptG143–243), did not refine to high resolution, were modelled as rigid fits of domains from PDB 6MHU into the consensus structure and are shown in transparent grey. c, Density map for LptB2FGCA with a transparent envelope corresponding to the low-pass-filtered map (10 Å) to illustrate the micelle and the periplasmic domains of LptFGCA. d, Model of LptB2FGCA coloured by subunit. The periplasmic domains of LptFGC and LptA, which did not refine to high resolution, were modelled as rigid fits of domains from PDB 6MHU or the LPS-free LptB2FGCADE and are shown in transparent grey. e, Representative immunoblots showing the uncrosslinked LPS present in each sample used for structure determination, with anti-LptB used as a loading control. Blots with different antibodies represent identical samples processed in parallel. f, IM local refinement of LptB2FGCADE bound to LPS. Top, IM density for LPS-bound LptB2FGCADE, low-pass-filtered to 4.1 Å, shown from the top (left) and, in cross-section, from the front. The dotted line indicates the position of the cross-section. Bottom, top view of the model for the LPS-bound lumenal region of LptB2FGCADE. g, IM local refinement of LPS-free LptB2FGCADE. Top, IM density for LptB2FGCADE without LPS, low-pass-filtered to 4.1 Å, with views as in f. Bottom, top view of the model for the LPS-free lumenal region of LptB2FGCADE. h, LptB2FGCA bound to LPS. Top, density for LPS-bound LptB2FGCA, low-pass-filtered to 4.1 Å, with views as in f. Bottom, top view of the model for the LPS-bound lumenal region of LptB2FGCA.
The OM portions of the two bridges are very similar (RMSD < 0.5 Å); however, the IM local refinement of LptB2FGC(T47pBPA)ADE has well-resolved density for the TM-LptC and adopts a conformation similar to that of LptB2FGC (Supplementary Fig. 6). Moreover, there was no clear density for LPS in the LptFG lumen for the LptB2FGC(T47pBPA)ADE bridge (Fig. 2g). We compared the relative amount of LPS that copurified with each of the two Lpt bridge preparations (Fig. 2e, lanes 1 and 2) and found that the LptB2FGC(T47pBPA)ADE sample contained a small amount of uncrosslinked LPS relative to the LptB2FGCADE sample. We also obtained a low-resolution structure of LptB2FGCADE lacking LPS in the LptFG lumen (Extended Data Fig. 5), and the TM-LptC in this structure is ordered. Thus, when there is no LPS in the LptFG lumen, the TM-LptC is in its resting position even in the intact bridge. Together, the two types of bridge structure—that is, with and without LPS bound in the LptFG lumen—suggest that LPS binding causes a conformational change in the TM helices of LptFG with concomitant delocalization of the TM-LptC. We refer to these two states of the Lpt bridges as the LPS-bound or the LPS-free state on the basis of whether the LptFG lumen contains LPS.
Our two structures of the Lpt bridge imply that LPS binding in the LptFG lumen and dissociation of the TM-LptC are linked. However, previous structures of LptB2FGC with LPS bound in the LptFG lumen show that the TM-LptC is ordered38,39. Hence, we wondered whether movement of the TM-LptC is dependent not only on LPS, but also on bridge formation. We prepared a sample of LPS-bound LptB2FGCA (Fig. 2e, lane 3) and determined its structure by cryo-EM to a resolution of 3.1 Å (Fig. 2c,d, Extended Data Fig. 6 and Supplementary Video 2). We detected clear density in the LptFG lumen for both LPS and the ordered TM-LptC (Fig. 2h). By contrast, in the LPS-bound and LPS-free structures of LptB2FGCADE, we observed either clearly resolved density for LPS in the LptFG lumen but no density for the TM-LptC (LPS-bound LptB2FGCADE; Fig. 2f) or well-resolved density for the TM-LptC and negligible density for LPS in the LptFG lumen (LPS-free bridges; Fig. 2g). To compare the conformations of the IM portions of each structure, we aligned the models for each structure based on LptF (Fig. 2f–h, bottom panels). The position of LPS is similar in LPS-bound LptB2FGCA and the LPS-bound full bridge; however, the TM helices of LptG shift from the more open state observed in the LPS-bound LptB2FGCA and the LPS-free full bridge to a more constricted state in the LPS-bound full bridge. The shift in the positions of TM helices 1–3 of LptG, which allows the formation of stabilizing interactions with the bound LPS, displaces the TM-LptC. This conformational change occurs only in the context of the full Lpt bridge (see Supplementary Fig. 7 for additional comparisons to published structures38). Because LptB2FGCADE is formed from purified Lpt subcomplexes without nucleotide added, we conclude that the TM-LptC can move independent of nucleotide binding only in the assembled, LPS-loaded Lpt bridge.
Previous work has shown that the TM-LptC regulates the ATP hydrolysis activity of IM Lpt complexes30,38. We tested the LPS transport activity of IM complexes with and without the TM-LptC at different ATP concentrations using our established biochemical transport assay that monitors LPS transport to LptA by site-specific photocrosslinking23 (Extended Data Fig. 7a). In this assay, pBPA is incorporated at position I36 in LptA and LPS cross-linking to LptA is monitored over time for IM complexes in proteoliposomes containing LPS, incubated in the presence of ATP and LptA. We found that LptB2FG(ΔTM-LptC) was able to transport LPS to LptA at lower concentrations of ATP, compared with complexes containing full-length LptC. This result suggests that IM complexes lacking the TM-LptC have a higher affinity for ATP than do wild-type IM complexes, consistent with previous findings that ΔTM-LptC suppresses defects of LptB variants that are deficient in ATP binding40.
We measured the ATP binding affinity for catalytically inactive (containing an LptB E163Q mutation) LptB(E163Q)2FG and LptB(E163Q)2FGC in the presence and absence of LPS using a scintillation proximity assay (SPA; Fig. 3a and Supplementary Figs. 8 and 9). LptB(E163Q)2FG binds more tightly to ATP (equilibrium dissociation constant (Kd) ≈ 20 μM) than does LptB(E163Q)2FGC (Kd ≈ 40 μM). In both cases, the measured binding affinities were unchanged by the presence of LPS. We also measured the ATP binding affinity for the partial bridge (LptB(E163Q)2FGCA) and the full bridge (LptB(E163Q)2FGCADE) in the presence and absence of LPS. We found that the ATP binding affinity of the partial bridge with or without LPS resembled that of LptB(E163Q)2FGC (Fig. 3b). For the full bridge, however, there was a difference in ATP binding affinity with and without LPS. In the presence of LPS, the ATP binding affinity matched that of LptB(E163Q)2FG (Kd ≈ 20 μM), whereas in the absence of LPS, the ATP binding affinity matched that of LptB(E163Q)2FGC (Kd ≈ 40 μM; Fig. 3b). A table summarizing the Kd values is shown in Fig. 3e. These results suggest that the presence of LPS in full Lpt bridges changes the ATP binding affinity by displacing the TM-LptC from its binding site.
a, Equilibrium binding affinity of ATP to LptB(E163Q)2FGC and LptB(E163Q)2FG measured in the presence and absence of LPS by SPA. Catalytically dead LptB(E163Q) was used to prevent ATP turnover. Data are mean ± s.d. from three independent experiments. b, Equilibrium binding affinity of ATP to LptB(E163Q)2FGCA and LptB(E163Q)2FGCADE measured in the presence or absence of added LPS by SPA. Catalytically dead LptB(E163Q) was used to prevent ATP turnover. Data are mean ± s.d. from three independent experiments. c, Composite density map of ATP-bound LptB(E163Q)2FGCADE consisting of the local refinements of LptADE and LptB(E163Q)2FGC, shown from a front view perpendicular to the micelle containing LptDE. The consensus map used for local refinement, low-pass-filtered to 10 Å, is shown as a transparent outline. d, Semi-transparent density map for the IM-focused local refinement of ATP-bound LptB(E163Q)2FGCADE, shown from front and top views with PDB 6MI8 (a model corresponding to vanadate-bound LptB2FGC) docked. e, Table summarizing the Kd values for the experiments presented in a,b. The mean of three independent experiments was analysed by a cooperative binding model yielding Kd. f, ATPase activity of LptB2FGCA in proteoliposomes without or with LPS, measured with increasing amounts of proteoliposomes containing LptDE added to the reactions. Experiments were performed in duplicate. The LptDE-alone sample contained an equivalent molar amount of LptDE in proteoliposomes to that of the sample with a 64-fold molar ratio of LptDE to LptB2FGCA. g, ATPase activity of LptB2FGCA in proteoliposomes without or with LPS, incubated with or without a 56-fold molar ratio of LptDE in proteoliposomes. Data are mean ± s.d. from three independent experiments.
Source data
To assess whether ATP binding to the catalytically dead LptB(E163Q)2FGCADE bridges leads to LptB dimerization and collapse of the LptFG lumen, we obtained a structure of the LptB(E163Q)2FGCADE bridge bound to ATP. We purified catalytically inactive LptB(E163Q)2FGCADE bridges following the same procedure we used to purify the LPS-bound LptB2FGCADE bridges and incubated them with ATP and MgCl2 before sample preparation for cryo-EM. Following a similar data-processing workflow to that used for the other full Lpt bridge datasets, we obtained an OM local refinement that resolved to 4.5 Å; however, heterogeneity of the IM region prevented us from obtaining a high-resolution local refinement for the same particle set (Extended Data Fig. 8). After 3D classification, we obtained a local refinement of the IM complex bound to ATP that resolved to 7.4 Å. The corresponding OM local refinement for the same particle subset resolved to 7.0 Å, and we constructed a composite map from these local refinements (Fig. 3c and Extended Data Fig. 8). At this resolution, α-helices are clearly resolved and the structure of the IM complex for this particle subset is in close agreement with previously determined structures of LptB2FGC bound to nucleotide38 (Fig. 3d). In this conformation, LptB is in a closed dimer, the TM helices of LptFG have rearranged to constrict the LptFG lumen and there is no resolved density for the TM-LptC. Thus, assembled Lpt bridges pass through the same nucleotide-bound intermediate state in the catalytic cycle as partial bridges do. We propose that the differences in ATP binding we observe for Lpt bridges with and without LPS reflect whether ATP binding is required to facilitate TM-LptC movement or not.
We observed one additional class for the IM portion of ATP-bound LptB(E163Q)2FGCADE; this resolved to 9.2 Å and exhibited ordered density for only one copy of LptB, which is bound to LptF (Extended Data Fig. 8). Previous data have suggested42 that LptB interacts differently with LptF than it does with LptG; however, understanding the relevance of the low-resolution subpopulation we observe will require further experiments.
A puzzling aspect of Lpt machine function has been that LptB2FGC, unlike typical ABC transporters, does not show an activation of ATPase activity in response to the presence of its substrate43. However, differences in ATPase activity have been observed between LptB2FG, LptB2FGC and LptB2FGC(ΔTM), with the presence of the TM-LptC resulting in decreased ATPase activity30,38. We tested the ATPase activity of proteoliposomes containing LptB2FGCA with and without LPS and incubated with increasing amounts of proteoliposomes containing LptDE. For samples that did not contain LPS, ATPase activity was similar regardless of the mole fraction of LptDE proteoliposomes (Fig. 3f). However, ATPase activity increased markedly as LptDE was added to LptB2FGCA proteoliposomes containing LPS (Fig. 3f). Compared with LptB2FGCA alone, the ATPase activity increased considerably when both LPS and a high mole fraction of LptDE were present (Fig. 3g). This increase in ATPase activity for Lpt bridges bound to LPS shows that activity turns on in response to the presence of substrate, but only when trans-envelope Lpt bridges are assembled.
To test the relationship between Lpt bridge assembly and TM-LptC localization in vivo, we introduced a change in LptA (LptA(A152R)) that prevents association with LptD and formation of trans-envelope Lpt complexes (Fig. 4a). A similar change in LptC (LptC(G153R)) disrupts association with LptA (refs. 35,44; Extended Data Fig. 7b). To test whether Lpt bridge formation affects the localization of the TM-LptC in vivo, we overexpressed either wild-type LptB2FGCA or LptB2FGCA(A152R) with pBPA incorporated at position I33 in TM1 of LptG, which is located in close proximity to the TM-LptC (Fig. 4b, top). We detected increased cross-link formation between LptG and LptC for the mutant that prevents the assembly of Lpt bridges, suggesting that dissociation of the TM-LptC depends on bridge assembly in vivo (Fig. 4b, bottom).
a, Top, close-up view of LPS-free LptB2FGCADE showing the interface between LptA (red) and LptD (blue), with the position of A152R indicated in yellow. Bottom, immunoblots detecting bridge components from copurifications of wild-type (WT) LptCA and LptCA(A152R) (eluate), with inputs shown on the left. b, Top, close-up view of LPS-free LptB2FGCA showing the position of LptG(I33pBPA) (yellow star). Bottom, LptB2FGCA and LptB2FGCA(A152R) complexes were subjected to UV cross-linking in vivo and adducts were detected by immunoblotting for LptG. LptG cross-links to LptCA only when bridges cannot form. c, Photocrosslinking experiments were performed in vivo for LptB2FGCA and LptB2FGCA(A152R) with or without the TM-LptC. pBPA was incorporated at LptA L116 in the LptCA fusion. LPS and LptCA were detected by immunoblotting with anti-LPS and anti-His antibodies. Dissociation of the TM-LptC is required for LPS transport to LptA in vivo. d, Serial dilutions of E. coli strains expressing wild-type LptE or LptE(K136D) and with or without the TM-LptC were spotted on MacConkey agar at the indicated dilutions. e, The LptE K136D mutation does not prevent the formation of Lpt bridges. Formation of LptB2FGCADE complexes was assessed by pulling down His-tagged wild-type LptB2FGCA in a background of wild-type or mutant LptE and immunoblotting with antibodies against LptD, LptE and LptC–His. f, The LptE K136D mutation increases cross-link formation between the TM-LptC and LptG. LptB2FGCA with pBPA at LptG I33 was expressed in a background of wild-type or mutant LptE and photocrosslinked in vivo. Adducts were detected by blotting for LptC–His. g, Deleting the TM-LptC restores LPS transport in a LptE(K136D) background. LptB2FGC and LptB2FGC(ΔTM) were expressed with LptA(L116pBPA) in a background of either wild-type LptE or LptE(K136D) and photocrosslinked in vivo. Cross-linking was detected by immunoblotting for LPS and LptC–His. All data are representative of three independent experiments. Blots against different antibodies represent identical samples processed in parallel.
To test the effect of bridge assembly and TM-LptC delocalization on LPS transport in vivo, we incorporated pBPA at position L116 in LptA and assayed cross-linking of LPS at this position for wild-type and LptA(A152R) complexes with and without the TM-LptC. For the wild-type complexes that are competent for bridge formation, we observed similar levels of LPS cross-linking in the presence and absence of the TM-LptC (Fig. 4c). However, when bridge formation was prevented, we observed cross-links to LPS only when the TM-LptC was absent, indicating that delocalization of the TM-LptC is required for LPS transport to occur in vivo (Fig. 4c). We obtained similar results using the LptC(G153R) mutant and assaying LPS transport by monitoring cross-linking to LptC(T47) (Extended Data Fig. 7c,d). Together, our results suggest that the TM-LptC has an important role in regulating LPS transport. Although the TM-LptC is not strictly essential, the fact that full-length LptC is broadly conserved implies a strong selection for the retention of the TM-LptC (refs. 35,40,45).
Because the LPS transporter increases activity in response to LPS bound in the TM cavity of the LptB2FGC complex in assembled bridges, we wondered whether the TM-LptC can also respond to the build-up of LPS at the OM translocon LptDE to turn off transport once the outer leaflet is full. We have previously shown that a variant of LptE, LptE(K136D), has reduced binding affinity for LPS, resulting in OM permeability46. Furthermore, in vitro LPS transport assays have shown that less LPS is transported by LptDE complexes containing LptE(K136D) and that ATP hydrolysis for such transport also decreases25,47. To test the role of the TM-LptC in the LPS transport defects observed for LptE(K136D), we tested the growth of LptE(K136D), LptC(ΔTM) double-mutant bacteria and found that growth on MacConkey agar was restored for the LptE(K136D) variant when the TM-LptC was deleted (Fig. 4d). To characterize the mechanistic basis of the growth rescue, we characterized bridge assembly and localization of the TM-LptC in the LptE(K136D) background. We found that LptE(K136D) does not prevent the assembly of Lpt bridges, but that cross-linking of the TM-LptC to LptG increases, implying increased TM-LptC localization proximal to LptG (Fig. 4e,f). Consistent with these results, we found that LPS cross-linking to LptA in the LptE(K136D) background with full-length LptC is decreased and that deletion of the TM-LptC restores LPS transport, explaining the growth rescue and suggesting that the TM-LptC also modulates transport activity in response to changes at the OM translocon (Fig. 4g).
We have previously proposed a ‘PEZ’ model for LPS transport, in which each LPS molecule extracted from the IM by LptB2FGC pushes the previous LPS along the length of the Lpt bridge to the OM, similar to a PEZ sweet dispenser8,23. As we were unable to structurally characterize the Lpt bridge with LPS bound in the periplasmic region (see above), we sought biochemical support for the PEZ model of transport. The PEZ model suggests that it should be possible to capture simultaneous LPS cross-linking at multiple positions in the bridge. We therefore incorporated pBPA at sites in LptC and LptA in the same Lpt complex (LptB2FGC(T47pBPA)A(L116pBPA)) and assessed LPS cross-linking in vitro in the presence of excess LPS to maximize the chances of capturing a double cross-link. We used a variant of the LptCA fusion with a thrombin cleavage site incorporated into the linker to allow detection of the number of LPS molecules cross-linked to LptCA (uncleaved) and analysis of the relative ratios of LPS cross-linking to LptC versus LptA (after thrombin cleavage; Fig. 5a). We observed two LPS-reactive bands corresponding to LptCA cross-linked to one or two LPS molecules before thrombin cleavage, and bands corresponding to LPS cross-linked to either LptC or LptA after thrombin cleavage (Fig. 5b). Analysis of the cross-linking to LptC and LptA after thrombin cleavage shows that cross-links accumulate at LptC and LptA at approximately equal ratios over time, as would be predicted by the PEZ model. In fact, we could also observe multiple LPS cross-links to LptB2FGC(T47pBPA)A(L116pBPA) in vivo (Fig. 5c). This supports the hypothesis that in vivo LPS transport occurs by the PEZ model, which would allow the transport of more LPS per unit time.
a, Schematic for the detection of multiple LPS cross-links. LptB2FGCA complexes were substituted at LptC T47 and LptA L116 with pBPA (orange stars). A thrombin cleavage site (grey) added in the linker between LptC and LptA allows analysis of cross-linking to individual proteins after digestion of the linker. b, Simultaneous in vitro photocrosslinking of LPS to LptC and LptA. Purified LptB2FGC(T47pBPA)A(L116pBPA) was incubated with LPS, and transport was initiated by adding ATP. Samples were removed at the indicated time points and exposed to UV light, and cross-linking was analysed before and after thrombin cleavage by western blotting with an anti-LPS antibody using anti-His and anti-LptC as loading controls. CA, LptC(T47pBPA)A(L116pBPA) fusion before thrombin cleavage; A, cLptA(L116pBPA) after thrombin cleavage; C, LptC(T47pBPA) after thrombin cleavage. c, Simultaneous in vivo photocrosslinking of LPS to LptC and LptA. Proteins substituted at both LptC T47 and LptA L116 with pBPA were overexpressed in E. coli. Samples were taken during mid-log phase and exposed to UV light, and cross-linking was analysed both before and after thrombin cleavage by western blotting with an anti-LPS antibody. All western blot data are representative of three independent experiments performed with similar results. Blots against different antibodies represent identical samples processed in parallel.
OM biogenesis requires the movement of millions of LPS molecules to the cell surface each division cycle48. Transport of LPS to the outer leaflet of the OM through an ATP-powered trans-envelope protein bridge is essential to meet the high demand for efficient trafficking. However, bridge assembly must be coordinated to ensure that LPS does not accumulate in the outer leaflet of the IM or the inner leaflet of the OM, because mislocalization of LPS is toxic49,50. Previous studies have shown that the TM-LptC must move to allow the ATP-dependent dimerization of LptB monomers, which is necessary for LPS transport38,39,40,41. We have now shown that dissociation of the TM-LptC occurs when assembled Lpt bridges are bound to LPS. In this ‘primed’ state (Fig. 6), the TM helices of LptFG, particularly TM1–TM3 of LptG, constrict to maximize contacts with LPS, displacing the TM-LptC and leading to increased ATP binding affinity and ATP hydrolysis activity. Movement of the TM-LptC is required for LPS transport to occur in vivo and provides a link between Lpt transport activity and the presence of LPS in the IM. Furthermore, we show that movement of the TM-LptC mediates changes in transport activity in response to the LptE(K136D) mutation in the OM portion of the transporter, which has lower affinity for LPS. Bridges containing this mutation cannot efficiently insert LPS into the outer leaflet, and transport less LPS because the TM-LptC does not remain in the primed (stably dissociated) state. Previous experiments have shown that reducing the levels of LPS in vivo reduces the fraction of Lpt bridges in a cell28. We propose a model in which the LPS transporter responds to the presence of LPS at either the IM or the OM, turning transport up or down by moving the TM-LptC.
Model for the mechanism of regulation of membrane-to-membrane LPS transport.
Coupling Lpt bridge assembly and activity to the presence of LPS in the IM provides a mechanism to increase LPS transport and thus prevent the accumulation of LPS in the IM. In E. coli, there is another mechanism to deal with high concentrations of LPS at the IM that involves degradation of LpxC, the rate-limiting enzyme in the LPS biosynthesis pathway, by the YejM–FtsH protease pair10,12,51,52. This mechanism ensures that toxic levels of LPS do not accumulate, but it does so by inhibiting LPS biosynthesis, representing an emergency negative-feedback mechanism. The regulation we describe allows modulation of LPS transport activity in response to LPS levels at the IM and OM and represents a homeostatic mechanism to match the transport of LPS to its cellular abundance. Two other non-essential proteins in the Lpt pathway have recently been identified (LptY and LptM; formerly known as YedD and YifL, respectively)53,54,55,56,57. Whether these proteins also have a role in modulating flux through the Lpt transport pathway remains to be seen.
Our model for the mechanism of LPS transport, informed by the structures of the Lpt bridge and our biochemical experiments, both explains how LPS transport is regulated by the transport machinery, and also changes our understanding of the predominant conformational state of the transporter during active transport in vivo. We now know that during active LPS transport, the TM-LptC is predominantly delocalized, with LPS bound in the TM cavity of LptFG. The LPS transport machinery has attracted considerable attention as a target for therapeutics to treat antibiotic-resistant Gram-negative infections9. One compound that targets the Lpt machinery is zosurabalpin58,59 (currently in phase III clinical development), which binds to LPS in LptB2FG. Its binding site overlaps with the position of the TM-LptC when it is localized, suggesting that zosurabalpin specifically targets the state of the Lpt transporter with the TM-LptC dissociated; that is, the active state of the Lpt bridge. That zosurabalpin binds to and inhibits the active state of the Lpt transporter helps explain its efficacy. The structures reported here provide a roadmap to understanding how the Lpt bridge regulates LPS transport, and how to design inhibitors of this process.

To prepare a lipid stock solution, E. coli polar lipid extract (Avanti Polar Lipids) was dissolved in water and sonicated for 30 min to make a 30 mg ml−1 aqueous suspension stock. The lipid stock was flash-frozen in liquid nitrogen and stored at −80 °C. To prepare a 2 mg ml−1 aqueous suspension of LPS, purified LPS extracted from E. coli EH100 (Ra mutant; Sigma) was dissolved in water and sonicated for 30 min. The LPS stock solution was flash-frozen in liquid nitrogen and stored in aliquots at −80 °C. To prepare the fluorescent lipid stock solutions for flow cytometry, E. coli polar lipid extract (Avanti Polar Lipids) was dissolved in chloroform with 1% (molar ratio) ATTO-488 DPPE, ATTO-565 DPPE or ATTO-647N DPPE (ATTO-TEC). Lipid mixtures were dried into a film, hydrated and lyophilized overnight. The lipid mixtures were dissolved in water and sonicated for 30 min to make a 30 mg ml−1 aqueous suspension stock. The lipid stock solution was flash-frozen in liquid nitrogen and stored at −80 °C.
SDS–PAGE was performed using homemade Tris-HCl 4-20% polyacrylamide gradient gels with Tris-glycine running buffer. Samples were mixed at a 1:1 ratio with 2× SDS sample loading buffer (100 mM Tris pH 6.8, 4% SDS, 20% glycerol, 0.05% bromophenol blue and 2% β-mercaptoethanol). SDS–PAGE gels were run for 45–60 min at 200 V. SDS–PAGE gels were stained with Coomassie Brilliant Blue (Alfa Aesar) and imaged using the ‘Gel’ feature of an Azure c400 imager (Azure Biosystems). For immunoblotting, proteins were transferred onto Immun-Blot PVDF membranes (Bio-Rad), incubated with sterile-filtered casein blocking buffer (Sigma Aldrich) for 1 h and then incubated with the appropriate antibodies. The following primary antibodies were used: anti-LPS core mouse monoclonal antibody (Hycult Biotechnology, HM6011, clone WN1 222-5, 1:5,000–1:20,000 dilution); anti-His-HRP (QIAGEN, 1:5,000 dilution); mouse anti-His HRP conjugate (BioLegend, 652504, 1:10,000 dilution); rabbit anti-LptD antiserum (provided by S. Matsuyama, 1:10,000 dilution); rabbit anti-LptA antiserum21; rabbit anti-LptC antiserum31; and rabbit anti-LptB antiserum26. The anti-LptA, anti-LptB and anti-LptC antisera were prepared by inoculating rabbits with purified protein prepared in the laboratory of D.K. The following secondary antibodies were used: donkey anti-rabbit HRP conjugate (GE Amersham, NA934-1ML, 1:10,000 dilution) and sheep anti-mouse HRP conjugate (GE Amersham, LNA931V/AH, 1:10,000 dilution). Bands were visualized using ECL Prime Western Blotting Detection Reagent (GE Amersham) and imaged using an Azure c400 (Azure Biosystems). Uncropped immunoblots are available in Supplementary Fig. 10.
C43(DE3) cells were transformed with pCDFDuetLptB2FG and pBAD-HisA-LptC-link-LptA-thr-His7. Overexpression and purification of the LptB2FGCA complexes were done similarly to the previously reported protocol for purification of LptB2FGC with some modifications30. Cultures were grown at 37 °C after diluting overnight cultures 1:100 into fresh LB broth supplemented with 50 μg ml−1 spectinomycin (Sigma) and 50 μg ml−1 carbenicillin (Teknova). Expression was induced with 300 μM isopropyl-β-d-1-thiogalactopyranoside (IPTG; Sigma) and 0.02% l-arabinose at an optical density at 600 nm (OD600 nm) of about 1, and cultures were grown for an additional 16–18 h at 18 °C. Cells were collected by centrifugation at 4,200g for 20 min and resuspended in 20 mM Tris-HCl pH 8.0, 150 mM NaCl supplemented with 1 mM phenylmethanesulfonyl fluoride (PMSF, Sigma), 100 μg ml−1 lysozyme (Sigma) and 100 μg ml−1 DNase I (Sigma). Cells were passed through an EmulsiFlex-C5 high-pressure cell disruptor three times. Cell lysate was centrifuged at 10,000g for 10 min to remove unbroken cells. Membranes were isolated by centrifugation at 100,000g for 1 h. Membranes were resuspended and solubilized in 20 mM Tris-HCl pH 8.0, 150 mM NaCl, 10% glycerol and 1% n-dodecyl-β-d-maltopyranoside (DDM, Anatrace) at 4 °C for 2 h, followed by centrifugation at 100,000g for 30 min. The supernatant was applied to Ni-NTA Superflow resin (QIAGEN) and the buffer was exchanged during the Ni-NTA purification to 20 mM Tris-HCl pH 8.0, 150 mM NaCl 10% glycerol, 0.01% DDM and 0.04% glycol-diosgenin (GDN). The protein was eluted with 20 mM Tris-HCl pH 8.0, 150 mM NaCl, 10% glycerol, 0.01% DDM, 0.04% GDN and 200 mM imidazole. The eluate was concentrated with an Amicon centrifugation filter with a 100-kDa molecular weight cut-off (MWCO; Amicon Ultra; Millipore), and subjected to SEC on a Superdex 200 increase 10/300 GL or a Superose 6 increase 10/300 GL column (GE Healthcare) in in 20 mM Tris-HCl pH 8.0, 150 mM NaCl, 1% glycerol, 1% dimethyl sulfoxide (DMSO), 1% ethylene glycol and 0.02% GDN. Fractions were analysed by SDS–PAGE and selected fractions were used for the in vitro assembly of LptB2FGCADE in detergent (described below). For the structure of LptB2FGCA bound to LPS, the eluate after Ni-NTA affinity purification was incubated with a fivefold molar excess (final concentration approximately 160 μM) of LPS from E. coli EH100 dissolved in water (Ra mutant; Sigma) while sonicating for 15 min in ice water, and then incubated for a further 1 h on ice before purification by SEC. Fractions from SEC were pooled and concentrated to 7–8 mg ml−1 using a 100-kDa-MWCO Amicon filter and the protein was prepared for microscopy as described below.
Purification of LptB2FGC(T47pBPA)A was done as above with the following modifications. Protein was expressed from C43(DE3) cells transformed with pCDFDuetLptB2FG, pSup-BpaRS-6TRN and pBAD-HisA-LptCT47pBPA-link-LptA-thr-His7. Cultures were grown at 37 °C after diluting overnight cultures 1:100 into fresh LB broth supplemented with 50 μg ml−1 spectinomycin (Sigma), 50 μg ml−1 carbenicillin (Teknova), 30 μg ml−1 chloramphenicol (Millipore Sigma) and 0.5 mM pBPA (Bachem). Expression was induced with 300 μM IPTG (Sigma) and 0.02% l-arabinose at OD600 nm ≈ 0.6, and cultures were grown for an additional 3 h at 37 °C. Cells were collected by centrifugation at 4,200g for 20 min and resuspended in 20 mM Tris-HCl pH 8.0 and 150 mM NaCl. To obtain LPS–LptC cross-links, the collected cells were resuspended in 500 ml of buffer consisting of 20 mM Tris pH 8.0 and 150 mM NaCl and exposed to UV light for 20 min on ice with mixing. Cells were collected by centrifugation at 4,200g for 20 min and resuspended in 20 mM Tris-HCl pH 8.0, 150 mM NaCl and 2 mM MgCl2 supplemented with 1 mM PMSF (Sigma), 100 μg ml−1 lysozyme (Sigma) and 100 μg ml−1 DNase I (Sigma) as above. The rest of the purification was performed as described for wild-type LptB2FGCA.
Purification of LptB(E163Q)2FGCA was done as described above with the following modifications. Protein was expressed from C43(DE3) cells transformed with pCDFDuetLptBE163Q2FG and pBAD-HisA-LptC-link-LptA-thr-His7. During the membrane solubilization step, 2 mM ATP and 5 mM MgCl2 were added to the solubilization buffer. The rest of the purification was performed as described for wild-type LptB2FGCA.
C43(DE3) cells were transformed with pCOLADuet-LptE-His6-LptDY63Am and pSup-BpaRS-6TRN plasmids. Overexpression and purification of the LptDE complexes was done similarly to the previously reported protocol for purification of LptDE with some modifications22. Cultures were grown at 37 °C after diluting overnight cultures 1:100 into fresh LB broth supplemented with 50 μg ml−1 kanamycin (Millipore Sigma), 30 μg ml−1 chloramphenicol (Millipore Sigma) and 0.5 mM pBPA (Bachem). Expression was induced with 500 μM IPTG (Sigma) at OD600 nm ≈ 1, and cultures were grown for an additional 16–18 h at 26 °C. Cells were collected by centrifugation at 4,200g for 20 min and resuspended in 20 mM Tris-HCl pH 8.0, 150 mM NaCl and 2 mM MgCl2 supplemented with 1 mM PMSF (Sigma), 100 μg ml−1 lysozyme (Sigma), and 100 μg ml−1 DNase I (Sigma). Resuspended cells were passed through an EmulsiFlex-C5 high-pressure cell disruptor three times. The cell lysate was centrifuged at 10,000g for 10 min to remove unbroken cells. Membranes were isolated by centrifugation at 100,000g for 1 h. The membrane pellet was resuspended and solubilized in 20 mM Tris-HCl pH 8.0, 150 mM NaCl, 1.0% Anzergent 3-14 at 4 °C for 3 h and then ultracentrifuged as above. The supernatant was applied to Ni-NTA Superflow resin (QIAGEN) and the buffer was exchanged during the Ni-NTA purification first to 20 mM Tris-HCl pH 8.0, 150 mM NaCl 10% glycerol and 0.05% DDM and then to 20 mM Tris-HCl pH 8.0, 150 mM NaCl, 10% glycerol, 0.01% DDM and 0.04% GDN. The protein was eluted with 20 mM Tris-HCl pH 8.0, 150 mM NaCl, 10% glycerol, 0.01% DDM, 0.04% GDN and 200 mM imidazole. The eluate was concentrated with an Amicon centrifugation filter with a 100-kDa MWCO (Amicon Ultra; Millipore), and then subjected to SEC on a Superdex 200 increase 10/300 GL or a Superose 6 increase 10/300 GL column (GE Healthcare) in 20 mM Tris-HCl pH 8.0, 150 mM NaCl, 1% glycerol, 1% DMSO, 1% ethylene glycol and 0.02% GDN. Fractions were analysed by SDS–PAGE to assess the purification, and selected fractions were used for in vitro assembly of LptB2FGCADE in detergent.
To assemble stabilized LptB2FGCADE complexes in detergent, approximately 2 mg of LptD(Y63pBPA)E at a concentration of 1 mg ml−1 was mixed at a 1:1.1 molar ratio with approximately 3 mg of LptB2FGCA and incubated on ice for 15 min. The samples were exposed to UV light for 10 min on ice, mixing every 2 min. The cross-linked mixture was concentrated with an Amicon centrifugation filter, 100-kDa MWCO (Amicon Ultra; Millipore), and then subjected to SEC on a Superose 6 increase 10/300 GL column (GE Healthcare) in 20 mM Tris-HCl pH 8.0, 150 mM NaCl, 1% glycerol, 1% DMSO, 1% ethylene glycol and 0.02% GDN. Fractions were analysed by SDS–PAGE to assess the purification, and selected fractions were used for preparation of grids for cryo-electron microscopy. For preparation of the LPS-bound LptB2FGCADE complex (LPS-bound LptB2FGCADE; Fig. 1 and Extended Data Fig. 2), LptB2FGCA and LptD(Y63pBPA)E were used. For preparation of the LptB2FGCADE complex without LPS (LPS-free LptB2FGCADE; Fig. 2 and Extended Data Fig. 4), LptB2FGC(T47pBPA)A and LptD(Y63pBPA)E were used. For preparation of the low-resolution LptB2FGCADE complex without LPS (Extended Data Fig. 5), LptB2FGCA and LptD(Y63pBPA)E were used. For preparation of the ATP-bound LptB(E163Q)2FGCADE complex (ATP-bound LptB(E163Q)2FGCADE; Fig. 3 and Extended Data Fig. 8), LptB(E163Q)2FGCA and LptD(Y63pBPA)E were used.
Proteins were purified as described above. All samples were prepared by plunge-freezing in liquid-nitrogen-cooled liquid ethane using a Thermo Fisher Scientific Vitrobot Mark IV. For the LPS-bound and LPS-free structures of LptB2FGCADE, purified protein (4 μl) was applied to glow-discharged C-flat 20 nm holey carbon 1.2-μm-hole-diameter, 1.3-μm-hole-spacing, 400-mesh copper grids (Protochips) and blotted for 7 s at 4 °C and 100% humidity with a wait time of 0, 15 or 30 s and the blot force set to 12. For the ATP-bound structure of LptB(E163Q)2FGCADE, purified protein was incubated on ice with 5 mM ATP and 5 mM MgCl2 for 45 min with gentle agitation. For this sample, 0.65 mM fluorinated fos-choline-8 (Anatrace) was added immediately before plunge-freezing using the conditions described above. Sample (4 μl) was applied to glow-discharged C-flat 20 nm holey carbon 1.2-μm-hole-diameter, 1.3-μm-hole-spacing, 400-mesh copper grids (Protochips) and blotted for 6.5 s at 4 °C and 100% humidity with the blot force set to 12.
For the LPS-bound and high-resolution LPS-free structures of LptB2FGCADE along with the LPS-bound structure of LptB2FGCA, image stacks were recorded using a Titan Krios G3i electron cryo-microscope (Thermo Fisher Scientific) operated at a 300-kV accelerating voltage. Image stacks (movies) were recorded on a Gatan BioQuantum K3 Imaging Filter (Gatan) using the super-resolution counting mode and a calibrated magnification of 105,000×, for a pixel size of 0.825 Å after 2× binning (for the LPS-free bridge structure) and 0.830 Å after 2× binning (for the LPS-bound LptB2FGCA structure), using SerialEM v.4.0.562. For our LPS-bound LptB2FGCADE structure, movies were recorded using counted double-subtracted super-resolution mode with a pixel size of 0.415 Å. The slit of the energy filter was set to 20 eV with a defocus range between 1.1 and 2.2 μm.
For the low-resolution structure of LPS-free LptB2FGCADE, image stacks were recorded using a Talos Artica electron cryo-microscope (Thermo Fisher Scientific) operated at 200 kV accelerating voltage. Image stacks (movies) were recorded on a Gatan BioQuantum K3 Imaging Filter (Gatan) using the super-resolution counting mode and a calibrated magnification of 36,000×, for a pixel size of 1.1 Å after 2× binning using SerialEM v.4.0.562. The defocus range was between 1.2 and 2.6 μm. The subframe time was set to allow the collection of 50 subframes per image stack, with an electron dose rate of around 1–1.278 e− per Å2 per frame. The total electron dose was 50–64 e− per Å2. The multi-shot scheme in SerialEM was used for data collection, with settings of nine holes per stage move and two shots per hole.
For the ATP-bound structure of LptB(E163Q)2FGCADE, image stacks were recorded using a Titan Krios G3i electron cryo-microscope. Image stacks (movies) were recorded on a Falcon 4i direct electron detector with a Selectris energy filter (slit width 10 eV) using the super-resolution counting mode and a calibrated magnification of 165,000×, for a pixel size of 0.74 Å after 2× binning using EPU v.3.6. The slit of the energy filter was set to 10 eV with a defocus range between 1.1 and 3.0 μm. The electron dose rate was 0.6237 e− per Å2 per frame. The total electron dose was 51.147 e− per Å2.
The movie frames were motion-corrected and dose-weighted and the contrast transfer function (CTF) parameters were estimated using CryoSPARC Live and the patch CTF estimation functionality in cryoSPARC63. For counted double-subtracted super-resolution images, micrographs were 2× binned during motion correction. For the LptB2FGCA structure, particle picking was done using the cryoSPARC blob picker. For LptB2FGCADE structures, particle picking was done using a self-trained Topaz model64. The model was trained iteratively using a combination of manual picks, picks selected by the previous best Topaz model and a curated set of particle picks that was generated using template-based picking. The templates used for the template-based picking were generated using manual picks, and the template-based picks used for Topaz training were curated by 2D and 3D classification followed by shift-based recentring. The best model had the expected number of particles per micrograph set to 150, although the training dataset ultimately had closer to 50 good particles per micrograph. The best model had the ResNet8 architecture, was trained with batch normalization turned off and trained without denoising the micrographs. In total, three custom models were used: one for the low-resolution LptB2FGCADE structure, one for the ATP-bound LptB2(E163Q)FGCADE structure and one shared for the LPS-free and LPS-bound LptB2FGCADE structures.
Junk particles were filtered out by successive rounds of 2D classification in cryoSPARC. Initial models were generated using the ab initio reconstruction in cryoSPARC and particles were filtered by successive rounds of heterogeneous refinement. After an initial non-uniform refinement job, particles were subjected to local refinement. If applicable, the particle shifts calculated during local refinement were used to recentre the particles, the particles were re-extracted in a smaller box size to focus on only one half of the bridge and new initial models were generated using ab initio reconstruction. For the ATP-bound structure, the globally refined bridge was manually recentred to focus on the IM using cryoSPARC before particle re-extraction using a smaller IM-focused box size. Particles were further filtered by heterogenous refinement, after which they were subjected to non-uniform refinement, local CTF refinement, global CTF refinement (fit for beam tilt, beam trefoil and spherical aberration), local refinement with masks focusing on the relevant parts of the complex and reference-based motion correction. Note that, for the ATP-bound structure, 3D classification focused on LptB2 was also used to generate initial volumes for heterogeneous classification. For all bridge structures, particle sets tools in cryoSPARC were used after focused classification before final map construction to ensure that all final maps came from the same particle sets. Masks were made either using the Segger tool in UCSF Chimera or using the molmap feature in UCSF Chimera65,66,67. For all structures, particle heterogeneity was analysed using both cryoDRGN and 3D variability analysis in cryoSPARC. Classification and/or masking in RELION and cryoDRGN did not yield improvements for any of our other structures68,69,70. Maps used for figures were filtered according to local resolution with B-factor sharpening in cryoSPARC; map sharpening using DeepEMhancer71 was also tested without yielding improvements. Structural biology applications used in this project were compiled and configured by SBGrid72.
Models were built using the relevant local refinements and then combined by rigidly fitting into the global map. The initial model of LptDE was generated using ModelAngelo73. The initial model of LptA was generated by docking in the crystal structure of LptA (Protein Data Bank (PDB) 2R1A)18, and the initial model of the LptC periplasmic domain was generated using SwissModel74. LPS coordinates from the PDB (ligand code JSG) were used to generate restraints for the ligand with the PRODRG server75. The published model of LPS-containing LptB2FG (PDB 6S8H)39 was used as the initial model for the IM portion of the LPS-containing bridge, and SwissModel was used to generate the initial model of LptB2FGC for the LPS-free bridge. The initial models were all docked into the relevant maps. The coordinates were then refined using PHENIX76,77. The model was further optimized using ISOLDE78 accessed through ChimeraX79,80,81. Manual model building was done in Coot82. The LPS molecule in the LptB2FGCA structure was first manually fitted into the map and then real-space-refined in Coot. LPS atoms lacking clear density were manually deleted. The final models were visually inspected for general fit to the map, and further inspected using MolProbity, the residue-wise local quality estimation DAQ and CheckMySequence83,84,85. All residues in our models had DAQCα scores greater than 0, except for LptC residues 44–62 for the LptB2FGCADE–LPS structure (DAQCα > −0.5) and LptB residues 237–240 for the LPS-free LptB2FGCADE structure (DAQCα > −0.2). There were no reported errors by CheckMySequence. The model validation statistics are summarized in Extended Data Tables 1 and 2.
In the high-resolution LPS-free and LPS-bound structures of LptB2FGCADE, the periplasmic portions of LptFG (LptF residues 137–246, LptG residues 142–244 for the LPS-bound structure; LptF residues 139–243 and LptG residues 143–243 for the LPS-free structure) have low local resolution because of flexibility, and in the structure of LptB2FGCA the periplasmic portions of LptFGCA (LptF residues 146–236, LptG residues 150–236, LptC residues 38–183, all of LptA), also have low local resolution because of flexibility. For this reason, we did not build the model in these regions for PDB deposition. For visualization, the atomic models for the periplasmic portions of LptFG (LptF residues 146–236, LptG residues 150–236) in the two structures of LptB2FGCADE were generated by docking previously determined structures into our map (PDB 6MHU)38 without further refinement. For the low-resolution regions of LptB2FGCA (LptF residues 146–236, LptG residues 150–236, LptC residues 38–183, all of LptA), atomic models for visualization were generated by docking the relevant portion of the LPS-free LptB2FGCADE structure without further refinement.
The low-resolution LPS-free structure of LptB2FGCADE is too low resolution for atomic model building. For visualization, we rigid-body-fitted the structures of LptB2FGC and LptDE generated using our high-resolution LPS-free dataset into the map without further refinement. Similarly, the ATP-bound structure of LptB2FGCADE is too low resolution for atomic model building. For visualization, we rigid-body-fitted PDB 6MI8 (a model corresponding to vanadate-bound LptB2FGC) and the structure of LptADE generated from our LPS-bound LptB2FGCADE dataset into the map without further refinement. We did not deposit a model for these two structures to the PDB because of these resolution constraints.
Calculation of the internal volume of the periplasmic domains of the LPS-bound and LPS-free structures of LptB2FGCADE was done using pyKVFinder (v.0.9.2) accessed through ChimeraX (1.11.1)37,79,80,81.
The LptB2FG, LptB2FGC and LptB2FGCA complexes used for reconstitution into proteoliposomes were purified as described for cryo-electron microscopy with the following modifications. The affinity buffer was 20 mM Tris-HCl pH 7.4, 150 mM NaCl, 15% glycerol and 0.05% DDM. The SEC buffer was 20 mM Tris-HCl pH 7.4, 150 mM NaCl, 5% glycerol and 0.05% DDM. After SEC, fractions were analysed by SDS–PAGE to assess the purification and selected fractions were pooled, concentrated to approximately 5 mg ml−1, flash-frozen and stored at –80 °C until use.
LptB2FG and LptB2FGC used for LPS release in detergent was purified as described for LptB2FGCA for cryo-electron microscopy with the following modifications. Carbenicillin (50 μg ml−1) was not added to the growth medium for expression of LptB2FG. Expression was induced with 200 μM IPTG. Expression of LptB2FG did not require the addition of 0.02% arabinose. The affinity buffer was 20 mM Tris-HCl pH 7.4, 300 mM NaCl, 15% (vol/vol) glycerol, 0.01% (wt/vol) DDM, and 0.04% (wt/vol) GDN. The SEC buffer was 20 mM Tris-HCl pH 7.4, 150 mM NaCl, 5% (vol/vol) glycerol and 0.02% GDN. After SEC, fractions were analysed by SDS–PAGE to assess the purification and selected fractions were pooled, concentrated to approximately 5 mg ml−1, flash-frozen and stored at –80 °C until use.
LptB2FGC(T47pBPA)A(L116pBPA) used for LPS release in detergent was purified as described for LptB2FGC(T47pBPA)A for cryo-electron microscopy with the modifications listed above and the following. Protein was expressed from BL21(DE3) cells transformed with pCDFDuetLptB2FG, pSup-BpaRS-6TRN and pBAD-HisA-LptCT47pBPA-thr-link-LptAL116pBPA-His7. Cultures were grown with 0.8 mM pBPA. After induction, cultures were grown for 16–18 h at 18 °C before cells were collected. Collected cells were not exposed to UV light before protein purification.
IM complexes used for SPA experiments were purified as described above with the following modifications. All complexes used for SPA had an E163Q mutation in LptB to trap the ATP-bound state. LptB(E163Q)2FG and LptB(E163Q)2FGC were expressed with an N-terminal His6 tag on LptB to allow interaction with copper-coated PVT SPA beads (Perkin Elmer, Revvity). LptB(E163Q)2FGCA used to generate bridges was purified using a C-terminal His7 tag on the LptCA fusion, as for structural samples. After Ni-NTA affinity purification, eluate was concentrated to approximately 5 mg ml−1 and split between the samples with and without LPS. The +LPS samples were incubated with a fivefold molar excess (final concentration approximately 160 μM) of LPS from E. coli EH100 dissolved in water (Ra mutant; Sigma) while sonicating for 15 min in ice water, and then incubated for a further 1 h on ice before purification by SEC. The −LPS samples were also purified by SEC. Cross-linked LptB(E163Q)2FGCADE bridges with or without LPS were prepared by incubating the prepared LptB(E163Q)2FGCA +LPS or −LPS samples with LptD(Y63pBPA)E in the presence of UV light and purifying by SEC. For all samples, fractions were pooled after SEC, concentrated to approximately 5 mg ml−1, flash-frozen and stored at –80 °C until use. Purification of the corresponding proteins from Acinetobacter baylyi was done as reported previously58, with the exception that the mutant that does not produce LPS86 was used in place of the wild type and E. coli proteins were expressed instead of Acinetobacter ones. The amount of LPS bound to each sample was assessed by immunoblotting using an anti-LPS antibody (Hycult Biotechnology, HM6011, clone WN1 222-5, 1:5,000–1:20,000 dilution), with anti-LptB as a loading control26.
Overexpression and purification of LptDE wild-type and truncated complexes were done as previously reported22,87 for the wild-type LptDE with minor modifications. In brief, an overnight culture containing C43(DE3) cells transformed with pCOLADuet-LptE-His6LptD was diluted 1:100 into 1.5-l flasks containing LB supplemented with 0.2% glucose and 30 µg ml−1 kanamycin. Cultures were grown at 37 °C until an OD600 nm of around 0.6–0.8 and induced with 500 µM IPTG (Teknova) for 3–4 h at 37 °C. Cells were collected at 4,200g for 10 min and resuspended in 20 mM Tris-HCl pH 8.0, 150 mM NaCl supplemented with a cOmplete protease tablet (Roche), 100 µg ml−1 lysozyme (Sigma) and 100 µg ml−1 DNase I (Sigma). The resuspended cells were passed through an EmulsiFlex-C3 high-pressure cell disruptor twice and centrifuged at 15,000g for 20 min to remove unbroken cells. The supernatant was centrifuged at 100,000g for 30 min to isolate membranes. The membranes were solubilized in 20 mM Tris-HCl pH 8.0, 150 mM NaCl and 0.5% n-lauroylsarcosine sodium salt (Sigma) supplemented with a cOmplete protease inhibitor tablet (Roche) at 4 °C for 2 h and centrifuged for 30 min at 100,000g. The membrane pellet was resuspended in 20 mM Tris-HCl pH 8.0 and 150 mM NaCl and centrifuged for 30 min at 100,000g to remove the remaining n-lauroylsarcosine sodium salt. The washed pellet was solubilized in 20 mM Tris-HCl pH 8.0, 300 mM NaCl, 20 mM imidazole and 1.0% Anzergent 3-14 (Anatrace) at 4 °C for 2 h and then centrifuged at 100,000g for 1 h. The supernatant was applied to Ni-NTA Superflow (QIAGEN) and the flowthrough was collected and reapplied to the column. The column was washed with 20 column volumes of 20 mM Tris-HCl pH 8.0, 300 mM NaCl, 20 mM imidazole and 0.02% Anzergent 3-14 (Anatrace) followed by 20 column volumes of 20 mM Tris-HCl pH 8.0, 300 mM NaCl, 20 mM imidazole and 1.0% n-octylglucoside (OG, Anatrace). The column was eluted with six column volumes of 20 mM Tris-HCl pH 8.0, 300 mM NaCl, 200 mM imidazole and 1.0% OG (Anatrace). The eluate was concentrated with a 100-kDa-MWCO Amicon centrifugation filter and purified by SEC in 20 mM Tris-HCl pH 8.0, 150 mM NaCl and 1.0% OG. Fractions were pooled and concentrated with a 100-kDa Amicon centrifugation filter to approximately 5–10 mg m−1. Purified complexes were analysed by SDS–PAGE. Samples were flash-frozen in liquid nitrogen and stored at −80 °C until use.
LptA(I36pBPA) was purified as described previously23. In brief, BL21(λDE3) E. coli cells containing pSup-BpaRS-6TRN and pET22b-LptAI36Am were grown to an OD600 nm of about 0.5 at 37 °C in LB medium containing 50 μg ml−1 carbenicillin, 30 μg ml−1 chloramphenicol and 0.8 mM pBPA (Bachem). Cells were induced with 50 μM IPTG, allowed to grow for 2 h, collected, resuspended in a mixture containing 50 mM Tris-HCl pH 7.4, 250 mM sucrose and 3 mM EDTA, incubated on ice for 30 min and pelleted (6,000g, 10 min). The supernatant was supplemented with 1 mM PMSF and 10 mM imidazole and pelleted (100,000g, 30 min). The supernatant was incubated with Ni-NTA resin, which was washed twice (20 column volumes of 20 mM Tris-HCl pH 8.0, 150 mM NaCl, 10% glycerol and 20 mM imidazole). LptA was eluted with wash buffer supplemented with an additional 180 mM imidazole, concentrated using a 10-kDa-cut-off Amicon centrifugal concentrator (Millipore) to approximately 3 mg ml−1, flash-frozen and stored at −80 °C until use.
To purify ΔTM-LptC, BL21 (λDE3) E. coli cells containing pBAD-HisA-LptC24-191-thrombin-His7 were grown to an OD600 nm of around 0.6 at 37 °C in LB medium containing 50 μg ml−1 carbenicillin. Cells were induced with 0.2% l-arabinose, allowed to overnight at 16 °C, collected and resuspended in lysis buffer (20 mM Tris pH 8.0, 150 mM NaCl and 10% glycerol). Lysozyme, DNase I and PMSF were added to final concentrations of 100 µg ml−1, 50 µg ml−1 and 1 mM, respectively. Cells were passed through an EmulsiFlex-C3 high-pressure cell disruptor twice. The cell lysate was centrifuged (5,000g, 10 min) and the supernatant was further centrifuged (100,000g, 30 min). The supernatant was spiked with imidazole to a final concentration of 10 mM, and then rocked with Ni-NTA Superflow resin (QIAGEN) for 1 h. Protein was eluted with lysis buffer containing 200 mM imidazole, concentrated using a 10-kDa-MWCO Amicon Ultra centrifugal filter (Millipore) and purified by SEC on a Superdex 200 increase column in SEC buffer (20 mM Tris pH 8.0, 150 mM NaCl and 10% glycerol). Fractions were pooled and incubated with thrombin (BioVision) overnight at 4 °C. The solution was spiked with 8 mM imidazole and passed over a column containing Ni-NTA Superflow resin (QIAGEN) and Benzamidine Sepharose 4 Fast Flow resin (GE Healthcare) three times. The final flowthrough was concentrated, separated into 10-µl aliquots, flash-frozen and stored at −80 °C.
Proteoliposomes were prepared as described previously23. A mixture of 20 mM Tris-HCl pH 8.0, 150 mM NaCl, 7.5 mg ml−1 E. coli polar lipids, 0.5 mg ml−1 LPS and 0.25% DDM was prepared and kept on ice for 10 min. Purified LptB2FGC or LptB2FG was added to a final concentration of 0.86 μM, and the mixture was left on ice for 20 min. The mixture was diluted 100-fold with cold 20 mM Tris-HCl pH 8.0 and 150 mM NaCl and kept on ice for 20 min. The proteoliposomes were pelleted (300,000g, 2 h, 4 °C), resuspended in 20 mM Tris-HCl pH 8.0 and 150 mM NaCl, diluted 100-fold and centrifuged (300,000g, 2 h, 4 °C). The pellets were resuspended in a mixture of 20 mM Tris-HCl pH 8.0, 150 mM NaCl and 10% glycerol (250 μl per 100 μl of the original pre-dilution solution), homogenized by sonication, flash-frozen and stored at −80 °C until use.
Proteoliposomes containing LptDE-His6 were prepared as previously reported22,25, with minor modifications as detailed here. A mixture of 20 mM Tris pH 8.0, 150 mM NaCl, 9 mg ml−1 lipid stock and 1.25% OG was prepared and kept on ice for 10 min. Purified LptDE was added to a final concentration of 1.43 µM and the mixture was incubated on ice for 10 min. The mixture was diluted 100-fold with cold 20 mM Tris-HCl pH 8.0 and 150 mM NaCl and kept on ice for 10 min. The proteoliposomes were pelleted (300,000g, 2 h, 4 °C). The pellets were resuspended in a mixture of 20 mM Tris-HCl pH 8.0, 150 mM NaCl and 10% glycerol (22.5 μl per 100 μl of the original pre-dilution solution), homogenized by sonication, flash-frozen and stored at –80 °C until use.
ATP binding to LptB(E163Q)2FG, LptB(E163Q)2FGC, LptB(E163Q)2FGCA and LptB(E163Q)2FGCADE was assessed using an SPA adapted from a standard protocol88. Buffer conditions were adapted to match the structural studies of the Lpt complexes (20 mM Tris-HCl pH 7.5, 150 mM NaCl, 3 mM MgCl2, 5% glycerol and 0.02% GDN). Lpt complex in detergent (50 nM) was incubated with ATP (4–600 μM total ATP), composed of a mixture of 0.2% [2,8-3H]-ATP (Perkin Elmer, Revvity) and 99.8% non-radioactive ATP (Sigma). Copper-chelated PVT SPA beads (Perkin Elmer) were added to a final concentration of 5 mg ml−1. The mixture was dispensed in a clear-bottom, white 96-well plate (Corning 3604) and was mixed on a 96-well plate shaker (Eppendorf) at 900 rpm and 30 °C for 15 min, with 30 s shaking and 30 s resting. Signal to noise was optimal when SPA plates were kept 4 °C for about 36 h and shaken at 900 rpm as described, allowing beads to settle for 35 min before data collection. The SPA was performed at 25 °C in cpm mode (Perkin Elmer MicroBeta2). The total binding signal was background-corrected to account for non-proximity effects. Data are shown as mean ± s.d. from three experiments. Nucleotide binding was analysed by a specific binding model with cooperative slope using GraphPad Prism (see equation below, in which Y indicates binding, Bmax indicates maximum binding; h is the Hill coefficient; X denotes the ligand (ATP); and Kd is the equilibrium dissociation constant). Raw data are in Supplementary Fig. 9.
To visualize differences in Kd in the overlaid datasets, the curve fit equations and raw data were all normalized to bind two ATP molecule equivalents (one per each subunit of LptB) at the calculated Bmax. This was done by dividing all data points for a curve by the calculated Bmax for the pertinent dataset and multiplying by two ATP equivalents. This treatment allows for simple visualization of all the curves as they converge towards a maximum of two ATP molecules bound, and does not alter the parameters of the Hill equation.
ATPase assays were done using a modified molybdate method, as previously reported, with slight modifications30. ATPase reactions of proteoliposomes were performed in 20 mM Tris-HCl pH 8.0, 150 mM NaCl and 10% glycerol with varying concentrations of proteoliposomes. Proteoliposomes were mixed at varying ratios and allowed to incubate at 30 °C for 10 min to allow for bridge formation. Reactions were initiated at 30 °C by the addition of a pre-heated initiation buffer. The initiation buffer was added at 10% of reaction volume for a final concentration of 2 mM ATP and 2 mM MgCl2. Reactions were incubated at 30 °C, with aliquots taken at various time points. Aliquots were quenched with 12% sodium dodecyl sulfate (SDS) at 1:1 vol/vol. ATPase experiments containing liposomes with LptB2FGCA and LptDE were run such that the LptB2FGCA concentration was 80 nM and LptDE was added to the stoichiometries indicated. The +LptDE reactions (Fig. 3g) contained a final concentration of 4.48 μM of LptDE for a 56-fold excess, and reactions were done in triplicate. Titration data (Fig. 3f) were collected in duplicate, except for the 64-fold excess of LptDE for the +LPS sample. The amounts of inorganic phosphate were determined using a colorimetric method as previously reported, with potassium phosphate used as a standard89. The absorbance at 850 nm (Spectramax 384 Plus) at each time point was measured 30 min after quenching in a transparent 384-well plate (Corning 3701).
In vivo photocrosslinking experiments were performed as previously described23,30 with some modifications. The indicated strains were grown overnight in LB overnight at 37 °C supplemented with 0.2% glucose, 50 µg ml−1 spectinomycin, 50 µg ml−1 carbenicillin and 30 µg ml−1 chloramphenicol. Cultures were diluted 1:100 in fresh LB containing appropriate antibiotics and supplemented with 0.8 mM pBPA. Cultures were grown until an OD600 nm of around 0.6, and then 0.02% arabinose and 200 µM IPTG were added. Cultures were grown for an additional 2 h, and then half of the culture was placed on ice and UV irradiated for 10 min. Cells were collected at 5,000g for 10 min and pellets were resuspended in lysis buffer containing 20 mM Tris-HCl (pH 8.0), 300 mM NaCl supplemented with 1 mM PMSF, 50 µg ml−1 DNAse I, 100 µg ml−1 lysozyme and 5 mM MgCl2. Resuspended cells were freeze-thawed for three cycles and 1% Anzergent 3-14 was added to the lysate. All solubilized lysates were spun at 12,000g for 10 min to remove insoluble debris. Clarified lysate was loaded onto 250 µl of Ni-NTA resin that had been washed with Milli-Q water and equilibrated with buffer containing 20 mM Tris-HCl, 300 mM NaCl and 0.1% Anzergent 3-14. Columns were washed with 20 column volumes of buffer containing 20 mM Tris-HCl, 300 mM NaCl, 0.1% Anzergent 3-14 and 20 mM imidazole. Columns were eluted with 750 µl followed by 600 µl of buffer containing 20 mM Tris-HCl, 300 mM NaCl, 0.1%, Anzergent 3-14 and 200 mM imidazole. All eluate samples were precipitated with 10% trichloroacetic acid and pellets were resuspended in 2× SDS–PAGE loading buffer normalized to the optical density of the original cultures. All samples were run on SDS–PAGE gels and immunoblotted with appropriate antibodies, as described above.
The levels of release of LPS from proteoliposomes to LptA were measured as previously described23,27. Assays used 60% vol/vol proteoliposomes in a solution containing 50 mM Tris-HCl pH 8.0, 500 mM NaCl, 10% glycerol, 2 µM LptA(I36pBPA) and 0.96 µM ΔTM-LptC where applicable. Reactions were initiated by the addition of ATP (varying concentration, as relevant) and 2 mM MgCl2, and proceeded at 30 °C. Samples were taken after 60 min, irradiated with UV light (365 nm) on ice for 10 min using a B-100AP lamp (Thermo Fisher Scientific) and analysed by SDS–PAGE and immunoblotting.
LPS was diluted in buffer (20 mM Tris-HCl pH 8.0, 150 mM NaCl, 1.35 mM MgCl2, 10% glycerol and 0.02% GDN) to 34 μM. This mixture was sonicated for 30 s. Buffer (20 mM Tris-HCl pH 8.0, 150 mM NaCl, 10% glycerol and 0.02% GDN) and LptB2FGC(T47pBPA)A(L116pBPA) was added for a final concentration of 0.8 μM LptB2FGC(T47pBPA)A(L116pBPA) and 4 μM LPS. This mixture was incubated at 30 °C for 20 min. The reaction was initiated by the addition of 3 mM ATP and MgCl2. Samples were taken immediately after ATP addition, after 5 min, after 15 min and after 30 min. Immediately after collection, each sample was irradiated with UV light (365 nm) on ice for 10 min using a B-100AP lamp (Thermo Fisher Scientific), and 2× SDS–PAGE loading buffer was added to uncleaved samples immediately after UV irradiation. Thrombin-cleaved samples were treated with 1.5 units of thrombin (Cytiva) for 24 h at room temperature before 2× SDS–PAGE loading buffer was added. Samples were analysed by SDS–PAGE and immunoblotting as described above with the following modification: 10% Criterion Tris-HCl gels (Bio-Rad) were used to analyse uncleaved samples and 15% Criterion Tris-HCl gels (Bio-Rad) were used to analyse thrombin-cleaved samples.

D.K. and R.J.T. conceived the project and designed research. R.J.T. designed and developed methods to stabilize and purify Lpt bridges. R.J.T. and K.S.P. performed cryo-EM experiments, with help from R.M.W. and A.C. K.S.P. designed the strategy to reconstruct Lpt structures from the cryo-EM data, and K.S.P. and R.J.T. solved and refined the structures with input from R.M.W. A.C. designed and performed ATP binding assays and S.J.R. designed and performed ATP hydrolysis experiments. V.B. designed and developed methods to obtain LPS-free Lpt. N.R. designed genetic experiments to validate the cellular functionality of the Lpt–LptA fusion, and performed them with S.A.E. and A.W. R.J.T. and S.J.R. confirmed bridge formation in vitro. S.A.E. designed and performed in vivo biochemical experiments in Fig. 4, with supporting in vitro experiments performed by K.S.P. B.P. designed and performed in vitro experiments in Fig. 5, with supporting in vivo experiments performed by S.A.E. D.K. and R.J.T. wrote the manuscript, with strategic input from S.C.H. R.J.T. and K.S.P. prepared the figures, with help from B.P., A.C., S.A.E. and S.J.R. All authors reviewed and edited the manuscript.
