a, Cartoon of the IM complex containing the LptC–LptA fusion protein where LptC and LptA are connected by a poly-glycine-serine linker and comprise a single polypeptide. b, Locus analysis of ΔlptC, ΔlptA, and ΔlptCΔlptA strains by PCR. The expected sizes are 2,188 bp (wild type), 3,039 bp (ΔlptC::kan), 3,057 bp (ΔlptA::kan) and 2,513 bp (ΔlptCA::kan). Four independent isolates of each strain were phenotypically characterized. After confirming that all selected isolates behaved the same phenotypically, one of them was kept after confirming the genotype by PCR. c, The LptC–LptA fusion plasmid complements ΔlptC, ΔlptA, and ΔlptCΔlptA strains. Spot dilutions were plated on M63 minimal medium. d, The quantities of LptC, LptA, and LptC–LptA present in lysate from ΔlptC, ΔlptA, and ΔlptCΔlptA strains complemented with the LptCA fusion were analysed by immunoblotting with anti-LptC and anti-LptA with anti-RpoA as a loading control. The blots shown are representative of 3 independent replicates. Blots against different antibodies represent identical samples processed in parallel. e, Site-specific in vivo photocrosslinking of LPS to LptC, LptA, or LptC–LptA. Proteins substituted at the indicated positions with the photocrosslinkable unnatural amino acid pBPA were overexpressed in E. coli. Samples were taken during mid-log phase, exposed to UV light and cross-linking was analysed by immunoblotting with anti-LPS with anti-His as a loading control. The blots shown are representative of 3 independent replicates. Blots against different antibodies represent identical samples processed in parallel. f, ATPase activity of LptB2FGC and LptB2FGCA in proteoliposomes with and without LPS. The data reported are the mean and standard deviation of three independent measurements. g, Flow cytometric analysis of Lpt bridge formation using fluorescently labelled proteoliposomes. IM proteoliposomes are labelled with ATTO-565 (y-axis, PE) and OM proteoliposomes are labelled with ATTO-488 (x-axis, FITC). Particles containing signal in both channels represent associated IM and OM proteoliposomes that have formed Lpt bridges. Protein combinations tested from left to right are: LptB2FGC + LptA + LptDE (positive control), LptB2FGCA + LptDE, and LptB2FGCA + truncated LptDE lacking the N-terminal periplasmic domain (negative control). h, Table summarizing the results of replicates from the flow cytometric analysis of Lpt bridge formation.

Invalid traffic on AI Max search doubled to 5.28%. Get the channel-by-channel retail benchmarks before Black Friday.
The AI Search Playbook. What's Actually Driving Traffic Now.
Where search leaders are putting 2027 budget, plus the AI visibility scorecard that replaces session reporting.
Ask the SEJ experts you already read what AI, agents, and algorithm changes mean for your traffic. Private, human to human. $97/mo.
Here is what the ruling covers, and how advertisers could potentially recover up to 3x the amount paid in hidden costs.
This post was sponsored by Silver Arbitration Law, PLLC. The opinions expressed in this article are the sponsor’s own.
If you manage Google Search campaigns, you have explained rising CPCs many times: more competitors, broader match types, a lower Quality Score, an account that needed restructuring. Each explanation is plausible.
I have worked in arbitration since before Google existed, as both counsel and arbitrator. Below I set out what the record shows: what the court found, which auction changes it named and when they ran, and what you would have to establish about your own account before any of it applies to you.
In August 2024, after a ten-week trial, the court found that Google “has exercised its monopoly power by charging supracompetitive prices for general search text ads” (Mem. Op. at 4).
Supracompetitive means above the price a competitive market would produce.
The trial record showed that Google’s ads team used internal “pricing knobs” that raised what a winning ad paid without any change in bids, ad quality or competition, mostly by inflating the runner-up’s score that sets the winner’s CPC.
Four of these instances have date ranges you can check against your own account:
Advertisers were not told. Google’s Vice President of Ads testified: “We tend not to tell advertisers about pricing changes” (Tr. 1226, Dischler). The court found that “[m]any advertisers do not even realize that Google is responsible for the changes in price” (Mem. Op. at 91–93, 260).
Individual advertisers still need to establish whether they were affected and by how much. The first step is to get informed, and there are two ways to do it. You can ask an arbitration lawyer to assess your account; I do this for advertisers, and you can book a short call with me. Or you can read up on the case first: this research prompt has an AI assistant brief you on what the courts found and whether it fits a business like yours.
The search ruling covers general search text ads. The court did not find a monopoly in the broader market for all search advertising, which includes ads sold by retailers such as Amazon and Walmart. If much of your spend went to Shopping or Performance Max, separate the text-ad portion before drawing conclusions about your account. This research prompt has an AI assistant tell you which ad types and years of spend count.
Note which legal entity accepted Google’s advertising terms and paid the invoices, and which agencies or manager accounts ran the campaigns and when. Those details determine who can review the account and who would raise any claim, and they are the first thing counsel will ask for.
Display spend falls under a second ruling. On April 17, 2025, Judge Leonie Brinkema of the U.S. District Court for the Eastern District of Virginia found that Google had monopolized two parts of the display advertising chain.
That case focused largely on publishers, and the court did not accept every market claim the government brought. If you bought display inventory through Google’s ad tech tools, it raises a separate question about how those transactions worked. Which ruling matters to you depends on the products you used and the years you used them.
Court findings in this article are quoted with a page cite to the opinion. Anything cited to an exhibit number (UPX, DX) or a transcript (Tr., Dep.) is a trial exhibit or sworn testimony, most of it Google’s own. The two are not the same: a finding is what the court concluded; an exhibit is evidence the court heard.
Google disagrees with the ruling and is appealing. The court’s September 2025 remedies decision requires Google to disclose material changes to its ad auction publicly. Advertisers should therefore have more information about changes that affect what they pay.
Google’s advertising terms contain an arbitration clause. Advertisers bound by that clause must pursue covered disputes through individual arbitration and waive the right to bring a class action. Each advertiser pursuing such a claim brings it through the American Arbitration Association. The court’s findings provide a starting point; reviewing an account and deciding whether to file remain separate steps.
A claim could be potentially worth 15% to 30% of your eligible search spend. Economists estimate Google’s overcharge at about 5% to 10%, and antitrust law triples proven damages.
If your business spent $10 million on eligible search ads during the relevant period, that points to a potential claim of $1.5 million to $3 million, before fees and case-specific adjustments. That is the size of the claim. An arbitrator or a settlement decides what is paid.
Advertisers ask me this more than anything else. Thousands of U.S. advertisers are already pursuing individual claims, and I am not aware of any reported retaliation against one for filing. Retaliating against a group of claimants that size would create a new legal and reputational problem for Google while two federal courts are overseeing its conduct.
In my view the practical risk is low. You are also not suing Google: you are using the arbitration process Google wrote into its own advertising terms. Ask your lawyer how the filing is handled and what Google sees. The research prompt guide I’ve put together ends with three questions to put to a lawyer.
Less than most advertisers expect. For a standard claim, gathering and submitting the account records should take about one hour of staff time in total, usually split between someone with access to the Google Ads account and an authorized signatory.
After that, your lawyer should handle the assessment, the filing and the coordination, and come back to you only when a decision or case-specific input is needed. If a firm asks for much more of your time than that, ask why. Any time your in-house counsel spends reviewing the engagement is separate and depends on your internal approval process.
Whoever paid for the ads holds the claim, and anything paid out on it goes back to them. If your agency ran the campaigns and you paid the bills, the claim is yours. Your agency can pull the spend history, name the campaigns and years involved, and help you take the questions above to a lawyer.
If you have questions about Google-related risk or what to consider before pursuing a claim, you can book a short call with our team. The call is free and nothing is shared with Google.

90,2026-10-07,"Yves here. Tom Neuburger has been publishing a step-by-step primer on how money creation and Federal spending work for governments that are monetary sovereigns. This final entry in his series covers the unduly contentious topic of national debt.
Keep in mind that the Modern Monetary Theory framework does not dispute the idea that governments can engage in excessive spending, and that that has consequences. But they are not involuntary default, aka bankruptcy, but too much inflation.
In a bit of synchronicity, another writer, this one in the UK, has just addressed the misdirected concern about government borrowing. Colonel Smithers sent on Don’t Panic, Mr Mainwaring! The Myth of the Bond Vigilantes. From the top of this article:
I recently got asked by a musician friend if we should be worried about high government borrowing costs because ‘UK government bond yields are at a historic high’. …
With all of that in mind, in this article I will be examining a number of assumptions that underlie this story as well as pointing out some things that are missing from their narrative.
Those things will include:
Now to the main event.
By Tom Neuburger. Originally published at God’s Spies

“[T]he government … ought to be so constituted as to protect the minority of the opulent against the majority.”
—James Madison, June 26, 1787
“[A positive interest rate policy is] basic income for those who already have money.”
—Warren Mosler, here
This is Part 5 of a brief series on money. We started it here, with a look at what dollars are. There we uncovered the most basic fact about dollars, obvious yet never ever acknowledged:
• You can’t create dollars. Only the government does that.
Everything else in this series proceeds from that fact. If you memorize that, you’ll understand everything. Our story so far:
• Part 1 is about the dollar: “Why ‘Entitlement’ Cuts May Succeed”
• Part 2 explains why taxes are levied: “Why Governments Tax: A List”
• Part 3 discusses the ‘deficit’: “What Is This ‘Deficit’ We Hear So Much About?”
• Part 4 uncovers the true nature of the Treasuries market: “What Is the Federal Bond Market Really?”
And this, Part 5, addresses the question, “If our government makes dollars from nothing, what’s the ‘national debt’?”
To answer we need to go back to Part 4, and its discussion of the voluntary requirement to sell Treasury bonds. You can read that discussion, but in short, our government has voluntarily tied its own hands. Though it can create dollars directly, and it can authorize (via Congress) to spend as much as it wants, the Treasury is forced to sell bonds to finance itself entirely by choice, by laws that can be repealed.
First Principles First
To begin, consider these points:
• The source of all U.S. dollars is ultimately the government.
There’s no other source. The Fed creates dollars because the government says it can. Banks create dollars by lending beyond their reserves — lending cash they don’t have — because the government says it can. The government’s always in charge of dollar creation.
• Therefore, the government has no inherent need to borrow. Do airlines need to borrow “airline miles” to give them away? Of course not; they make them. End of.
• Each dollar spent by the government is a dollar in somebody’s pocket. No spending, no dollars.
• The Treasury is financed with bonds only because that’s the law. If those laws were repealed, the Treasury could just create dollars and buy what it needs.
• Treasury bonds are therefore a gift, offered to people with money. If you don’t have money, or not enough of it, you can’t buy government bonds. If you have money to spare, you can.
The ‘National Debt’
So what’s the “national debt”? It’s the sum of all Treasury bonds held outside the government. This includes bonds held by you, by mutual funds and the like, by banks and the Fed itself, and by foreign governments and their own central banks.
Thus:
• The “national debt” is the market for U.S. Treasuries.
• Because having a Treasuries market is voluntary, the “national debt” is really just an investment vehicle, a way to give money to people who already have money via interest payments.
• The “national debt” is therefore not a debt, but a gift.
Why Have a Treasuries Market?
So why do we have it, this market for U.S. Treasuries?
First, think of the wealthy. The world of the rich needs a 100% guaranteed place to stash money, especially when the world seems unstable. The Treasuries market is that place.
Now think of the money supply. Dollars exist because government spends and because banks are permitted to lend money they don’t have. So if there’s too much money around, the government takes some of it back, reducing the supply.
How? Here’s where it gets interesting. Government has two ways of taking money out of the economy. The first is through federal taxes. Everyone is taxed, but the system is so skewed that the rich avoid being taxed almost entirely.
Between 2014 and 2018, Elon Musk’s wealth grew by $13.9 billion, yet he paid only $455 million in federal income taxes, resulting in an actual tax rate of about 3.27% (ProPublica, 2021). In contrast, the average American family’s effective federal income tax rate was 13.3% in 2018 (Tax Foundation, 2021). This stark difference highlights how the current tax system enables wealth accumulation without proportionate tax contributions. [emphasis mine]
And from ProPublica:
Some years billionaires pay no federal income taxes: Jeff Bezos paid zero in 2007 and 2011, Elon Musk paid zero in 2018, Michael Bloomberg paid zero several times in “recent years”, and George Soros paid zero three years in a row.
So how does the government extract excess money from the rich? That’s the second way: It pays the rich to surrender its cash temporarily by offering the Treasuries market. Bonds bought means money retired, at least for a while.
Two ways to reduce the money supply, taxation and the Treasuries market. Thus another of the great imbalances in our system. When government wants to reduce the money supply it extracts unreimbursed and involuntary-given money from you via your taxes, and reimbursed and voluntary-given money from the wealthy via Treasury bonds.
Or, as Cory Doctorow puts it:
Taxes coercively reduce the purchasing power of the private sector (they’re a stick). T-bills do the same thing, but voluntarily (they’re the carrot).

The answer has to be clear from what you just read. If the “national debt” is the market for U.S. Treasuries, and the government…
…the U.S. Treasuries market would disappear. In whole, not in part.
Does anyone with money want that? You may not care, though if you have enough to invest in a Treasury fund, you’d be getting above 5%. But the super-rich definitely care. They need a place to stash cash when nothing seems safe, and our government is nothing if not a servant of wealth (see quotes above).
The national debt is the sum of all “deficit” spending expressed as the total of publicly-held Treasury bonds. If we paid off the “debt,” we’d be in a whole new world.

No, the Fed controls the money supply via reserve requirements. 
The Fed controls the supply of money by increasing or decreasing the monetary base. The monetary base is related to the size of the Fed’s balance sheet; specifically, it is currency in circulation plus the deposit balances that depository institutions hold with the Federal Reserve. The Fed has essentially complete control over the size of the monetary base.

The household budget analogy is the most pernicious and persistent fallacy propagated by the neoclassical economic orthodoxy and their fellow travellers in the think tank industry like the Atlas Network et al. They have wholly captured the public discourse via the usual suspects in the western corporate media organs.
And it’s true for municipal governments
It’s an outgrowth from when currency was backed with gold. Back then, especially around the year 1900, it was true. Countries with gold backed currency could not issue more currency without either reducing the exchangeability of the currency ($20 will no longer convert to 1 oz, but now you need $25) or increasing the size of the held reserves (we mined a literal ton of gold, so at the $20 an ounce rate, we can issue/spend $580,000 in currency that is backed) Taxation was seen as reclaiming that gold liability. 
One can argue that if a government can control the currency/gold exchange rate, that they are fully sovereign and aren’t bound to the household finances concept, similar to the voluntary issuance of treasuries. However if a government commits to maintaining a strict exchange rate, then they do lock themselves into operating under that household finance analogy.
However, the bigger problem, is that in 1933 this was only partially true (FDR ends gold redemption for US citizens), and since 1971 this was completely untrue (Nixon ends international convertibility, and the $ is now pure fiat) 
Since there are still people alive who 1) witnessed the Nixon shock happen, 2) experienced a dramatic increase in inflation in the 70s and early 80s, 3) are unfamiliar with or have not considered the implications of MMT models in government finance, and 4) have not all completely retired or given up power from any important decision making positions they hold, they may still operate and plan/strategize around the incorrect household finances analogy.

When an economic actor spends more than it earns, then it is accruing debt. The US government hasn’t had a balanced budget since FY2001, the average deficit since then has been $1.326 Trillion per year. The size of the debt is accelerating, in the last 10 years the average deficit has been $2.061 Trillion – fun fact: over 1/2 of the national debt has accrued in the last 10 years. 
Some people say that it doesn’t matter because the US can create its own money, however they don’t understand that when money is created, it also creates debt. Here is one way money is created If I buy something and don’t have cash, I can sign an IOU to pay for it. I have created money, the IOU, I have also created debt – an obligation to pay for what I have bought. I can clear that debt by selling an asset I own like another good, or my labor to pay off the debt. I can’t pay it off by creating another IOU.
The US is $40 trillion in debt is a symptom of economic problems in the US. The country used to have sufficient economic growth to reduce its national debt as a percent of GDP and increase the standard of living for the average American, now it can’t do either. The last balanced budget was under Clinton and there were 4 years of real GDP growth of over 4% – which was the normal growth for the period starting at the end of WWII to the end of the 60s. Slow growth means less economic activity, stagnant wages, less tax revenue and a higher requirement for social services. The 2.1% average real GDP growth of the last 25 years hasn’t been healthy. 
One of the main reasons for lower growth has been the offshoring of manufacturing, having a positive balance of trade increases growth and having a deficit reduces it. The US has had over 50 years of consecutive trade deficits. Another issue is the way the government fought offshoring. It cut taxes to the wealthy, weakened the bargaining of labor and cut regulations – all in a hope to lower domestic costs. All it did was provide more capital to move factories offshore. 
Can the US get its economy back into stronger growth? I am not sure in today’s political and economic climate. Trump tried to do something with tariffs – but that just penalized American consumers without a real plan to re-industrialize. Besides it would take at least 10 years of pain, none of which was communicated. But imagine the inflation and how it would weaken the standard of living for Americans? Besides every 1% increase in interest rates will cost Americans $400 billion in interest payments – just on the federal debt. No wonder an economic theory that says we can create all the money we want and don’t have to worry about the debt, is becoming a popular theory. My guess is that the US will keep running $2+ trillion deficits and will hit $60 trillion in national debt within 10 years. It will get harder to attract investors unless the interest rate goes higher and that will create its own problems. Meanwhile all that deficit spending will let the country live beyond its means – until it doesn’t.
Your case that when money is created, it also creates a debt, suffers by being illustrated by a hypothetical example, when a direct factual description seems called for. 
The operation of a sovereign currency issuer, like the US government, is not adequately described as a generic economic agent. Your example assumes IOU creation after an act of exchange, but the US government creates money ex nihilo, prior to exchange, so an accurate version would a have to recognize that fact, as Neuberger’s articles do. 
The IOU example does have the virtue of clear expression and brevity, but the problem is the facts. Archaeological evidence — sometimes covered here at NC — suggests that money was historically created by the State, not as an addendum to a barter system. Even opponents of Modern Monetary Theory affirm that it accurately describes how the US government really funds itself as a currency issuer. So an effective IOU argument — whether historical or about the US national debt — would have to explain why those aren’t significant features, instead of sidestepping them with a clear but logically circular hypothetical.

Where I agree with the author:
If the government pays 5% interest on $10 trillion of Treasury securities, it is making enormous interest payments to the holders of those securities. And because ownership of financial assets is highly unequal, a significant portion of that interest ultimately goes to people and institutions that already possess substantial wealth.
Where I don’t:
Imagine you have $10,000 in your checking account. You go to your bank and say:
Give me a 1-year certificate of deposit for $10,000.
The bank takes your $10,000 checking deposit and gives you a $10,000 CD.
Would you say: The bank gave me a $10,000 gift because I already had money?
Obviously not. You exchanged one financial asset for another. And the CD may pay you interest because you’ve agreed to hold the bank’s liability for a specified period. Treasuries work in a broadly similar way. The important difference is that the Treasury is a liability of the federal government rather than a commercial bank.
The interest is the “gift”, not the principal. It’s clearly at the bank’s discretion (prior to purchase), since they can offer any interest rate they feel like (within reason), and most of the CDs for small savers have pretty terrible interest rates. At one time people just earned interest on their savings without commitment to not withdraw it for a set term (like there is with a CD). Now savings account interest is barely above zero.
Uh, that depends… On whether you have 10,000 in thr bank to begin with.
If you don’t, have 10,000, no CD and no interest for you.

…the finance industry is an industry that sucks in some of our most talented citizens. Citizens who could be working in other sectors such as health research or the mitigation of global warming.
This is a subject that does not get nearly enough discussion. We live in a capitalist society, so there is plenty of thinking about how to allocate capital efficiently. But rarely do we hear anyone talk about how to use the labor of our citizens efficiently from a societal standpoint. 
There was a lot of talk in the engineering community in the 1980s about how our best engineering brain power was going into designing weapons, and the “leftovers” were being used to engineer everything else (I mean absolutely no offense to anyone by saying this; for one thing, it means I am one of the leftovers!).
I believe back in the oughts there was also a lot of commentary about how the finance community was eating up most of our top brain power, with very little to show for it. 
Aside from these two rather isolated points in time, I don’t remember any real ongoing discussion about how to make the best use of our people to create the best society possible.
It’s a very strange oversight, especially at this point in human history when so many species-threatening problems are occurring and we desperately need all hands on deck and all cylinders firing.
As we begin the slow move beyond Trump, the type of economic/political/cultural regime we should push toward and the organizing strategy necessary become important issues.
The consolidated Treasury-Fed model within MMT, in my opinion, assumes an idealized centralized sovereign state.
In the pandemic response ,the U.S. spent five trillion dollars without a financing constraint, rates stayed pinned, the bond vigilantes never came and “how will you pay for it” seemed to die as a serious question.  But in the 2021-2022 period, MMT’s core (functional finance) requires that when real-resource constraint arrives, the government withdraws demand through timely tax increases or spending cuts.
Serious inflation arrived on schedule and the political system proved incapable of executing any offset; the adjustment was done by the Fed, with rates, in exactly the old regime way MMT deprecates.  What was on display was the absence of a nimble unified fiscal state that can fine tune demand the way a central bank moves a rate.
I am quite skeptical that our modern state (even if reorganized through a consolidated Treasury-Fed model) is the way to go.  My best guess, at this point, is to push for what I would call a more radical populist program centered on issues of who creates credit, at what scale, and answerable to whom.
A couple of additions:
Cash and bonds are both financial liabilities of the federal govt – the first indefinite and non-interest bearing, the second fixed term and interest bearing. There is a third difference and it has important consequences: cash deposits are guaranteed only to a limit, Treasuries are backed 100%.
Both taxation and sale of Treasuries to the private sector are cash drains. But consider taxation, why would we want to drain cash before it has even entered the economy (payroll deduction)? Surely money should be allowed to enter the economy, do its good work (exchange and investment) and be taxed at the end of business cycles. That means the prime target for taxation should be corporate profits. If there were ever such a thing as a business ‘charter’ it would surely be something like government will establish the currency, a legal framework and physical and social infrastructure while corporations agree to employ people at living wages, provide goods and services and invest any profits. Anything beyond this should be taxed.
The system we have today is wholly corrupted and results in the incredible inequality we see today. Trump’s (actually the GOP’s) big beautiful bill was a disgrace. But it’s a disgrace that mirrors what our economic system has become – a dingy gambling den in which none of the players want to pay for the lights and A/C and expect unlimited drinks on the house.

93,2026-10-07,"Scarlets have lost their opening two games in the 2026-27 United Rugby Championship
You pass the first reminder of Llanelli's rugby identity before you even reach the town.
In Felinfoel stands a statue of Phil Bennett, a key member of the Llanelli team which famously beat New Zealand in 1972.
A few miles further on, Welsh Sports Hall of Fame inductee Ray Gravell is immortalised outside Parc y Scarlets.
There are no footballers or cricketers competing for space here. The sporting story being told is overwhelmingly one of rugby.
Which makes the question hanging over the place particularly brutal: what happens if professional rugby disappears from Llanelli?
The Welsh Rugby Union (WRU) plans to cut its professional men's teams from four to three. Under its preferred model, Scarlets or Ospreys will cease to exist by 2028.
WRU sticking to three-team plan - Reddin
WRU seek to sell SRC side RGC to private investors
Inside Parc y Scarlets, photographs of former teams and famous victories cover the walls.
Rugby autobiographies fill a bookshelf in the cafe, where today's lunch for the players is roast beef.
Dwayne Peel wanders over.
The former Wales and British and Irish Lions scrum-half is back coaching at the club where he began his career. His grandfather was once a physio here, running on with the magic sponge for Gravell.
Now Peel talks about the school run and his son playing junior rugby.
Three generations, one game.
It is a theme that keeps returning during a day at Parc y Scarlets with the Scrum V Podcast.
Director of rugby Nigel Davies grew up in Trimsaran, a few miles away. Peel is from nearby Tumble. A large proportion of the current squad grew up playing rugby in the towns and villages Scarlets represent.
Even managing director Jon Daniels once played for nearby Llangennech.
He believes that connection is precisely what is at stake.
""We don't have Premier League or Championship football teams,"" he said.
""When they want to play sport, they still play rugby in the (school) yards in west Wales.""
Later, his language becomes starker.
""I genuinely fear for west Wales if this continues the way it is,"" Daniels said. ""For the young generations of Wales, I think there would be a real loss.""
This video can not be played
Rugby is still a big part of life in west Wales - Daniels
It is the emotional heart of Scarlets' argument for survival.
The problem is that Welsh rugby's current predicament cannot be solved by emotion alone.
The WRU holds most of the cards.
Scarlets can make their case, but cannot guarantee their own future.
Daniels argues Welsh rugby should be asking how it can preserve four teams rather than accepting one must disappear.
""We have not seen the numbers laid down in front of us to prove that we can't sustain four,"" he says.
But that raises an obvious question in return: where are Scarlets' numbers?
Daniels says season-ticket revenue is ahead of last year, attendances increased by 7% last season, sponsorship is performing well and the stadium is generating money away from matchdays.
But when pressed on where the additional money needed to sustain four properly funded teams would come from, there is no clear answer.
Instead, he argues for time.
""Can we put together a 10-year plan where, in 10 years' time, we can have four competitive, successful, viable teams?"" he asks.
It is an argument for more time but not yet a funded alternative, and Scarlets do not control the timetable.
James Davies won silver with Team GB at the 2016 Olympic sevens in Rio
On one of the cafe walls, among black-and-white photographs and faded old jerseys, is an image that looks strikingly modern.
The colours are sharp, the picture unmistakably digital. It shows Scarlets celebrating their 2017 league title - the last time a Welsh team won the competition.
James 'Cubby' Davies played in that team. Now he helps recruit the next generation.
He says the ""secret sauce"" was connection.
""We built success on the pitch through a lot of the things that we did off the pitch,"" he said. ""We just connected as a group.""
Now his job is to build another.
Davies says that uncertainty over Welsh rugby's future meant Scarlets were late entering the recruitment market last season.
There is another meeting with the WRU on the day we visit.
Davies talks about planning ahead, then hesitates.
""We're very hopeful of our... what's the word? Our existence, obviously,"" he said.
Existence.
A strange word to hear in the everyday surroundings of a professional sports club.
On the other side of the stadium, past a couple of burger vans with their shutters still down, around 40 players are playing football in an indoor training barn.
There is shouting, laughter and arguing over decisions.
After two opening defeats and plenty of analysis, director Davies wants people smiling again.
""It's all about bringing the energy,"" he says.
It is striking to watch at a club whose very existence is being debated.
Daniels calls the constant speculation around Welsh rugby the ""jungle drums"".
""Sometimes the potential impact on human beings is lost in all that,"" he said.
Yet everything carries on.
Players train. Coaches prepare. Contracts are considered. Lunch is served.
This is what uncertainty looks like inside a sports club.
Mostly, it looks surprisingly normal.
Nine miles away, another club can make its own case.
Ospreys have won three titles
Six Ospreys were selected for the 2013 British and Irish Lions tour - twice as many as the whole of Scotland. Only two Welshmen made the Lions squad in 2025.
Ospreys are planning for the future too, with St Helen's in Swansea being redeveloped as their new home.
The geography is striking.
Llanelli's built-up area has about 42,000 people and Swansea's around 170,000. Dublin city alone has almost 600,000. Johannesburg has millions.
It helps explain the WRU's logic.
On a map, consolidation can look obvious. On the ground, Scarlets and Ospreys have different histories, communities and identities.
For now, Daniels refuses to accept the choice and urges caution.
""We should do that before we just jump to 'right, we cut two teams', or now 'we cut one team',"" he says.
His argument is for four.
But if the WRU presses ahead with three, Scarlets and Ospreys will eventually have to make a different argument.
Not simply why they should survive, why they should survive instead of the other.
By lunchtime, the football has stopped.
Players drift back into the cafe. Plates of roast beef appear beneath photographs of those who came before them.
For now, Scarlets continue with the strangely ordinary business of running a professional rugby club while waiting to discover whether they will remain one.

The owner of two Queens pharmacies was arrested on charges he bilked Medicaid in a $31 million drug-buyback scheme involving HIV prescriptions, the New York attorney general’s office said on Wednesday.
Miguel Baron allegedly prescribed HIV meds to patients insured through Medicaid out of Guardiola Pharmacy and Baron Specialty Pharmacy in Jackson Heights, the AG’s office said in a statement.
Baron then either convinced customers to sell their pills back to him, so he could prescribe them again, or filled their prescriptions with cheaper meds he got on the black market, the attorney general’s office said.
The scheme allegedly netted Baron $31 million in stolen funds from Medicaid and the state’s AIDS Drug Assistance Program.
“Pharmacy owners who exploit patients with medication buyback schemes are putting New Yorkers’ health at serious risk,” Attorney General Letitia James said in a statement.
James added, “My office will continue to protect patients, shut down Medicaid fraud schemes, and hold criminals accountable to ensure that our tax dollars are not lining scammers’ pockets.”
Baron could not be reached for comment and did not have an attorney listed in court documents.
Baron laundered the funds he got from his Medicaid scheme through shell companies that he and his associates used to fund construction projects, purchase condos in Miami and buy luxury cars and World Cup tickets, according to the attorney general’s office.
Baron faces 24 charges, including grand larceny, healthcare fraud and money laundering, according to an indictment that was filed in a New York State Supreme Court in Queens in September and unsealed Wednesday.
Also on Wednesday, the state attorney general brought a civil asset forfeiture case against Baron to seize the property he allegedly purchased with stolen Medicaid funds. The attorney general’s office seeks more than $95 million in damages.
Prosecutors have cracked down on several similar operations in the past. In 2024, a Brooklyn man was sentenced to nine years in prison for selling black market HIV drugs to pharmacies for years.
Another man was extradited from Pakistan by the New York attorney general’s office in 2021 and charged with fraud for allegedly working with multiple coconspirators to buy HIV meds back from Medicaid patients in the Bronx.
Baron was arrested at LaGuardia Airport in June while attempting to flee to Canada, the attorney general’s office said.

She has covered COVID, a nurses' strike, the overdose crisis and New York’s marijuana legalization effort, and spent a year investigating patients' medical bills. Bruce Blakeman vowed Wednesday to clean up the drug and sex infested “Market of Sweethearts” in Queens, blaming Gov. Kathy Hochul and lefty pols for the soft-on-crime laws that let it happen.
Blakeman, the GOP gubernatorial candidate, said cashless bail and “Raise the Age” statutes have allowed immigrant gangs to thrive along Roosevelt Avenue for years, forcing neighborhood residents to live in fear while Albany does nothing. 
“There was a time in this community where you didn’t have to worry about walking around at 10 or 11 o’clock at night,” Blakeman said at a press conference on the seedy stretch. “This was a fun, vibrant, energetic community and it still is, but it’s being destroyed by politicians who have ignored the people.
“They come here for elections and after the elections you never see them,” said Blakeman, the Nassau County executive. “We have seen the growth of gangs like Tren de Aragua, MS13 and Triads. These people are preying on our communities, and Kathy Hochul is responsible.”
Roosevelt Avenue has been plagued by open air prostitution, drug peddling and rampant shoplifting for years, particularly after an influx of illegal immigrants were allowed to flood the Big Apple. 
Despite a series of raids, calls from elected officials and constant complaints from the neighborhood, gangs continue to thrive along the busy strip.
Meanwhile, the state’s bail reform statute, which bars judges from setting bail on most crimes, means criminals continually get cut loose and return to the streets to strike again. The “Raise the Age” statute means defendants as old as 19 get treated with kid gloves as juvenile offenders.
Blakeman — who’ll face Democratic incumbent Hochul on the November 3 ballot as she seeks a second, four-year term — claimed socialist Mayor Zohran Mamdani has made it harder to clean up Roosevelt Avenue by holding cops back and not filling vital posts that could take a bite out of crime.
“Governor Hochul and Zohran Mamdani will not protect you,” he said. “As governor, I will.”

Joining him at Wednesday’s press conference were frustrated local residents. 
“I am afraid to walk by myself,” said 65-year-old retired teacher Toni Diaz, a neighborhood activist who has lived in the area for over 50 years. “So many people in our communities feel like prisoners in our own neighborhood. There are not enough police.”
Another longtime local, Charles Castro, said he’s never seen the area in such dire condition.
“I’ve never seen anything so bad,” he said. “You can get whatever documents you want here. You can get a passport, you can get green cards. Women get trafficked here.
“We have people walking around like zombies — they’re all drugged up,” he added. “The governor sent the state troopers here for 90 days and it got better, and then took them out. That’s insane.”

IMC 2026: Starlink has designed its operations to comply with India’s security, regulatory needs: Starlink VP Dreyer
Starlink says it has built its India operations to meet security and regulatory requirements, including keeping Indian user data within India, and is now awaiting government clearance to begin services. The company has invested in 20 gateway sites and partnered with Bharti Airtel and Reliance Jio.
Bharti Airtel hikes postpaid tariffs by ₹50, adds free international roaming
Bharti Airtel will raise postpaid tariffs by Rs 50 from October 8, 2026, with new plans offering one free international roaming trip each year. The benefit includes 5GB of data, 60 minutes of calls and 25 outgoing SMS, valid for five days.
RBI’s hawkish shift signals more rate hikes ahead, say Economists
The RBI has raised the repo rate by 25 basis points to 5.5 per cent and shifted its monetary policy stance to “calibrated tightening”, signalling a stronger focus on controlling inflation. Economists expect at least two more rate hikes, despite resilient domestic growth.
Paramount closes Warner merger in historic Hollywood deal
Paramount Skydance has completed its $110 billion acquisition of Warner Bros. Discovery, creating a new media giant called Skydance with major film franchises, TV networks and streaming services including Paramount+ and HBO Max. The company now faces the challenge of integrating the businesses, managing nearly $87 billion in debt and delivering more than $6 billion in annual cost savings within three years.
